Drazin Inverses in Categories
Robin Cockett, Jean-Simon Pacaud Lemay, Priyaa Varshinee Srinivasan

Abstract
Drazin inverses are a fundamental algebraic structure which have been extensively deployed in semigroup theory, ring theory, and matrix theory. Drazin inverses can also be defined for endomorphisms in any category. However, beyond a paper by Puystjens and Robinson from 1987, there has been almost no further development of Drazin inverses in category theory. Here we provide a survey of the theory of Drazin inverses from a categorical perspective. We introduce Drazin categories, in which every endomorphism has a Drazin inverse, and provide various examples including the category of matrices over a field, the category of finite length modules over a ring, and finite set enriched categories. We also introduce the notion of expressive rank and prove that a category with expressive rank is Drazin. Moreover, we not only study Drazin inverses in mere categories, but also in additive categories and dagger categories. In an arbitrary category, we show how a Drazin inverse corresponds to an isomorphism in the idempotent splitting, as well as explain how Drazin inverses relate to Leinster’s notion of eventual image duality. In additive categories, we consider core-nilpotent decompositions, image-kernel decompositions, and Fitting decompositions. We also develop the notion of Drazin inverses for pairs of opposing maps, generalizing the usual notion of Drazin inverse for endomorphisms. As an application of this new kind of Drazin inverse, for dagger categories, we provide a novel characterization of the Moore-Penrose inverse in terms of being a Drazin inverse of the pair of a map and its adjoint.



This paper is dedicated to Bill Lawvere, a great mathematician, a mentor, and a friend.

Acknowledgements.
The authors would like to thank Mike Prest for his basic ring theoretic reassurance. For this research, the first named author was partially funded by NSERC, and the third named author was partially funded by the Estonian Research Council (MOB3JD1227).

1
Introduction

In a semigroup
S
, a
Drazin inverse
of
x\in S
is a
x^{D}\in S
such that:

[D.1]
there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
(called the Drazin index) such that
x^{k+1}x^{D}=x^{k}
;
[D.2]

x^{D}xx^{D}=x^{D}
; and
[D.3]

x^{D}x=xx^{D}
. While a Drazin inverse may not always exist, if a Drazin inverse exists then it is unique, so we may speak of
the
Drazin inverse. The term Drazin inverse is named after Michael P. Drazin, who originally introduced the concept of Drazin inverses in rings and semigroups under the modest name “pseudo-inverse”
[
12
]
. The “inverse” part in the term “Drazin inverse” is justified since it is a generalization of the usual notion of inverse, that is, if
S
is a monoid and
u\in S
is invertible, then its inverse is also its Drazin inverse,
u^{D}=u^{-1}
, and conversely elements of
S
with Drazin index zero are precisely the invertible elements of
S
. Drazin inverses have an extensive literature and have been well studied in both ring theory and semigroup theory
[
8
,
12
,
14
,
19
,
22
,
24
,
30
]
, and are deeply connected to strong
\pi
-regularity
[
1
,
3
,
11
,
25
,
30
]
and Fitting’s results
[
15
,
18
,
20
]
(sometimes called Fitting’s Lemma or Fitting’s Decomposition result). Moreover, they have also been extensively studied in matrix theory, and have been found to have many important applications as it is a useful tool in various computations
[
5
,
6
,
7
,
26
,
28
,
31
,
32
,
33
]
.

At the time Drazin first introduced the concept of Drazin inverses in
[
12
]
, category theory was still in its infancy. Thus, the theory of Drazin inverses was not developed from a categorical perspective nor, conversely, did the notion of a Drazin inverse become widely known in the categorical community. To the best of our knowledge, the only discussion of Drazin inverses in category theory appears in a section of a paper by Puystjens and Robinson
[
27
]
from 1987, where they develop the properties of Drazin inverses in additive categories.

The purpose of this paper is, thus, to develop Drazin inverses from a categorical perspective and to make their theory more readily available to a categorical audience. In order to achieve this we have tried to make the exposition as self-contained as possible and, in particular, we have included complete proofs. We do, however, acknowledge that many of the proofs below are available in the extensive ring theory and semigroup literature and we have tried to document this. Having said that, inevitably, there is new material in our exposition including, of course, new definitions, new examples, new constructions, and new proofs. These arise largely because the categorical perspective allows one to consider a broader range of settings which are not directly related to semigroups or rings. For example, without the categorical perspective, one might not have known how Drazin inverses relate to idempotent splittings or the idempotent completion. Nor would one have noticed that having a Drazin inverse implies having eventual image duality in the sense of Leinster
[
21
]
. Even more fundamentally, one might not see the point of considering Drazin inverses for arbitrary maps, not just endomorphisms.

We suspect this just scratches the surface of novel applications for Drazin inverses that the categorical perspective uncovers. This paper was never meant to be the final word on the subject of Drazin inverses in category theory. Rather our hope is that it can be a useful entry point for others into what is a fundamental algebraic structure. So we will be happy if we have left loose ends which others can pursue.

Conventions:
For an arbitrary category
\mathbb{X}
, we denote objects by capital letters
A,B,X,Y
, etc. and maps by lowercase letters
f,g,x,y
, etc. Homsets are denoted by
\mathbb{X}(A,B)
and maps as
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
. Identity maps are denoted as
1_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
. Composition is written in
diagrammatic order
, that is, the composition of a map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
followed by
g:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
is denoted
fg:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
.

2
Drazin Inverses, Drazin Objects, and Drazin Categories

In this section, we discuss Drazin inverses in a category and introduce the notions of Drazin categories and Drazin objects. We also provide examples of these concepts.

2.1
Drazin Inverses

The concept of Drazin inverses in an arbitrary category was first considered by Puystjens and Robinson in
[
27
, Sec 2]
, though they were particularly interested in studying Drazin inverses in
additive
categories – which we discuss in Section
6
. The notion of Drazin inverses in a category
\mathbb{X}
corresponds to the notion of Drazin inverses in the semigroup of endomorphisms,
\mathbb{X}(A,A)
, of each object
A
. Explicitly:

Definition 2.1

In a category
\mathbb{X}
, a
Drazin inverse

[
27
, Sec 2]
of
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is an endomorphism
x^{D}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that:

[D.1]

There is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
1
1

1

By convention,
x^{0}=1_{A}
.

x^{k+1}x^{D}=x^{k}
;

[D.2]

x^{D}xx^{D}=x^{D}
;

[D.3]

x^{D}x=xx^{D}
.

If
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
has a Drazin inverse
x^{D}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, we say that
x
is
Drazin
, and call the least
k
such that
x^{k+1}x^{D}=x^{k}
the
Drazin index of
x
, which we denote by
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
.

For the remainder of this section, we work in an arbitrary category
\mathbb{X}
. An important fact is that Drazin inverses, if they exist, are unique. Before proving this, here are some useful basic identities.

Lemma 2.2

Let
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
be Drazin with Drazin inverse
x^{D}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
:

(i)

For all
n\geq\mathsf{ind}(x)
,
x^{n+1}x^{D}=x^{n}=x^{D}x^{n+1}
;

(ii)

For all
n\geq\mathsf{ind}(x)
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
x^{n+m}(x^{D})^{m}=x^{n}=(x^{D})^{m}x^{n+m}
;

(iii)

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
x^{n}(x^{D})^{n+1}=x^{D}=(x^{D})^{n+1}x^{n}
.

Proof:
(i) Let
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
. Then for all
n\geq k
, using
[D.1]
we compute that:

x^{n+1}x^{D}=x^{n-k+k+1}x^{D}=x^{n-k}x^{k+1}x^{D}=x^{n-k}x^{k}=x^{n-k+k}=x^{n}

So
x^{n+1}x^{D}=x^{n}
. By
[D.3]
it follows that
x^{D}x^{n+1}=x^{n}
as well.

(ii) We prove this by induction on
m
. For the base case
m=0
, we clearly have that
x^{n}(x^{D})^{0}=x^{n}
.
Now suppose that for all
0\leq j\leq m
,
x^{n+j}(x^{D})^{j}=x^{n}
. Then using the induction hypothesis and (
i
), we compute that:

x^{n+m+1}(x^{D})^{m+1}=xx^{n+m}(x^{D})^{m}x^{D}=xx^{n}x^{D}=x^{n+1}x^{D}=x^{n}

So
x^{n+m+1}(x^{D})^{m+1}=x^{n}
. Therefore all
m
,
x^{n+m}(x^{D})^{m}=x^{n}
as desired. By
[D.3]
it follows that
x^{n}=(x^{D})^{m}x^{n+m}
as well.

(iii) We prove this by induction. For the base case
n=0
, we clearly have that
x^{0}x^{D}=x^{D}
. Now suppose that for all
0\leq j\leq n
,
x^{j}(x^{D})^{j+1}=x^{D}
. Then using the induction hypothesis,
[D.2]
, and
[D.3]
, we compute that:

x^{n+1}(x^{D})^{n+2}=xx^{n}(x^{D})^{n+1}x^{D}=xx^{D}x^{D}=x^{D}xx^{D}=x^{D}

So
x^{n+1}(x^{D})^{n+2}=x^{D}
. Therefore all
n
,
x^{n}(x^{D})^{n+1}=x^{D}
as desired. By
[D.3]
it follows that
(x^{D})^{n+1}x^{n}=x^{D}
as well.

\Box

Proposition 2.3

[
12
, Thm 1]
If
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
has a Drazin inverse, it is unique.

Proof:
Suppose that
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
has two possible Drazin inverses
y:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
z:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
. So explicitly, there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x^{k}xy=x^{k}
, and also that
yxy=y
and
xy=yx
, and there is a
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x^{k^{\prime}}xz=x^{k^{\prime}}
, and also that
xz=zx
and
zxz=z
. Now set
j=\max(k,k^{\prime})
. First observe that by Lemma
2.2
.(
i
) we have that
x^{j+1}y=x^{j}
and
zx^{j+1}=x^{j}
, while by Lemma
2.2
.(
iii
) we have that
y=x^{j}y^{j+1}
and
z=z^{j+1}x^{j}
. Then we compute that:

\displaystyle y=x^{j}y^{j+1}=zx^{j+1}y^{j+1}=zxx^{j}y^{j+1}=zxy=z^{j+1}xx^{j}y=z^{j+1}x^{j+1}y=z^{j+1}x^{j}=z

So
y=z
, and we conclude that the Drazin inverse is unique.

\Box

From now on we may speak of
the
Drazin inverse of an endomorphism
x
(if it exists of course) and denote it by
x^{D}
.

2.2
Drazin Categories

We call a category in which every endomorphism has a Drazin inverse a
Drazin category
. Since Drazin inverses are unique, being Drazin is a property of a category rather than a structure.

It is always possible to construct a Drazin category from any category by considering the full subcategory determined by the objects whose every endomorphism is Drazin: we call these Drazin objects.

Definition 2.4

An object
A
is a
Drazin object
if every endomorphism
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin. Then define
\mathsf{D}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
to be the full subcategory of Drazin objects of
\mathbb{X}
.

Lemma 2.5

\mathsf{D}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
is a Drazin category. Moreover,
\mathbb{X}
is Drazin if and only if
\mathsf{D}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)=\mathbb{X}
.

Proof:
Every endomorphism in
\mathsf{D}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
is Drazin, so
\mathsf{D}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
is Drazin. Also, clearly
\mathbb{X}
is Drazin if and only if every object is Drazin, which means that
\mathsf{D}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)=\mathbb{X}
.

\Box

Thus, an equivalent way of describing a Drazin category is as a category in which every object is Drazin. Here are now some examples of Drazin inverses, Drazin categories, and Drazin objects.

2.3
Complex Matrices

Arguably, the best-known (and most applied) examples of Drazin inverses are those of complex matrices. So let
\mathbb{C}
be the field of complex numbers and let
\mathsf{MAT}(\mathbb{C})
be the category of complex matrices, that is, the category whose objects are natural numbers
n\in\mathbb{N}
and where a map
{A:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
is an
n\times m
complex matrix. Composition given by matrix multiplication and the identity on
n
is the
n
-dimensional identity matrix. Endomorphisms in
\mathsf{MAT}(\mathbb{C})
correspond precisely to square matrices: so an endomorphism
{A:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}n}
is an
n\times n
square matrix
A

It is well-known that every complex square matrix has a Drazin inverse
[
6
, Chap 7]
making
\mathsf{MAT}(\mathbb{C})
Drazin. To compute the Drazin inverse, first recall that every
n\times n
complex matrix
A
can be written in the form
[
6
, Thm 7.2.1]
:

A=P\begin{bmatrix}C&0\\
0&N\end{bmatrix}P^{-1}

for some invertible
n\times n
matrix
P
, an invertible
m\times m
matrix
C
(where
m\leq n
), and a nilpotent
n-m\times n-m
matrix
N
(that is,
N^{k}=0
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
). See
[
6
, Algorithm 7.2.1]
for how this form is computed. The Drazin inverse of
A
is the
n\times n
matrix
A^{D}
defined as follows:

A^{D}=P\begin{bmatrix}C^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}P^{-1}

The Drazin index of
A
corresponds precisely to the
index
of
A

[
6
, Def 7.2.1]
, that is the least
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathsf{rank}(A^{k+1})=\mathsf{rank}(A^{k})
.

In fact, it is well-known
[
5
]
– and, as we shall shortly show in Section
4
– that a square matrix over any field has a Drazin inverse. Thus, for any field
k
, its category of matrices
\mathsf{MAT}(k)
is always a Drazin category. Moreover, as explained in Corollary
3.9
below, a category which is equivalent to a Drazin category is certainly itself Drazin. So
k\text{-}\mathsf{FVEC}
, the category of finite-dimensional
k
-vector spaces and
k
-linear maps between them, is Drazin as well.

2.4
Modules

Let
R
be a ring and let
R\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}
be the category of (left)
R
-modules and
R
-linear morphisms between them. In general,
R\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}
is not Drazin. Indeed, when
R=\mathbb{Z}
, consider the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear endomorphism
f:\mathbb{Z}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{Z}
given by multiplying by two,
f(x)=2x
. Now if
f
had a Drazin inverse
f^{D}:\mathbb{Z}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{Z}
, it must be of the form
f^{D}(x)=nx
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then by
[D.1]
we would have that for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
2^{k+1}nx=2^{k}x
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
. This would imply that
2n=1
, which is a contradiction. So
\mathbb{Z}\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}
(which recall is equivalent to the category of Abelian groups) is not Drazin.

That said, while
R\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}
may not always be Drazin, there are various characterizations of Drazin
R
-linear endomorphism – see for example
[
30
, Lemma 2.1]
. In particular, an
R
-linear endomorphism
f:M\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M
has a Drazin inverse if and only if
f
is
strongly
\pi
-regular
– which will be discussed in Section
3.2
below. Alternatively, an
R
-linear endomorphism
{f:M\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M}
is Drazin if and only if
M=\mathsf{im}(f^{k})\oplus\mathsf{ker}(f^{k})
for some
k\geq 1

[
30
, Lemma 2.1.(4)]
, and this decomposition is sometimes called
Fitting’s decomposition

[
20
, Lemma 2.4]
. Then an
R
-module
M
is said to satisfy
Fitting’s Lemma

[
2
, Page 665]
if every endomorphism has a Fitting’s decomposition (or equivalently if
R\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}(M,M)
is strongly
\pi
-regular
[
2
, Prop 2.3]
). As such, the Drazin objects in
R\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}
are precisely the
R
-modules which satisfy Fitting’s Lemma, or in other words,
\mathsf{D}\left(R\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}\right)
is the full subcategory of
R
-modules which satisfy Fitting’s Lemma. For more on modules which satisfy Fitting’s Lemma, see
[
1
,
2
,
25
]
, and also see
[
2
]
for classes of rings for which all finitely generated modules satisfy Fitting’s Lemma and are therefore Drazin.

Famously, “Fitting’s Decomposition Theorem” says that for every endomorphism of a
finite length

R
-module has a Fitting’s decomposition
[
18
, Page 113]
. This implies that every
R
-linear endomorphism of a finite length
R
-module is Drazin, and thus finite length
R
-modules are Drazin. Therefore the full subcategory of finite length
R
-modules is Drazin. It is important to note that while every finite length
R
-module is Drazin, there are modules which do not have finite length which are Drazin. Indeed, consider the rationals
\mathbb{Q}
seen as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module. Every
\mathbb{Z}
-linear endomorphism
f:\mathbb{Q}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{Q}
is also
\mathbb{Q}
-linear, therefore
f(x)=\frac{p}{q}x
for some fixed
\frac{p}{q}\in\mathbb{Q}
. Therefore, every
\mathbb{Z}
-linear endomorphism
f:\mathbb{Q}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{Q}
is either zero or an isomorphism. If
f
is zero, then as we will see in Lemma
6.7
,
f
is Drazin with
f^{D}=0
. On the other hand if
f
is an isomorphism, so
f(x)=\frac{p}{q}x
with
p,q\neq 0
, then as we we will see in Lemma
3.3
,
f
is Drazin with
f^{D}(x)=f^{-1}(x)=\frac{q}{p}x
. So it follows that
\mathbb{Q}
is Drazin. Modules of finite length are always finitely generated, while
\mathbb{Q}
is famously not finitely generated as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module, and therefore cannot be of finite length as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module. So
\mathbb{Q}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-module which is Drazin but not of finite length. As such, the category of finite length
\mathbb{Z}
-modules is a proper subcategory of
\mathsf{D}\left(\mathbb{Z}\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}\right)
.

2.5
Finite Sets

Let
\mathsf{SET}
be the category of sets and functions between them, and let
\mathsf{FinSET}
be the full subcategory of finite sets. Not all Drazin inverses exist in
\mathsf{SET}
. As an example, consider the successor function
s:\mathbb{N}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}
,
s(n)=n+1
. Suppose that
s
had a Drazin inverse
s^{D}:\mathbb{N}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}
. By
[D.3]
, we would have that
s^{D}(n)=s^{D}(s^{n}(0))=s^{n}(s^{D}(0))=s^{D}(0)+n
. Now if
\mathsf{ind}(s)=k
, by
[D.1]
we compute that
k=s^{k}(0)=s^{D}(s^{k+1}(0))=s^{D}(k+1)=s^{D}(0)+k+1
. This implies that
0=s^{D}(0)+1
– which is a contradiction since
s^{D}(0)\in\mathbb{N}
. So the successor function does not have a Drazin inverse, and therefore
\mathsf{SET}
is not Drazin.

On the other hand,
\mathsf{FinSET}
does have all Drazin inverses. In fact, every finite set enriched category is Drazin. Recall that a category is finite set enriched if and only if every homset is finite.

Lemma 2.6

Every finite set enriched category is Drazin.

Proof:
Let
\mathbb{X}
be a finite set enriched category. Let
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
be an endomorphism in
\mathbb{X}
. Since
\mathbb{X}(X,X)
is a finite set, of cardinality
|\mathbb{X}(X,X)|
, we have that
x^{0}=1_{A},x,x^{2},x^{3},\ldots,
and
x^{|\mathbb{X}(X,X)|}
are not all distinct. So as explained in the proof of
[
21
, Prop 6.3]
, there is a
m\geq 0
and a
k\geq 1
with
m+k\leq|\mathbb{X}(X,X)|
, such that
x^{m}=x^{m+k}
. Moreover,
x^{n+rk}=x^{n}
for all
n\geq m
and
r\geq 0
, and so in particular
x^{mk}=x^{2mk}
, implying
x^{mk}
is an idempotent. We now consider the following cases:

(i)

If
m=0
, then
x
is an isomorphism with inverse
x^{k-1}
. Then as we will review in Lemma
3.3
,
x
is Drazin with
x^{D}=x^{k-1}
.

(ii)

If
k=1
, then
x^{m}=x^{m+1}
and
x^{m}
is an idempotent. So setting
x^{D}=x^{m}
, we check that three Drazin identities hold:

[D.1]

x^{m+1}x^{D}=x^{m+1}x^{m}=x^{m}x^{m}=x^{m}

[D.2]

x^{D}xx^{D}=x^{m}xx^{m}=x^{m+1}x^{m}=x^{m}x^{m}=x^{m}

[D.3]

x^{D}x=x^{m}x=x^{m+1}=xx^{m}=xx^{D}

So
x^{D}
is the Drazin inverse of
x
.

(iii)

If
m\geq 1
and
k\geq 2
, then
mk-1\geq m\geq 0
. So setting
x^{D}=x^{mk-1}
, we show that the three Drazin axioms hold:

[D.1]

x^{mk+1}x^{D}=x^{mk+1}x^{mk-1}=x^{mk+1+mk-1}=x^{2mk}=x^{mk}

[D.2]

x^{D}xx^{D}=x^{mk-1}xx^{mk-1}=x^{mk-1+1+mk-1}=x^{mk-1+mk}=x^{mk-1}=x^{D}

[D.3]

xx^{D}=xx^{mk-1}=x^{1+mk-1}=x^{mk-1+1}=x^{mk-1}x=x^{D}x

So
x^{D}
is the Drazin inverse of
x
.

So, every
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin, and therefore
\mathbb{X}
is Drazin.

\Box

Corollary 2.7

FinSET
is a Drazin category.

When
X
is a finite set, another way of understanding the Drazin inverse of a function
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
, is to consider the inclusion of subsets
X\supseteq\mathsf{im}(f)\supseteq\mathsf{im}(f^{2})\supseteq\ldots
, which must eventually stabilize after at most
|X|
steps. So there is a smallest
k
such that
\mathsf{im}(f^{k})=\mathsf{im}(f^{k+1})=\ldots
. Then
f
becomes an isomorphism on
\mathsf{im}(f^{k})
, which we denote as
f|_{\mathsf{im}(f^{k})}:\mathsf{im}(f^{k})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{im}(f^{k})
. The Drazin inverse of
f
is defined as
f^{D}(x)=f|_{\mathsf{im}(f^{k})}^{-1}(f^{k}(x))
, and its Drazin index is
\mathsf{ind}(f)=k
.

On the other hand, while
\mathsf{SET}
may not be Drazin, we may still ask what are the Drazin objects in
\mathsf{SET}
. It turns out that they are precisely the finite sets.

Lemma 2.8

A set
X
is Drazin in
\mathsf{SET}
if and only if
X
is a finite set. Therefore
\mathsf{D}\left(\mathsf{SET}\right)=\mathsf{FinSET}
.

Proof:
By Corollary
2.7
, we know that finite sets are always Drazin. So, it remains to show if a set is infinite, that it cannot be Drazin. So suppose that
X
is an infinite set, then there is an injection
\phi:\mathbb{N}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
. Writing
\phi_{n}\colon=\phi(n)
, define the function
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
as
f(x)=x
if
x\notin\mathsf{im}(\phi)
, otherwise if
x=\phi_{n}
then set
f(x)=\phi_{n+1}
– which is well-defined since
\phi
is injective. So in particular,
f^{n}(\phi_{m})=\phi_{m+n}
. If
X
is Drazin,
f
would have a Drazin inverse
f^{D}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
and, if
\mathsf{ind}(f)=k
, then by
[D.1]
we would have that:

\phi_{k}=f^{k}(\phi_{0})=f^{D}(f^{k+1}(\phi_{0}))=f^{D}(\phi_{k+1})

So
f^{D}(\phi_{k+1})=\phi_{k}
. Now consider
f^{D}(\phi_{0})
. There are two possible cases:

(i)

Case 1:
f^{D}(\phi_{0})\notin\mathsf{im}(\phi)
. By definition of
f
, we have that
f^{k+1}(f^{D}(\phi_{0}))=f^{D}(\phi_{0})
. However by
[D.1]
and
[D.3]
:

f^{D}(\phi_{0})=f^{k+1}(f^{D}(\phi_{0}))=f^{D}(f^{k+1}(\phi_{0}))=f^{k}(\phi_{0})=\phi_{k}

So
f^{D}(\phi_{0})=\phi_{k}
which means
f^{D}(\phi_{0})\in\mathsf{im}(\phi)
, which is a contradiction.

(ii)

Case 2:
f^{D}(\phi_{0})\in\mathsf{im}(\phi)
. So there is some
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
f^{D}(\phi_{0})=\phi_{n}
. But by
[D.2]
, we get that:

\phi_{k}=f^{D}(\phi_{k+1})=f^{D}(f^{k+1}(\phi_{0}))=f^{k+1}(f^{D}(\phi_{0}))=f^{k+1}(\phi_{n})=\phi_{n+k+1}

Since
\phi
is injective, this would imply that
k=n+k+1
. In turn, this implies that
0=n+1
, which is a contradiction since
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Since both cases lead to a contradiction, we conclude that
X
cannot be an infinite set. Therefore, if
X
is Drazin,
X
must be a finite set. So
\mathsf{D}\left(\mathsf{SET}\right)=\mathsf{FinSet}
as desired.

\Box

3
Properties of Drazin Inverses

In this section, we review some well-known properties of Drazin inverses from ring theory literature, as well as providing some new properties of Drazin inverses with a more categorical flavour. In particular, we show that many basic categorical constructions behave nicely with respect to Drazin inverses. Thus, when applying these constructions to Drazin categories, we obtain new Drazin categories. Again, throughout this section we work in an arbitrary category
\mathbb{X}
.

3.1
Drazin maps whose composite is not Drazin.

We begin with the important observation that, unfortunately, Drazin inverses do not behave well with respect to composition. So here we take the opportunity to exhibit two Drazin endomorphisms whose composite is not Drazin. We first observe that a function
f:\mathbb{N}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{N}
which is strictly increasing (i.e.
f(n)>n
) and
order-preserving (i.e. if
m\leq n
then
f(m)\leq f(n)
) cannot be Drazin. Indeed, suppose that there is a Drazin inverse
f^{D}
with index
k
, then by
[D.1]
and
[D.3]
we get that:

f^{k}(0)=f^{D}(f(f^{k}(0)))=f(f^{k}(f^{D}(0)))>f^{k}(f^{D}(0))\geq f^{k}(0).

So
f^{k}(0)>f^{k}(0)
, which is a contradiction, and so
f
does not have a Drazin inverse. Now consider the two idempotents:

e(n)=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{ll}n+1&\mbox{if $n$ is even}\\
n&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.~~~~~e^{\prime}(n)=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{ll}n&\mbox{if $n$ is even}\\
n+1&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

As these are idempotents they certainly have a Drazin inverse: namely, themselves (as we will explain in Lemma
5.2
). Their composite
is the function:

e^{\prime}(e(n))=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{ll}n+2&\mbox{if $n$ is even}\\
n+1&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

which is strictly increasing and order-preserving, and therefore not Drazin.

3.2
Strong
\pi
-Regularity

In ring theory, an important equivalent way of describing being Drazin is in terms of a being
strongly
\pi
-regular

[
3
, Sec 2]
and there is a extensive literature on strongly
\pi
-regular rings. This characterization of being Drazin is also valid for maps and objects in a category as we now explain.

Definition 3.1

x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is
strongly
\pi
-regular
if there exists endomorphisms
{y:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A}
and
z:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, and
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
such that
yx^{p+1}=x^{p}
and
x^{q+1}z=x^{q}
. An object
A
is said to be
strongly
\pi
-regular
if every endomorphism of
A
is strongly
\pi
-regular. Similarly, a category
\mathbb{X}
is said to be
strongly
\pi
-regular
if every endomorphism of
\mathbb{X}
is strongly
\pi
-regular (or equivalently if every object is strongly
\pi
-regular).

Lemma 3.2

[
12
, Thm 4]

x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin if and only if it
x
is strongly
\pi
-regular. Moreover, an object (resp. category) is Drazin if and only if it is strongly
\pi
-regular.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), suppose that
x
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
. Then set
y=z=x^{D}
and
p=q=k
. By Lemma
2.2
.(
i
), we get
x^{q+1}z=x^{q}
and
yx^{p+1}=x^{p}
as desired.

For (
\Leftarrow
), let
k=\mathsf{max}(p,q)
and define
x^{D}\colon=x^{k}z^{k+1}
. We show that
x^{D}
satisfies the three Drazin axioms:

[D.1]

First observe that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, by assumption it follows that
x^{k+n+1}z^{n+1}=x^{k}
. Therefore, we compute that:

x^{k+1}x^{D}=x^{k+1}x^{k}z^{k+1}=x^{k+k+1}z^{k+1}=x^{k}

[D.3]

First observe that
yx^{k+1}=x^{k}=x^{k+1}z
, and so

y^{n}x^{k}=y^{n+1}x^{k+1}=x^{k+1}z^{n+1}=x^{k}z^{n}

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. As such, we also have that
x^{D}=y^{k+1}x^{k}
. Using this, we compute that:

xx^{D}=xx^{k}z^{k+1}=x^{k+1}zz^{k}=x^{k}z^{k}=y^{k}x^{k}=y^{k}yx^{k+1}=y^{k+1}x^{k}x=x^{D}x

[D.2]

Using
[D.3]
and
[D.1]
, we have:

x^{D}xx^{D}=x^{D}xx^{k}z^{k+1}=x^{D}x^{k+1}z^{k+1}=x^{k+1}x^{D}z^{k+1}=x^{k}z^{k+1}=x^{D}

So we conclude that
x^{D}
is the Drazin inverse of
x
. From this it is immediete that either an object or a category, being strongly
\pi
-regular is equivalent to being Drazin as well.

\Box

We will revisit strong
\pi
-regularity in an
additive
category in Section
6.1
.

3.3
Isomorphisms

What are the endomorphisms whose Drazin index is zero? It turns out that they precisely correspond to isomorphisms. So every isomorphism is Drazin and its Drazin inverse is precisely its inverse. Thus the notion of a Drazin inverse is a true generalization of an inverse. This implies that identity morphisms are Drazin and their own Drazin inverse. Furthermore, it provides another basic source of Drazin categories as any groupoid is immediately Drazin.

Lemma 3.3

x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(x)=0
if and only if
x
is an isomorphism. In particular, the identity
1_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin and its own Drazin inverse,
1^{D}_{A}=1_{A}
.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), suppose that
x
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(x)=0
. In particular,
[D.1]
can be rewritten as
xx^{D}=1_{A}
. It follows from
[D.3]
, that we also have that
x^{D}x=1_{A}
. Therefore
x
is an isomorphism with inverse
x^{D}
. For (
\Leftarrow
), suppose that
x
is an isomorphism. Then its inverse
x^{-1}
satisfies the three Drazin axioms:
[D.1]
,
xx^{-1}=1_{A}=x^{0}
;
[D.2]

x^{-1}xx^{-1}=x^{-1}
; and
[D.3]

xx^{-1}=1_{A}=x^{-1}x
. So
x^{-1}
is the Drazin inverse of
x
. Moreover,
[D.1]
holds for
k=0
, thus
\mathsf{ind}(x)=0
.

\Box

3.4
Group Inverses

Another special case to consider is when the Drazin index is less than or equal to
1
. In the literature, this is better known as having a
group inverse

[
6
, Def 7.2.4]
. In other words, a group inverse is the Drazin inverse for endomorphisms with Drazin index less than or equal to
1
. Puystjens and Robinson described group inverses in an arbitrary category in
[
27
, Sec 2]
.

Definition 3.4

A
group inverse

[
27
, Sec 2]
of
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is an endomorphism
x^{D}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that the following equalities hold:

\textbf{[G.1]}~xx^{D}x=x;~~~~~\textbf{[G.2]}~x^{D}xx^{D}=x^{D};~~~~~\textbf{[G.3]}~x^{D}x=xx^{D}.

Lemma 3.5

x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(x)\leq 1
if and only if
x
has a group inverse.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), suppose that
x
is Drazin and
\mathsf{ind}(x)\leq 1
. We show that its Drazin inverse
x^{D}
is a group inverse. Note that
[G.2]
and
[G.3]
are the same as
[D.2]
and
[D.3]
. Since
\mathsf{ind}(x)\leq 1
, by Lemma
2.2
.(
i
), we have that
x^{2}x^{D}=x
, which using
[D.3]
can be rewritten as
xx^{D}x=x
, so
[G.1]
also holds. Therefore
x^{D}
is a group inverse of
x
. For (
\Leftarrow
), suppose that
x
has a group inverse
x^{D}
. Again, note that
[D.2]
and
[D.3]
are the same as
[G.2]
and
[G.3]
. Now using
[G.3]
, we can rewrite
[G.1]
as
x^{1+1}x^{D}=x
, so
[D.1]
holds for at least
k=1
. Therefore,
x
is Drazin with Drazin inverse
x^{D}
and
\mathsf{ind}(x)\leq 1
.

\Box

Since group inverses are special cases of Drazin inverses, they are unique. A special case of an endomorphism with a group inverse is an idempotent. Later, in Lemma
5.3
, we will provide an alternative description of an endomorphism with a group inverse as a commuting
binary idempotent
.

3.5
The Drazin Inverse of a Drazin Inverse

A Drazin inverse is always Drazin, and in fact its Drazin inverse is a group inverse, meaning that the Drazin index of the Drazin inverse is less than or equal to one. The Drazin inverse of a Drazin inverse is called its
core
and will play an important role in Section
6
.

Lemma 3.6

Let
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
be Drazin. Then:

(i)

[
12
, Thm 3]

x^{D}
is Drazin where
x^{DD}\colon=xx^{D}x
and
\mathsf{ind}(x^{D})\leq 1
;

(ii)

[
12
, Cor 4]

x^{DD}
is Drazin where
x^{DDD}=x^{D}
;

(iii)

If
\mathsf{ind}(x)\leq 1
, then
x^{DD}=x
.

Proof:
We begin by checking that
x^{DD}\colon=xx^{D}x
satisfies the three group inverse axioms.

[G.1]

By
[D.2]
twice, we compute that:

x^{D}x^{DD}x^{D}=x^{D}xx^{D}xx^{D}=x^{D}xx^{D}=x^{D}

[G.2]

By
[D.2]
twice, we compute that:

x^{DD}x^{D}x^{DD}=xx^{D}xx^{D}xx^{D}x=xx^{D}xx^{D}x=xx^{D}x=x^{DD}

[G.3]

By
[D.3]
we get that:

x^{D}x^{DD}=x^{D}xx^{D}x=xx^{D}xx^{D}=x^{DD}x^{D}

So
x^{DD}
is a group inverse of
x^{D}
. By Lemma
3.5
,
x^{D}
is Drazin with Drazin inverse
x^{DD}
and
\mathsf{ind}(x^{D})\leq 1
. Now by applying (
i
) to
x^{DD}
, we have that
x^{DD}
also has a Drazin inverse given by
x^{DDD}=x^{D}x^{DD}x^{D}
. Expanding this out and applying
[D.2]
twice again, we compute that:

x^{DDD}=x^{D}x^{DD}x^{D}=x^{D}xx^{D}xx^{D}=x^{D}xx^{D}=x^{D}

So
x^{D}
is the Drazin inverse of
x^{DD}
, or in other words,
x^{DDD}=x^{D}
. Lastly, if
\mathsf{ind}(x)\leq 1
, then by Lemma
3.5
,
x^{D}
is a group inverse of
x
. So by
[G.1]
, we have that
x^{DD}=xx^{D}x=x
.

\Box

3.6
Drazin inverses are absolute

We now turn our attention to other properties of Drazin inverse that are more categorically flavoured. We first observe that Drazin inverses are
absolute
, that is, every functor preserves Drazin inverses on the nose.

Proposition 3.7

Let
\mathsf{F}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{Y}
be a functor and let
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
be Drazin in
\mathbb{X}
. Then
\mathsf{F}(x):\mathsf{F}(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{F}(A)
is Drazin in
\mathbb{Y}
where
\mathsf{F}(x)^{D}=\mathsf{F}(x^{D})
and
\mathsf{ind}\left(\mathsf{F}(x)\right)\leq\mathsf{ind}(x)
.

Proof:
We show that
\mathsf{F}(x)^{D}\colon=\mathsf{F}(x^{D})
satisfies the three Drazin inverse axioms.

[D.1]

Let
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
. By
[D.1]
for
x
, we compute that:

\mathsf{F}(x)^{k+1}\mathsf{F}(x)^{D}=\mathsf{F}(x^{k+1})\mathsf{F}(x^{D})=\mathsf{F}(x^{k+1}x^{D})=\mathsf{F}(x^{k})=\mathsf{F}(x)^{k}

[D.2]

By
[D.2]
for
x
, we compute that:

\mathsf{F}(x)^{D}\mathsf{F}(x)\mathsf{F}(x)^{D}=\mathsf{F}(x^{D})\mathsf{F}(x)\mathsf{F}(x^{D})=\mathsf{F}(x^{D}xx^{D})=\mathsf{F}(x^{D})=\mathsf{F}(x)^{D}

[D.3]

By
[D.3]
for
x
, we have:

\mathsf{F}(x)^{D}\mathsf{F}(x)=\mathsf{F}(x^{D})\mathsf{F}(x)=\mathsf{F}(x^{D}x)=\mathsf{F}(xx^{D})=\mathsf{F}(x)\mathsf{F}(x^{D})=\mathsf{F}(x)\mathsf{F}(x)^{D}

So we conclude that
\mathsf{F}(x^{D})
is indeed the Drazin inverse of
\mathsf{F}(x)
. Moreover, by the calculation for
[D.1]
, we have that
\mathsf{ind}\left(\mathsf{F}(x)\right)\leq\mathsf{ind}(x)
.

\Box

Being Drazin is, furthermore, a property that is preserved by categorical equivalence. To see this, we first show that conjugation by an isomorphism preserves being Drazin.

Lemma 3.8

If
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin, then for any isomorphism
p:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, the composite
pxp^{-1}:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is also Drazin where
(pxp^{-1})^{D}\colon=px^{D}p^{-1}
.

Proof:
We show that
(pxp^{-1})^{D}\colon=px^{D}p^{-1}
satisfies the three Drazin inverse axioms.

[D.1]

Suppose that
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
. Now note that
(pxp^{-1})^{n}=px^{n}p^{-1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. So by
[D.1]
for
x
, we have:

(pxp^{-1})^{k+1}(pxp^{-1})^{D}=px^{k+1}p^{-1}px^{D}p^{-1}=px^{k+1}x^{D}p^{-1}=px^{k}p^{-1}=(pxp^{-1})^{k}

[D.2]

By
[D.2]
for
x
, we compute:

(pxp^{-1})^{D}pxp^{-1}(pxp^{-1})^{D}=px^{D}p^{-1}pxp^{-1}px^{D}p^{-1}=px^{D}xx^{D}p^{-1}=px^{D}p^{-1}=(pxp^{-1})^{D}

[D.3]

By
[D.3]
for
x
, we have:

\displaystyle(pxp^{-1})^{D}pxp^{-1}\!=\!px^{D}p^{-1}pxp^{-1}\!=\!px^{D}xp^{-1}\!=\!px^{D}xp^{-1}\!=\!pxp^{-1}px^{D}p^{-1}=pxp^{-1}(pxp^{-1})^{D}

So we conclude that
(pxp^{-1})^{D}
is indeed the Drazin inverse of
pxp^{-1}
as desired.

\Box

Corollary 3.9

If
\mathbb{Y}
is Drazin and
\mathbb{X}
is equivalent to
\mathbb{Y}
, then
\mathbb{X}
is Drazin.

Proof:
Suppose that
\mathsf{F}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{Y}
and
\mathsf{G}:\mathbb{Y}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
form an equivalence with natural isomorphisms
\eta_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{G}\mathsf{F}(A)
and
\epsilon_{B}:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{F}\mathsf{G}(B)
. Now for every endomorphism
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
in
\mathbb{X}
, since
\mathbb{Y}
is Drazin, we have that the endomorphism
\mathsf{F}(x):\mathsf{F}(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{F}(A)
is Drazin. Then by Proposition
3.7
, we have that
\mathsf{G}\mathsf{F}(x):\mathsf{G}\mathsf{F}(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{G}\mathsf{F}(A)
is Drazin. Then by Lemma
3.8
, we have that
\eta_{A}\mathsf{G}\mathsf{F}(x)\eta^{-1}_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin. Of course,
x=\eta_{A}\mathsf{G}\mathsf{F}(x)\eta^{-1}_{A}
, so
x
is Drazin, and therefore
\mathbb{X}
is also a Drazin category.

\Box

3.7
Commutative Squares

Drazin observed that the Drazin inverse of an endomorphism commutes with everything with which that endomorphism commutes
[
14
]
. Here we generalize this observation and show that the idea applies to commutative squares:

Proposition 3.10

Let
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
y:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
be Drazin. If the square on the left commutes, then the square on the right commutes:

\begin{array}[c]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 10.50624pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.50624pt\raise-14.68056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.52531pt\raise 4.35625pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.35625pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.97946pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.97946pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 73.44748pt\raise-14.68056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.44748pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.72374pt\raise-30.78885pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 66.72374pt\raise-30.78885pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 10.50624pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.50624pt\raise-14.68056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.35pt\raise 5.85934pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.85934pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x^{D}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.97946pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.97946pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.44748pt\raise-14.68056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.44748pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.72374pt\raise-30.78885pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.44116pt\raise-37.26068pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.24684pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{y^{D}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.72374pt\raise-30.78885pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.72374pt\raise-30.78885pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

Proof:
Let
k=\max\left(\mathsf{ind}(x),\mathsf{ind}(y)\right)
. By Lemma
2.2
.(
i
), we have that
y^{k+1}y^{D}=y^{k}
and
x^{D}x^{k+1}=x^{k}
. Moreover, by the left square, we have that
x^{k}f=fy^{k}
. So we first compute:

x^{D}x^{k}f=x^{D}fy^{k}=x^{D}fy^{k+1}y^{D}=x^{D}x^{k+1}fy^{D}=x^{k}fy^{D}=fy^{k}y^{D}

So
x^{D}x^{k}f=fy^{k}y^{D}
. From this, we also get that
(x^{D})^{k+1}x^{k}f=fy^{k}(y^{D})^{k+1}
. Now by Lemma
2.2
.(
iii
), recall that we also have that
(x^{D})^{k+1}x^{k}=x^{D}
and
y^{k}(y^{D})^{k+1}=y^{D}
. Then we compute that:

x^{D}f=(x^{D})^{k+1}x^{k}f=fy^{k}(y^{D})^{k+1}=fy^{D}

So the square on the right commutes as desired.

\Box

Proposition
3.10
will be quite useful for constructing new Drazin categories.

3.8
Iteration

A basic operation on an endomorphism is to iterate it. The iteration of an endomorphism which is Drazin is again Drazin, and the Drazin inverse of that iteration is just the iteration of the Drazin inverse.

Lemma 3.11

[
12
, Thm 2]
If
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin, with
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
, then for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
x^{n}
is Drazin where
(x^{n})^{D}=(x^{D})^{n}
and
\mathsf{ind}(x^{n})\leq k
.

Proof:
When
n=0
, by Lemma
3.3
, we know that
x^{0}=1_{A}
is Drazin with
(x^{0})^{D}=(x^{D})^{0}=1_{A}
and
\mathsf{ind}(x^{0})=0
which is less than or equal to
k
. So now assume that
n\geq 1
. We show
x^{n}
has Drazin index
k
with
(x^{n})^{D}\colon=(x^{D})^{n}
by checking the three Drazin axioms:

[D.1]

First observe that, from
[D.1]
for
x
, we have that
x^{k+n}(x^{D})^{n}=x^{k}
and thus:

(x^{n})^{k+1}(x^{n})^{D}=x^{nk+n}(x^{D})^{n}=x^{(n-1)k}x^{k+n}(x^{D})^{n}=x^{(n-1)k}x^{k}=x^{nk}=(x^{n})^{k}

[D.2]

By
[D.2]
and
[D.3]
for
x
, we compute that:

(x^{n})^{D}x^{n}(x^{n})^{D}=(x^{D})^{n}x^{n}(x^{D})^{n}=\left(x^{D}xx^{D}\right)^{n}=(x^{D})^{n}=(x^{n})^{D}

[D.3]

By
[D.3]
for
x
we have:

(x^{n})^{D}x^{n}=(x^{D})^{n}x^{n}=x^{n}(x^{D})^{n}=x^{n}(x^{n})^{D}

So we conclude that
x^{n}
is Drazin.

\Box

More surprisingly, we use Proposition
3.10
to show that if a non-trivial iteration of an endomorphism is Drazin then the original endomorphism must have been Drazin.

Lemma 3.12

x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin if and only if there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x^{k+1}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), this is immediate by Lemma
3.11
. For (
\Leftarrow
), suppose that for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
x^{k+1}
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(x^{k+1})=p
. Define
x^{D}\colon=(x^{k+1})^{D}x^{k}
. We show that
x^{D}
satisfies the three Drazin axioms:

[D.1]

Using
[D.1]
for
x^{k+1}
, we compute that:

x^{kp+k+p+1}x^{D}=(x^{k+1})^{p+1}(x^{k+1})^{D}x^{k}=(x^{k+1})^{p}x^{k}=x^{kp+k+p}

[D.2]

Using
[D.2]
for
x^{k+1}
, we compute that:

x^{D}xx^{D}=(x^{k+1})^{D}x^{k}x(x^{k+1})^{D}x^{k}=(x^{k+1})^{D}x^{k+1}(x^{k+1})^{D}x^{k}=(x^{k+1})^{D}x^{k}=x^{D}

[D.3]

Since
xx^{k+1}=x^{k+1}x
, by Proposition
3.10
we get that
x(x^{k+1})^{D}=(x^{k+1})^{D}x
. So we get that
x^{k}(x^{k+1})^{D}=(x^{k+1})^{D}x^{k}=x^{D}
. Then using
[D.3]
for
x^{k+1}
, we get that:

x^{D}x=(x^{k+1})^{D}x^{k}x=(x^{k+1})^{D}x^{k+1}=x^{k+1}(x^{k+1})^{D}=xx^{k}(x^{k+1})^{D}=xx^{D}

So we conclude that
x
is Drazin.
\Box

3.9
Slice Categories

We now turn our attention to constructing Drazin categories from other Drazin categories. As a first example, we apply Proposition
3.10
to show that slice and coslice categories of a Drazin category are again Drazin. Indeed, for a category
\mathbb{X}
and an object
X
, consider the slice category
\mathbb{X}/X
and the underlying functor
\mathsf{U}:\mathbb{X}/X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
. Now by Proposition
3.7
, we know that if
x:(f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X)
has a Drazin inverse in
\mathbb{X}/X
then
U(x)=x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
will have a Drazin inverse in
\mathbb{X}
where
\mathsf{U}(x)^{D}=\mathsf{U}(x^{D})
. Now
\mathsf{U}
not only preserves Drazin inverses,
\mathsf{U}
also reflects them. This follows immediately from the following concrete observation:

Lemma 3.13

Let
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
be Drazin. Then if the diagram on the left commutes, then the diagram on the right commutes:

\begin{array}[c]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern Proof:
Apply Proposition
3.10
by setting
y=1_{X}
, which recall is Drazin by Lemma
3.3
.

\Box

Corollary 3.14

If
\mathbb{X}
is a Drazin category then for every object
X\in\mathbb{X}
, the slice category
\mathbb{X}/X
(respectively, the coslice
X/\mathbb{X}
) is Drazin.

More generally it is not hard to see that the same is true for comma categories.

Corollary 3.15

For functors
\mathsf{F}:\mathbb{Y}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
and
\mathsf{G}:\mathbb{Z}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
, if
\mathbb{Y}
and
\mathbb{Z}
are Drazin, then the comma category
\mathsf{F}/\mathsf{G}
is Drazin.

Proof:

An endomorphism on
a
in
\mathsf{F}/\mathsf{G}
is a pair of maps
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
and
g:Y\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
such that
the left diagram below commutes. As
F(f^{D})=F(f)^{D}
and
G(g^{D})=G(g)^{D}
then by Proposition
3.10
we have:

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{G(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G(y)}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g~~~~~~\Rightarrow}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{D}}
\textstyle{F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)^{D}}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{G(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G(y)^{D}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{D}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{G(Y)}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{G(Y)}
\textstyle{Y}

\Box

3.10
Finitely Specified Structures

We can also use Proposition
3.10
to show that for any Drazin category, finitely specified structures and their morphisms will also form a Drazin category.

Lemma 3.16

When
\mathbb{X}
is a Drazin category, if
(\mathbb{S},{\cal F},{\cal L})
is a sketch which involves only finitely many objects, then the category of models
{\sf Mod}((\mathbb{S},{\cal F},{\cal L}),\mathbb{X})
is Drazin.

Proof:

The Drazin inverse of a natural transformation (morphism of models) must be taken pointwise. Thus Proposition
3.10
assures us that this will result in a morphism of the models. In order to have an index which works globally for all the objects of
{\cal F}
, we must ensure that there is an index which works at each component of the natural transformation. To obtain this we have to take the maximum of the indices at each component, however, this is only guaranteed to be defined if there are only finitely many objects – hence the caveat.

\Box

As such, the category of
any
finitely specified structure over a Drazin category is itself Drazin. For example, the arrow category of a Drazin category is Drazin, and the category of directed graphs and graph morphisms of a Drazin category is Drazin. Many algebraic structures have a finite specification so this provides many examples: thus, monoids and rings (with respect to products) in a Drazin category form a Drazin category.

3.11
Algebras of Endofunctors

Another interesting application of Proposition
3.10
is that an endomorphism of an algebra of an endofunctor has a Drazin inverse whenever the underlying endomorphism has a Drazin inverse (and similarly for coalgebras). Recall that for an endofunctor
{\mathsf{T}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}}
, a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra is a pair
(A,\nu)
consisting of an object
A\in\mathbb{X}
and a map
\nu:\mathsf{T}(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, and that a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra morphism
f:(A,\nu)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,\nu^{\prime})
is a map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
\mathsf{T}(f)\nu^{\prime}=\nu f
.

Lemma 3.17

Let
x:(A,\nu)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,\nu)
be a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra endomorphism. If
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin, then
x^{D}:(A,\nu)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,\nu)
is a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra endomorphism.

Proof:
Suppose that
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin. By Proposition
3.7
,
\mathsf{T}(x):\mathsf{T}(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{T}(A)
is also Drazin where
\mathsf{T}(x)^{D}=\mathsf{T}(x^{D})
. By definition of being a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra endomorphism, the diagram below on the left commutes. Then by Proposition
3.10
, the diagram on the right commutes:

\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{T}(x)}
\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{A}

\scriptstyle{\Rightarrow}

\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{T}(x)^{D}=\mathsf{T}(x^{D})}
\textstyle{\mathsf{T}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x^{D}}
\textstyle{A}

However, the diagram on the right is precisely the statement that
x^{D}:(A,\nu)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,\nu)
is a
\mathsf{T}
-algebra endomorphism.

\Box

Therefore, it follows that the category of (co)algebras of any endofunctor on a Drazin category is again Drazin. For an endofunctor
\mathsf{T}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
, we denote
\mathsf{ALG}(\mathsf{T})
(resp.
\mathsf{COALG}(\mathsf{T})
) to be the category of
\mathsf{T}
-(co)algebras and
\mathsf{T}
-(co)algebra morphisms between them.

Corollary 3.18

If
\mathbb{X}
is Drazin, then
\mathsf{ALG}(\mathsf{T})
and
\mathsf{COALG}(\mathsf{T})
are Drazin categories.

It follows that the (co)Eilenberg-Moore category of a (co)monad on a Drazin category is also a Drazin category.

3.12
Chu Construction

Surprisingly, another important construction that behaves well with Drazin inverses is the
Chu construction
, which is a construction for building star-autonomous categories from monoidal closed categories
[
4
]
. We will show that if a monoidal category is Drazin, then its Chu construction is Drazin as well. In particular, we can apply the Chu construction to the category of finite sets or the category of finite-dimensional vector spaces to yield examples of Drazin star-autonomous categories.

For a monoidal category
\mathbb{X}
, we denote the monoidal product by
\otimes
and the monoidal unit by
I
. For the rest of this section we work in a monoidal category
\mathbb{X}
. We first observe that the monoidal product preserves Drazin inverses:

Lemma 3.19

If
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
y:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
are Drazin, then
x\otimes y:A\otimes B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A\otimes B
is Drazin where
(x\otimes y)^{D}=x^{D}\otimes y^{D}
.

Proof:
It is straightforward to see that if
x
and
y
are Drazin in
\mathbb{X}
, then
(x,y)
is also Drazin in
\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}
with
(x,y)^{D}=(x^{D},y^{D})
. Then since the monoidal product is a functor
\otimes:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
, by Proposition
3.7
we get that
\otimes(x,y)=x\otimes y
is Drazin with Drazin inverse
\otimes(x,y)^{D}=\otimes(x^{D},y^{D})
, which is precisely that
(x\otimes y)^{D}=x^{D}\otimes y^{D}
.

\Box

Now for each object
Z\in\mathbb{X}
, define
{\sf Chu}(\mathbb{X},Z)
, to be the category whose objects are triples
(X,Y,a)
consisting of objects
X
and
Y
and a map
a:X\otimes Y\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Z
, and whose maps are pairs
(f,g):(X,Y,a)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},a^{\prime})
consisting of maps
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X^{\prime}
and
g:Y^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
such that
(f\otimes 1_{Y})a=(1_{A}\otimes g)a^{\prime}
. Identities are
(1_{X},1_{Y}):(X,Y,a)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(X,Y,a)
, and composition is
(f,g)(h,k)=(fh,kg)
. It is well known that if
\mathbb{X}
is a monoidal closed category with finite limits that
{\sf Chu}(\mathbb{X},Z)
is a star-autonomous category.

Proposition 3.20

(x,y):(X,Y,a)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(X,Y,a)
in
{\sf Chu}(\mathbb{X},Z)
is Drazin whenever
x
and
y
are Drazin in
\mathbb{X}
.

Proof:

Let
(x,y):(X,Y,a)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(X,Y,a)
be an endomorphism in
{\sf Chu}(\mathbb{X},Z)
, so we have
(x\otimes 1_{Y})a=(1_{X}\otimes y)a
. Also, suppose that
x
and
y
are Drazin in
\mathbb{X}
. We first show that
(x^{D},y^{D}):(X,Y,a)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(X,Y,a)
is also an endomorphism in the Chu construction. So let
j=\mathsf{max}\left(\mathsf{ind}(x),\mathsf{ind}(y)\right)
. Using Lemma
2.2
we compute that:

\displaystyle(x^{D}\otimes 1_{Y})a=((x^{D})^{j+1}\otimes 1_{Y})(x^{j}\otimes 1_{Y})a=((x^{D})^{j+1}\otimes 1_{Y})(1_{X}\otimes y^{j})a

\displaystyle=\!(\!(x^{D})^{j+1}\otimes\!1_{Y})(1_{X}\otimes\!(y^{D})^{j+1})(1_{X}\otimes y^{j+j+1})a\!=\!(\!(x^{D})^{j+1}\otimes 1_{Y})(x^{j+j+1}\otimes 1_{Y})(1_{X}\otimes(y^{D})^{j+1})a

\displaystyle=(x^{j}\otimes 1_{Y})(1_{X}\otimes(y^{D})^{j+1})a=(1_{X}\otimes y^{j})(1_{X}\otimes(y^{D})^{j+1})a=(1_{X}\otimes y^{D})a

So
(x^{D}\otimes 1_{Y})a=(1_{X}\otimes y^{D})a
, and therefore
(x^{D},y^{D}):(X,Y,a)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(X,Y,a)
is indeed a map in
{\sf Chu}(\mathbb{X},Z)
. From here, by the definition of composition in
{\sf Chu}(\mathbb{X},Z)
, it is straightforward to see that
(x^{D},y^{D})
is a Drazin inverse of
(x,y)
.

\Box

Corollary 3.21

If
\mathbb{X}
is a monoidal category which is Drazin then
{\sf Chu}(\mathbb{X},Z)
is Drazin.

4
Rank

For a complex square matrix
A
, an intuitive way of finding its Drazin inverse is to iterate
A
until the rank does not change (which is always guaranteed to happen), that is,
\mathsf{rank}(A^{k})=\mathsf{rank}(A^{k+1})=\mathsf{rank}(A^{k+2})=\ldots
. When this happens, one can reverse any later iterations and thus build a Drazin inverse. The same principle holds true for linear endomorphisms on a finite-dimensional vector space or endomorphisms on a finite set. The purpose of this section is to make this procedure rigorous. This involves, in particular, making precise what is meant by “rank”. As such, we introduce the concept of
expressive rank
for an arbitrary category and show that any category with expressive rank is Drazin.

4.1
Expressive Rank

In linear algebra, the rank of a matrix or a linear transformation is the dimension of its image space. We wish to generalize this in a category by associating every map to a natural number which represents its rank. We express this in terms of a
colax
functor into a 2-category which we call
\mathsf{Rank}
2
2

2

Steve Lack pointed out to us that
\mathsf{Rank}
is precisely the span category for the natural numbers.
. To help with notation, for
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
we denote
n\wedge m=\mathsf{min}(n,m)
.
\mathsf{Rank}
is the 2-category defined as follows:

[0-cells]
:
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

[1-cells]
:
m:n_{1}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}n_{2}
where
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\leq n_{1}\wedge n_{2}
, the identity on
n
is
n:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}n
, and composition of
m_{1}:n_{1}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}n_{2}
and
m_{2}:n_{2}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}n_{3}
is
m_{1}\wedge m_{2}:n_{1}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}n_{3}
;

[2-cells]
:
m_{1}\Rightarrow m_{2}
if and only if
m_{1}\leq m_{2}
.

For a category
\mathbb{X}
, by a colax functor
\mathsf{rank}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{Rank}
, we mean a mapping which associates objects
A
of
\mathbb{X}
to
0
-cells
\mathsf{rank}(A)\in\mathbb{N}
, maps
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
of
\mathbb{X}
to
1
-cells
\mathsf{rank}(f):\mathsf{rank}(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{rank}(B)
, so in particular
\mathsf{rank}(f)\leq\mathsf{rank}(A)
and
\mathsf{rank}(f)\leq\mathsf{rank}(B)
. We also ask that a colax functor preserves identities,
\mathsf{rank}(1_{A})=\mathsf{rank}(A)
, while for composition we only require that
\mathsf{rank}(fg)\leq\mathsf{rank}(f)\wedge\mathsf{rank}(g)
. For objects, we think of
\mathsf{rank}(A)
as the dimension of
A
, while for maps we think of
\mathsf{rank}(f)
as the rank of
f
.

The notion of expressive rank is defined on a category with a
factorization system
3
3

3

In this paper, by a factorization system we mean an
orthogonal
factorization system.
, and we ask that the colax functor into
\mathsf{Rank}
be compatible with the factorization system as well as reflects isomorphisms. To this end, we note that the only 1-cell isomorphisms in
\mathsf{Rank}
are the identity 1-cells
n:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}n
. Moreover, given any 1-cell
r:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m
we may factorize the map as
n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to15.02814pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\scriptsize$r$}}}r\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to15.02814pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\scriptsize$r$}}}m
. Thus setting
\mathcal{E}=\{r:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}r|~\forall n,r\in\mathbb{N},r\leq n\}
and
\mathcal{M}=\{r:r\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}n|~\forall n,r\in\mathbb{N},r\leq n\}
, we get a factorization system on the
1
-cells.

Definition 4.1

A category
\mathbb{X}
is said to have
expressive rank
if:

[ER.1]

\mathbb{X}
comes equipped with a colax functor
\mathsf{rank}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{Rank}
;

[ER.2]

\mathbb{X}
has a factorization system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
which
expresses rank
, that is, for every map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, if the diagram on the left is a factorization of
f
with
\varepsilon_{f}\in\mathcal{E}
and
m_{f}\in\mathcal{M}
,

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{x}}
\textstyle{{\sf im}(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{f}}
\textstyle{B}

\textstyle{\mathsf{rank}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{rank}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{rank}(\varepsilon_{f})}
\textstyle{\mathsf{rank}({\sf im}(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{rank}(m_{f})}
\textstyle{\mathsf{rank}(B)}

then
\mathsf{rank}(f)=\mathsf{rank}(\varepsilon_{f})=\mathsf{rank}(m_{f})=\mathsf{rank}({\sf im}(f))
.

[ER.3]

\mathsf{rank}
reflects isomorphisms, that is, if
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
\mathsf{rank}(f)=\mathsf{rank}(A)=\mathsf{rank}(B)
, then
f
is an isomorphism.

In a category
\mathbb{X}
with expressive rank, we call
\mathsf{rank}(A)
the
dimension
of an object
A
, and we call
\mathsf{rank}(f)
the
rank
of a map
f
.

Here are now some examples of expressive rank.

4.2
Matrices

For a field
k
,
\mathsf{MAT}(k)
has expressive rank, where the factorization system is the usual surjection-injection factorization system,
\mathsf{rank}(n)=n
, and for a matrix
A
,
\mathsf{rank}(A)
is the usual rank of the matrix.

4.3
Finite-Dimensional Vector Spaces

Slightly generalizing the above example, for a field
k
,
k\text{-}\mathsf{FVEC}
also has expressive rank, where the factorization system is the usual surjection-injection factorization system, for a finite-dimensional vector space
V
,
\mathsf{rank}(V)=\mathsf{dim}(V)
, and for a linear transformation
f
,
\mathsf{rank}(f)=\mathsf{dim}(\mathsf{im}(f))
, which recall is called the rank of
f
.

4.4
Finite Sets

\mathsf{FinSet}
has expressive rank, where the factorization system is the usual surjection-injection factorization system, for a finite set
X
,
\mathsf{rank}(X)=|X|
, and for a function
f
,
\mathsf{rank}(f)=|\mathsf{im}(f)|
.

4.5
Finite Length Modules

Let
R
be a ring. Recall that for an
R
-module
M
, a
composition series
of
M
is a finite chain of submodules
0=M_{0}\subseteq M_{1}\subseteq M_{2}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq M_{n}=M
such that the quotients
M_{i+1}/M_{i}
are simple. Then
M
is said to be of
finite length

n
if it has a composition series of length
n
, and we call
\mathsf{length}(M)=n
the length of
M
. The
Jordan-Hölder theorem

[
18
, Page 108]
says that this length of decompositions and the factors are unique. Equivalently,
M
has finite length if and only if
M
is Artinian and Noetherian
[
18
, Theorem 3.5]
. So let
R\text{-}\mathsf{FLMOD}
be the category of
R
-modules of finite length and
R
-linear morphisms between them. Then
R\text{-}\mathsf{FLMOD}
has expressive rank, where the factorization system is again the usual surjection-injection factorization system, for a finite length
R
-module
M
,
\mathsf{rank}(M)=\mathsf{length}(M)
, and for an
R
-linear morphism
f
between finite length
R
-modules,
\mathsf{rank}(f)=\mathsf{length}(\mathsf{im}(f))
. It is clear that this gives a colax functor
\mathsf{rank}:R\text{-}\mathsf{FLMOD}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{Rank}
and that the factorization system expresses rank, but it is not immediately clear why it reflects isomorphisms. So here is an explanation for why
\mathsf{rank}
indeed reflects isomorphisms. It is well-known that if
0\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M_{1}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M_{2}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M_{3}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}0
is a short exact sequence of
R
-modules, then
M_{2}
has finite length if and only if
M_{1}
and
M_{3}
have finite length, and in this case
\mathsf{length}(M_{2})=\mathsf{length}(M_{1})+\mathsf{length}(M_{3})
. So in particular if
f:M\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}N
is a
R
-linear morphism between finite length
R
-modules, then since
0\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}N\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}N/{\sf im}(f)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}0
is a short-exact sequence and
N
has finite length, as do
\mathsf{im}(f)
and
N/{\sf im}(f)
, and moreover
\mathsf{length}(N)=\mathsf{length}(\mathsf{im}(f))+\mathsf{length}(N/\mathsf{im}(f))
. Now suppose that
\mathsf{rank}(f)
is an isomorphism. This means that
\mathsf{rank}(f)=\mathsf{rank}(M)=\mathsf{rank}(N)
, so
M
,
N
, and
\mathsf{im}(f)
all have the same length. However if
\mathsf{length}(N)=\mathsf{length}(\mathsf{im}(f))
, then we must have that
\mathsf{length}(N/{\sf im}(f))=0
. The only
R
-module of length zero is
0
, so
N/{\sf im}(f)=0
. This gives us that
N={\sf im}(f)
, and so
f
is surjective. Finally, when
f
is surjective we have, similarly,
\mathsf{length}(\mathsf{ker}(f))+\mathsf{length}(M)=\mathsf{length}(N)
so the kernel is
0
and the map is also injective and so is an isomorphism. Thus,
\mathsf{rank}
reflects isomorphisms.

4.6
From Expressive Rank to Drazin

We now prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 4.2

A category which has an expressive rank is Drazin.

Proof:
Let
\mathbb{X}
be a category with expressive rank, so with colax functor
\mathsf{rank}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\sf Rank}
and factorization system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
. We will first show that for every endomorphism
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that the rank of
x^{k}
is equal to
x^{k+1}
. First observe that by colaxity, for any suitable maps we get the inequalities:
\mathsf{rank}(g)\geq\mathsf{rank}(f)\wedge\mathsf{rank}(g)\wedge\mathsf{rank}(h)\geq\mathsf{rank(fgh)}
. As such, for any endomorphism
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
we get a descending chain of inequalities:
\mathsf{rank}(A)=\mathsf{rank}(x^{0})\geq\mathsf{rank}(x^{1})\geq\mathsf{rank}(x^{2})\geq\ldots
. As the natural numbers are well-ordered there is an
r\in\mathbb{N}
which is the minimum of all these ranks, so
r=\mathsf{min}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(\mathsf{rank}(x^{n}))
. So set
k
to be the least natural number such that
\mathsf{rank}(x^{k})=r
. Once the sequence hits this rank, all subsequent ranks are equal, so
r=\mathsf{rank}(x^{k})=\mathsf{rank}(x^{k+1})=\ldots
.

Now consider a factorization of
x^{k}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
via
\varepsilon_{x^{k}}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\sf im}(x^{k})
and
m_{x^{k}}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\sf im}(x^{k})
, so
x^{k}=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}
with
\varepsilon_{x^{k}}\in\mathcal{E}
and
m_{x^{k}}\in\mathcal{M}
. In order to define the Drazin inverse, we will first show that the composite
m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}
is an isomorphism, and so is the unique map
\gamma_{x^{k}}:{\sf im}(x^{k})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\sf im}(x^{k})
induced by the factorization system which makes the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x^{k}}
\scriptstyle{x}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{x^{k}}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{x^{k}}}
\scriptstyle{x^{k}}
\textstyle{{\sf im}(x^{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{x^{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{x^{k}}}
\textstyle{{\sf im}(x^{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{x^{k}}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{X}

Now since
x^{k+1}=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}\gamma_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}
, by colaxity and
[ER.2]
, we get:

\displaystyle\mathsf{rank}(x^{k})\!=\!\mathsf{rank}({\sf im}(x^{k}))\!\geq\!\mathsf{rank}(\gamma_{x^{k}})\!\geq\!\mathsf{rank}(\varepsilon_{x^{k}}\gamma_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}})\!=\!\mathsf{rank}(x^{k+1})\!=\!\mathsf{rank}(x^{k})\!=\!\mathsf{rank}({\sf im}(x^{k})\!)

So we get that
\mathsf{rank}(\gamma_{x^{k}})=\mathsf{rank}({\sf im}(x^{k}))
. Then since
\mathsf{rank}
reflects isomorphism, by
[ER.3]
we get that
\gamma_{x^{k}}
is an isomorphism. Now note that by transposing the above diagram we also get that the following diagram commutes as well:

\textstyle{{\sf im}(x^{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{x^{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{x^{k}}}
\textstyle{{\sf im}(x^{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{x^{k}}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{x^{k}}}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{x^{k}}}
\textstyle{{\sf im}(x^{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{x^{k}}}
\textstyle{{\sf im}(x^{k})}

Moreover, by definition of
\gamma_{x^{k}}
we also get that:

\varepsilon_{x^{k}}\gamma_{x^{k}}^{k}m_{x^{k}}=x^{k}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}=x^{k}x^{k}=x^{2k}

So
\varepsilon_{x^{k}}\gamma_{x^{k}}^{k}m_{x^{k}}=x^{2k}
. Also by
[ER.2]
, since
\mathsf{rank}({\sf im}(x^{k}))=\mathsf{rank}(m_{x^{k}})=\mathsf{rank}(\varepsilon_{x^{k}})
, we of course have that
\mathsf{rank}({\sf im}(x^{k}))=\mathsf{rank}(m_{x^{k}})\wedge\mathsf{rank}(\varepsilon_{x^{k}})
. Then using colaxity and
\mathsf{rank}(x^{k})=\mathsf{rank}(x^{2k})
, we have that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{rank}({\sf im}(x^{k}))=\mathsf{rank}(m_{x^{k}})\wedge\mathsf{rank}(\varepsilon_{x^{k}})\geq\mathsf{rank}(m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}})\geq\mathsf{rank}(\varepsilon_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{rank}(x^{2k})=\mathsf{rank}(x^{k})=\mathsf{rank}({\sf im}(x^{k}))

Thus
\mathsf{rank}({\sf im}(x^{k}))=\mathsf{rank}(m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}})
, and by
[ER.3]
, we obtain that
m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}
is also an isomorphism. Moreover, we also compute that:

\displaystyle\gamma^{k}_{x^{k}}=\gamma^{k}_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}})^{-1}=m_{x^{k}}x^{k}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}})^{-1}=m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}})^{-1}=m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}

So
\gamma_{x^{k}}^{k}=m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}
. So in particular,
\gamma_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}=\gamma_{x^{k}}^{k+1}
.

Finally, setting
x^{D}\colon=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}m_{x^{k}}
, we show that
x^{D}
satisfies the Drazin axioms:

[D.1]

Using
\gamma_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}=\gamma_{x^{k}}^{k+1}
, we compute that:

\displaystyle x^{k+1}x^{D}=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}\gamma_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}m_{x^{k}}=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}\gamma_{x^{k}}^{k+1}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}m_{x^{k}}=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}=x^{k}

[D.2]

Again using
\gamma_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}=\gamma_{x^{k}}^{k+1}
, we compute that:

\displaystyle x^{D}xx^{D}=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}m_{x^{k}}x\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}m_{x^{k}}=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}\gamma_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}m_{x^{k}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}\gamma_{x^{k}}^{k+1}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}m_{x^{k}}=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}m_{x^{k}}=x^{D}

[D.3]

Here we use that
\gamma_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}=\gamma_{x^{k}}^{k+1}=m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}\gamma_{x^{k}}
, so we compute that:

\displaystyle x^{D}x=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}m_{x^{k}}x=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}\gamma_{x^{k}}m_{x^{k}}=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k}m_{x^{k}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}\gamma_{x^{k}}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}m_{x^{k}}=x\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}m_{x^{k}}=xx^{D}

Thus
x
is Drazin with Drazin inverse
x^{D}
, and so we conclude that
\mathbb{X}
is Drazin.

\Box

4.7
Drazin Inverses in Proper Factorization Systems

The proof of Theorem
4.2
suggests that having a factorization system allows for an equivalent description of an endomorphism
x
being Drazin in terms of
\gamma_{x^{k}}
and
m_{x^{k}}\varepsilon_{x^{k}}
being isomorphisms. We show that this is true for a
proper
factorization system, that is a factorization system in which all the
{\cal E}
-maps are epic and all the
{\cal M}
-maps are monic.

Proposition 4.3

In a category
\mathbb{X}
with a proper factorization system
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{E})
, an endomorphism
{x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A}
is Drazin if and only if there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
for which
x^{k+1}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
has a factorization via
\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\sf im}(x^{k+1})\in\mathcal{E}
and
m_{x^{k+1}}:{\sf im}(x^{k+1})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A\in\mathcal{M}
for which
m_{x^{k+1}}\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}:{\sf im}(x^{k+1})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\sf im}(x^{k+1})
is an isomorphism.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), Suppose
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin and let
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
. Then for
x^{k+1}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, let
x^{k+1}
factor via
\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\sf im}(x^{k+1})
and
m_{x^{k+1}}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\sf im}(x^{k+1})
, so
x^{k+1}=\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}m_{x^{k+1}}
with
\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}\in\mathcal{E}
and
m_{x^{k+1}}\in\mathcal{M}
. Denote
\beta_{x^{k+1}}\colon=m_{x^{k+1}}\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}
. Now observe that by the factorization system,
\beta_{x^{k+1}}
is the unique map which makes the below diagram on the left commute. On the other hand by
[D.3]
, we have that
(x^{D})^{k}x^{k}=x^{k}(x^{D})^{k}
. Therefore, define
\beta_{x^{k+1}}^{-1}
as the unique map induced by the factorization system which makes the below diagram on the right commute:

28.16267pt\raise-24.9623pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.64435pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta^{-1}_{x^{k+1}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.26459pt\raise-31.86165pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise
