0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\sf im}(x^{k+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.83234pt\raise-48.15497pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.22874pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{m_{x^{k+1}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 78.52917pt\raise-56.8858pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise-63.7233pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.49037pt\raise-70.39514pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.09685pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(x^{D})^{k+1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 72.06116pt\raise-63.7233pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 72.06116pt\raise-63.7233pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

Now by Lemma
2.2
.(
iii
), we compute that:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}\beta_{x^{k+1}}\beta^{-1}_{x^{k+1}}m_{x^{k+1}}=x^{k+1}\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}m_{x^{k+1}}(x^{D})^{k+1}

\displaystyle=x^{k+1}x^{k+1}(x^{D})^{k+1}=x^{k+1+k+1}(x^{D})^{k+1}=x^{k+1}=\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}m_{x^{k+1}}

Since
\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}
is epic and
m_{x^{k+1}}
is monic, we get that
\beta_{x^{k+1}}\beta^{-1}_{x^{k+1}}=1_{{\sf im}(x^{k+1})}
. Similarly, we can also show that
\beta^{-1}_{x^{k+1}}\beta_{x^{k+1}}=1_{{\sf im}(x^{k+1})}
as well. So we conclude that
\beta_{x^{k+1}}\colon=m_{x^{k+1}}\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}
is an isomorphism as desired.

For (
\Leftarrow
), suppose that there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
x^{k+1}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, and a factorization
\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\sf im}(x^{k+1})\in\mathcal{E}
and
m_{x^{k+1}}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\sf im}(x^{k+1})\in\mathcal{M}
, that
\beta_{x^{k+1}}\colon=m_{x^{k+1}}\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}
is an isomorphism. Now consider the unique map induced by the factorization system
\gamma_{x^{k+1}}:{\sf im}(x^{k+1})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}{\sf im}(x^{k+1})
defined as in the proof of Theorem
4.2
. Recall that in that proof we needed
\gamma_{x^{k+1}}
to be an isomorphism to build the Drazin inverse, and we achieved this with an argument involving expressive rank. However, we will now show that from
\beta_{x^{k+1}}
being an isomorphism, it follows that
\gamma_{x^{k+1}}
is also an isomorphism. So by definition of
\gamma_{x^{k+1}}
, we first compute that:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}\gamma^{k+1}_{x^{k+1}}m_{x^{k+1}}=x^{k+1}\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}m_{x^{k+1}}=\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}m_{x^{k+1}}\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}m_{x^{k+1}}=\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}\beta_{x^{k+1}}m_{x^{k+1}}

Since
\varepsilon_{x^{k+1}}
is epic and
m_{x^{k+1}}
is monic, we get that
\gamma^{k+1}_{x^{k+1}}=\beta_{x^{k+1}}
. However, this implies that
\gamma_{x^{k+1}}
is an isomorphism with inverse
\gamma^{-1}_{x^{k+1}}=\gamma^{k}_{x^{k+1}}\beta^{-1}_{x^{k+1}}=\beta^{-1}_{x^{k+1}}\gamma^{k}_{x^{k+1}}
. Then setting
x^{D}\colon=\varepsilon_{x^{k}}(\gamma_{x^{k}}^{-1})^{k+1}m_{x^{k}}
, using the same calculations as in the proof of Theorem
4.2
, we get that
x^{D}
is a Drazin inverse of
x
.
\Box

5
Drazin Inverses and Idempotents

An important class of endomorphisms in any category are the idempotents, that is, endomorphisms
e:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
e^{2}=e
. In this section, we explore the connection between Drazin inverses and idempotents. In particular, we will show how to characterize Drazin maps in terms of isomorphisms in the idempotent splitting of a category – this displays an aspect of Drazin inverses which is categorically inspired. Moreover, this allows us to relate the notion of a Drazin inverse to Leinster’s concept of an eventual image
[
21
]
. Throughout this section, we work in an arbitrary category
\mathbb{X}
.

5.1
Idempotents

We begin by observing that every Drazin endomorphism induces an idempotent by composing it with its Drazin inverse.

Lemma 5.1

Let
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
be Drazin. Define the map
e_{x}\colon=x^{D}x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
(or equivalently by
[D.3]
as
e_{x}=xx^{D}
). Then
e_{x}
is an idempotent.

Proof:
This is immediate by
[D.2]
since:
e_{x}e_{x}\colon=x^{D}xx^{D}x=x^{D}x=e_{x}
.

\Box

On the other hand, every idempotent is Drazin and, indeed, is its own Drazin inverse. Moreover, every idempotent has Drazin index at most one, meaning that it is its own group inverse as well. The only idempotents with Drazin index zero are the identity maps. Thus, non-trivial idempotents have Drazin index one.

Lemma 5.2

An idempotent
e:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin, its own Drazin inverse,
e^{D}=e
, and
\mathsf{ind}(e)\leq 1
. Moreover,
\mathsf{ind}(e)=0
if and only if
e=1_{A}
.

Proof:
By Lemma
3.5
, to show that
e
is its own Drazin inverse and
\mathsf{ind}(e)\leq 1
, it suffices to show that it is its own group inverse. Then setting
e^{D}=e
, we clearly have that:
[G.1]

ee^{D}e=e
;
[G.2]

e^{D}ee^{D}=e^{D}
; and
[G.3]

e^{D}e=ee^{D}
. So
e
is its own group inverse, and therefore also its own Drazin inverse with
\mathsf{ind}(e)\leq 1
. Now if
\mathsf{ind}(e)=0
, then by Lemma
3.3
,
e
is an isomorphism and its Drazin inverse is its inverse. This would mean that
e^{-1}=e^{D}=e
, so
e
is its own inverse. However, by idempotency, this would imply that
1_{A}=ee^{-1}=ee=e
. So the only idempotent with Drazin index zero is the identity.

\Box

The converse is not true: that is not every Drazin endomorphism with Drazin index at most one is an idempotent. Instead, having a Drazin index less than or equal to one can be equivalently described in terms of
binary idempotents
.

5.2
Binary Idempotents

A
binary idempotent

[
29
, Def 9.2]
is a pair
(f,g)
consisting of maps
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
g:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
fgf=f
and
gfg=g
. If
(f,g)
is a binary idempotent, then
fg:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
gf:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
are both idempotents. Now when
A=B
, a
commuting binary idempotent
is a pair
(x,y)
consisting of endomorphisms
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
y:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
(x,y)
is a binary idempotent and
xy=yx
. For a commuting binary idempotent
(x,y)
we denote its induced idempotent as
e_{(x,y)}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, that is,
e_{(x,y)}=xy=yx
.

Lemma 5.3

x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(x)\leq 1
if and only if there is an endomorphism
x^{D}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
(x,x^{D})
is a commuting binary idempotent.

Proof:
Recall that by Lemma
3.5
,
x
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(x)\leq 1
if and only if
x
has a group inverse
x^{D}
. However, the requirements for
(x,x^{D})
being a commuting binary idempotent are precisely the same as saying that
x^{D}
is a group inverse of
x
.

\Box

While not every Drazin endomorphism and its Drazin inverse form a commuting binary idempotent, we can always obtain one involving the Drazin inverse.

Corollary 5.4

If
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin, then
(x^{D},x^{DD})
is a commuting binary idempotent. Moreover, the resulting idempotent is precisely
e_{(x^{D},x^{DD})}=e_{x}
.

Proof:
By Lemma
3.6
.(
i
), if
x
is Drazin, then
x^{D}
is also Drazin and
\mathsf{ind}(x^{D})\leq 1
. Then by Lemma
5.3
, it follows that
(x^{D},x^{DD})
is a commuting binary idempotent. Now recall that
x^{DD}=xx^{D}x
. Then using
[D.2]
, we have that:

e_{(x^{D},x^{DD})}=x^{D}x^{DD}=x^{D}xx^{D}x=x^{D}x=e_{x}

So
e_{(x^{D},x^{DD})}=e_{x}
as desired.

\Box

5.3
Drazin split

We can also consider the case when the induced idempotent of a Drazin endomorphism is
split
. Recall that an idempotent
e:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A

splits
(or is a
split idempotent
) if there are maps
r:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
s:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
rs=e
and
sr=1_{A}
.

Definition 5.5

x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is
Drazin split
if it is Drazin and the induced idempotent
e_{x}\colon=xx^{D}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
splits.

We can alternatively characterize Drazin split endomorphisms as follows:

Lemma 5.6

x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin split if and only if there is an idempotent
e:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, with splitting
r:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
s:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, a
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and an isomorphism
\alpha:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x^{k}}
\scriptstyle{x^{k}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{A}

Proof:
For
(\Rightarrow)
, suppose that
x
is Drazin split where
e_{x}
splits via
r:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
s:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, and with
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
. For the required split idempotent we take
e_{x}
, and for the natural number we take
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
. For the isomorphism, define the map
\alpha:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
as the composite
\alpha\colon=sxr
, and define the map
\alpha^{-1}:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
as the composite
\alpha^{-1}\colon=sx^{D}r
. We first check that these maps are inverses of each other. To do so, we use
[D.2]
,
[D.3]
, and the splitting:

\alpha\alpha^{-1}=sxrsx^{D}r=sxe_{x}x^{D}r=sxx^{D}xx^{D}r=sxx^{D}r=se_{x}r=srsr=1_{B}

\alpha^{-1}\alpha=sx^{D}rsxr=sx^{D}e_{x}xr=sx^{D}xx^{D}xr=sx^{D}xr=se_{x}r=srsr=1_{B}

So
\alpha
is indeed an isomorphism. Now we show that the diagram commutes. By
[D.1]
, the triangle on the left commutes:

x^{k}rs=x^{k}e_{x}=x^{k}xx^{D}=x^{k+1}x^{D}=x^{k}

By
[D.2]
,
[D.3]
, and the splitting, we can show that both the top and bottom squares commute:

xr=xr\alpha^{-1}\alpha=xrsx^{D}r\alpha=xe_{x}x^{D}r\alpha=xx^{D}xx^{D}r\alpha=xx^{D}r\alpha=e_{x}r\alpha=rsr\alpha=r\alpha

sx=\alpha\alpha^{-1}sx=\alpha sx^{D}rsx=\alpha sx^{D}e_{x}x=\alpha sx^{D}xx^{D}x=\alpha sx^{D}x=\alpha se_{x}=\alpha srs=\alpha s

So we conclude that the desired identities hold.

For (
\Leftarrow
), define
x^{D}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
as the composite
x^{D}\colon=r\alpha^{-1}s
. We show that
x^{D}
satisfies the three Drazin inverse axioms.

[D.1]

Using the
k\in\mathbb{N}
in the assumptions, and that
x^{k}e=e
and
r=xr\alpha^{-1}
we have:

x^{k+1}x^{D}=x^{k}xr\alpha^{-1}s=x^{k}rs=x^{k}e=x^{k}

[D.2]

Using
sx=\alpha s
, we compute that:

x^{D}xx^{D}=r\alpha^{-1}sxr\alpha^{-1}s=r\alpha^{-1}\alpha sr\alpha^{-1}s=rsr\alpha^{1}s=r\alpha^{-1}s=x^{D}

[D.3]

Using both
sx=\alpha s
and
xr=r\alpha
, we have:

x^{D}x=r\alpha^{-1}sx=r\alpha^{-1}\alpha s=rs=r\alpha\alpha^{-1}s=xr\alpha^{-1}s=xx^{D}

So
x
is Drazin with Drazin inverse
x^{D}
. Next, observe that the induced idempotent
e_{x}
is
e
since:

e_{x}=x^{D}x=r\alpha^{-1}sx=r\alpha^{-1}\alpha s=rs=e

So the induced idempotent is precisely the idempotent with which we started. Since
e
was assumed to be split, we conclude that
x
is Drazin split.

\Box

Recall that a category is
idempotent complete
if all its idempotents split. Similarly, we can consider categories in which every Drazin endomorphism Drazin splits:

Definition 5.7

A
Drazin complete category
is a category in which every endomorphism Drazin splits.

Lemma 5.8

A category is Drazin complete if and only if it is Drazin and idempotent complete.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), by definition of being Drazin complete, every endomorphism is Drazin split, so in particular Drazin, and therefore the category is Drazin. Moreover, again by definition of being Drazin complete, every idempotent is Drazin split and therefore since by Lemma
5.2
it is its own induced idempotent, every idempotent also splits. For (
\Leftarrow
), by definition of being Drazin and idempotent complete, every endomorphism is Drazin and the induced idempotent splits. Therefore, every endomorphism is Drazin split and so the category is Drazin complete.
\Box

Every Drazin category embeds into a Drazin complete category via the idempotent splitting construction. Indeed, recall that every category
\mathbb{X}
embeds into an idempotent complete category
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
called its idempotent splitting. The objects of
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
are pairs
(A,e)
consisting of an object
A
and an idempotent
e:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, and where a map
f:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e^{\prime})
is a map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
such that
efe^{\prime}=f
(or equivalently
ef=f=fe^{\prime}
). Composition in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
is defined as in
\mathbb{X}
, while the identity for
(A,e)
is the idempotent
e
, that is,
1_{(A,e)}\colon=e:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
. To show that the idempotent splitting of a Drazin category is Drazin complete, we first show that for any endomorphism in the idempotent splitting, whose underlying endomorphism in the base category is Drazin, has its Drazin inverse also an endomorphism in the idempotent splitting.

Lemma 5.9

If
x:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
is an endomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
and
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin in
\mathbb{X}
, then
x^{D}:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
is an endomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
, and moreover,
x:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
is Drazin with Drazin inverse
x^{D}:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
.

Proof:
Since
x:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
is a map in the idempotent splitting, we have that
exe=x
. Then using
[D.2]
and
[D.3]
, we compute that:

ex^{D}e=ex^{D}xx^{D}e=ex^{D}x^{D}xe=ex^{D}x^{D}x=exx^{D}x^{D}x=xx^{D}x^{D}=x^{D}xx^{D}=x^{D}

So
ex^{D}e=x^{D}
, and therefore we have that
x^{D}:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
is indeed an endomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. Since the composition in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
is the same as in
\mathbb{X}
, we get that
x^{D}:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
is also the Drazin inverse of
x:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
.

\Box

Proposition 5.10

If
\mathbb{X}
is a Drazin category, then
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
is Drazin complete.

Proof:
Since we know that
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
is idempotent complete, to show that it is also Drazin complete, by Lemma
5.8
we need only show that it is also Drazin. So consider
x:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. Since
\mathbb{X}
is Drazin,
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin. So by Lemma
5.9
, we have that
x:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
is also Drazin. So
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
is Drazin, and so we conclude that
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
is Drazin complete as well.

\Box

5.4
Intertwining idempotents

We now begin to build up towards showing that Drazin inverses give rise to isomorphisms in the idempotent splitting. Toward this goal, we first introduce a useful intermediate notion. Let
(e,e^{\prime})
be a pair of idempotents
e:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
e^{\prime}:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
. We shall say that a map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is
(e,e^{\prime})
-intertwined
if
ef=fe^{\prime}
, so the below diagram on the left commutes. We say that an
(e,e^{\prime})
-intertwined map
f
has an
(e,e^{\prime})
-intertwined inverse
in case there is a map
g:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
e^{\prime}ge=g
(or equivalently if
e^{\prime}g=g=ge
), and
fg=e
and
gf=e^{\prime}
, that is, the diagram on the right below commutes.

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 31.2149pt\raise 5.8pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.19176pt\raise-14.65623pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.57185pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{e^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.19176pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.56847pt\raise-30.8375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.74374pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.46803pt\raise-56.2239pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.56847pt\raise-15.41875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.74374pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.71072pt\raise-45.49373pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.35625pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{e}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-54.8375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise-30.8375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.19176pt\raise-45.49373pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.57185pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{e^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.19176pt\raise-54.8375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.56847pt\raise-46.25624pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.74374pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.46803pt\raise-58.9526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise-61.67499pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.2149pt\raise-67.47499pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise-61.67499pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise-61.67499pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\\
(e,e^{\prime})\mbox{-intertwined}&~&(e,e^{\prime})\mbox{-intertwined inverse}\end{matrix}

Lemma 5.11

Let
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
be a
(e,e^{\prime})
-intertwined map in
\mathbb{X}
. Then:

(i)

fe^{\prime}:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e^{\prime})
is a map in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
;

(ii)

If
f
has an
(e,e^{\prime})
-intertwined inverse
g:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, then
fe^{\prime}:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e^{\prime})
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
with inverse
g:(B,e^{\prime})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
.

Proof:
For (i), since
f
is
(e,e^{\prime})
-intertwined, we have that:

efe^{\prime}e^{\prime}=eefe^{\prime}=efe^{\prime}=eef=ef=fe^{\prime}

So
fe^{\prime}:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e^{\prime})
is indeed a map in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. For (ii), by definition of an
(e,e^{\prime})
-intertwined inverse, we have that
e^{\prime}ge=g
. So we get that
g:(B,e^{\prime})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
is indeed a map in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. Using that
fg=e
and
gf=e^{\prime}
, we can show that
fe^{\prime}
and
g
are inverses in the idempotent splitting:

\displaystyle fe^{\prime}g=efg=ee=e=1_{(A,e)}

\displaystyle gfe^{\prime}=e^{\prime}e^{\prime}=e^{\prime}=1_{(B,e^{\prime})}

So we conclude that
fe^{\prime}:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e^{\prime})
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
.
\Box

We can now characterize being Drazin in terms of being idempotent intertwined.

Lemma 5.12

x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin if and only if there is an idempotent
e:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x^{k}e=x^{k}
and

x
is an
(e,e)
-intertwined map with an
(e,e)
-intertwined inverse.

Proof:
For
(\Rightarrow)
, suppose that
x
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
. Take the idempotent
e
to be the induced idempotent
e_{x}
. Then by
[D.1]
, we have that:

x^{k}e_{x}=x^{k}xx^{D}=x^{k+1}x^{D}=x^{k}

So
x^{k}e_{x}=x^{k}
. Now by
[D.3]
, we have that:

e_{x}x=xx^{D}x=xe_{x}

So
x
is
(e_{x},e_{x})
-intertwined. Next, by
[D.2]
, we have that:

e_{x}x^{D}=x^{D}xx^{D}=x^{D}

Moreover, by definition, we also have that
xx^{D}=e_{x}
and
x^{D}x=e_{x}
. So we conclude that
x
has a
(e_{x},e_{x})
-intertwined inverse and it is
x^{D}
.

For
(\Leftarrow)
, let
x^{D}
be the
(e,e)
-intertwined inverse of
x
. We show that
x^{D}
satisfies the three Drazin inverse axioms.

[D.1]

Using that
xx^{D}=e
and
x^{k}e=x^{k}
, we compute that:

x^{k+1}x^{D}=x^{k}xx^{D}=x^{k}e=x^{k}

[D.2]

Using that
xx^{D}=e
and
x^{D}e=x^{D}
, we compute that:

x^{D}xx^{D}=x^{D}e=x^{D}

[D.3]

By definition of being an
(e,e)
-intertwined inverse, we have that
xx^{D}=e
and
x^{D}x=e
. So
xx^{D}=x^{D}x
.

So we conclude that
x
is Drazin with Drazin inverse
x^{D}
.
\Box

We can therefore completely capture the requirements of a Drazin inverse with the following commuting diagram:

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{x}}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{x}}
\scriptstyle{x^{D}}
\scriptstyle{x^{D}}
\scriptstyle{e_{x}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x^{k}}
\scriptstyle{x^{k}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{x}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{x}}
\scriptstyle{x^{D}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{A}

Moreover, by Lemma
5.11
, we see how being Drazin induces an isomorphism on its induced idempotent:

Corollary 5.13

Let
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
be Drazin in
\mathbb{X}
, then
xe_{x}:(A,e_{x})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{x})
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
with inverse
x^{D}:(A,e_{x})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{x})
.

Proof:
In the proof of Lemma
5.12
, we showed that
x
is
(e_{x},e_{x})
-intertwined and
x^{D}
is its
(e_{x},e_{x})
-intertwined inverse. Applying Lemma
5.11
.(
ii
), we get that
{xe_{x}\!:\!(A,e_{x})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{x})}
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
with inverse
x^{D}:\!(A,e_{x})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{x})
.
\Box

5.5
Munn’s Power Theorem

We now generalize Drazin’s
[
12
, Thm 7]
to the categorical context using idempotent splitting. In the context of semigroups, this result was expressed particularly succinctly by Munn as “An element
x
of a semigroup
S
is pseudo-invertible if and only if some power of
x
lies in a subgroup of
S
”
[
24
, Thm 1]
. We can understand this statement categorically using the idempotent splitting, that is, a map
x
is Drazin if and only if some positive power of
x
underlies an endo-isomorphism in the idempotent splitting.

Theorem 5.14

x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin in
\mathbb{X}
if and only if there is an idempotent
e:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
x^{k+1}:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), let
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
be Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
. By Lemma
3.11
, we know that
x^{k+1}
is also Drazin. Therefore by Corollary
5.13
,
x^{k+1}e_{x^{k+1}}:(A,e_{x^{k+1}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{x^{k+1}})
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. Now recall that
(x^{k+1})^{D}=(x^{D})^{k+1}
. Then by using
[D.3]
, we compute that:

e_{x^{k+1}}=(x^{k+1})(x^{k+1})^{D}=x^{k+1}(x^{D})^{k+1}=(xx^{D})^{k+1}=e_{x}^{k+1}=e_{x}

So
e_{x^{k+1}}=e_{x}
. Moreover, using
[D.1]
we compute that:

x^{k+1}e_{x}=x^{k+1}x^{D}x=x^{k}x=x^{k+1}

So
x^{k+1}e_{x}=x^{k+1}
. So we conclude that
x^{k+1}:(A,e_{x})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{x})
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
as desired.

For (
\Leftarrow
), this direction requires more work. So suppose that there is some
k
such that
x^{k+1}:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
with inverse
v:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
. Explicitly this means that the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle ex^{k+1}=x^{k+1}=x^{k+1}e

\displaystyle ev=v=ve

\displaystyle x^{k+1}v=1_{(A,e)}=e

\displaystyle vx^{k+1}=1_{(A,e)}=e

We will first show that
x^{k}
and
v
commute with each other. To do so, we use the above identities:

\displaystyle x^{k}v=x^{k}ev=x^{k}x^{k+1}vv=x^{k+1}x^{k}vv=ex^{k+1}x^{k}vv=ex^{k}x^{k+1}vv=ex^{k}ev=ex^{k}v

\displaystyle=vx^{k+1}x^{k}v=vx^{k}x^{k+1}v=vx^{k}e=vex^{k}e=vvx^{k+1}x^{k}e=vvx^{k}x^{k+1}e

\displaystyle=vvx^{k}x^{k+1}=vvx^{k+1}x^{k}=vex^{k}=vx^{k}

So
vx^{k}=x^{k}v
. Then define
x^{D}\colon=vx^{k}=x^{k}v
. We show that
x^{D}
satisfies the three Drazin inverse axioms.

[D.1]

Here we use that
x^{k+1}v=e
and
x^{k+1}e=x^{k+1}
:

x^{(k+1)+1}x^{D}=x^{k+1}xx^{k}v=x^{k+1}x^{k+1}v=x^{k+1}e=x^{k+1}

[D.2]

Here we use that
x^{k+1}v=e
and
ve=v
:

x^{D}xx^{D}=x^{k}vxx^{k}v=x^{k}vx^{k+1}v=x^{k}ve=x^{k}v=x^{D}

[D.3]

Here we use that
x^{k+1}v=e=vx^{k+1}
:

xx^{D}=xx^{k}v=x^{k+1}v=e=vx^{k+1}=vx^{k}x=x^{D}x

So we conclude that
x
is Drazin.
\Box

5.6
Eventual Image Duality

In
[
21
]
, Leinster introduced the concept of the
eventual image
of an endomorphism in a category. Naively, the idea is that after iterating an endomorphism enough times, it will eventually stabilize, and this is the eventual image. Since this concept of iterating an endomorphism until it stabilizes is also closely linked to Drazin inverses, it is natural to ask what the relationship is between Drazin inverses and Leinster’s eventual image. Here we answer this question by using the results relating Drazin inverses to idempotents. In particular, we show that every Drazin split endomorphism has an eventual image which is absolute.

Let
\mathbb{Z}
be the set of integers. Then
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
in
\mathbb{X}
has
eventual image duality

[
21
, Definition 2.1]
in the case the diagram
\mathsf{EI}(x):\mathbb{Z}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
given by the doubly infinite chain:

...\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to15.63461pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\scriptsize$x$}}}A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to15.63461pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\scriptsize$x$}}}A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to15.63461pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\scriptsize$x$}}}A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to15.63461pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\hbox{\scriptsize$x$}}}...

has both a limit cone
(\pi_{i}:L\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
and a colimit cocone
(\sigma_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
such that the canonical map
\tilde{x}\colon=\pi_{i}\sigma_{i}:L\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M
(which is independent of the choice of
i\in\mathbb{Z}
) is an isomorphism. Of course, we can arrange for this isomorphism to be the identity, so we can obtain:

\textstyle{{\sf im}^{\infty}(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{-2}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}

\scriptstyle{....}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{-2}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{-1}}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{0}}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{....}

\textstyle{{\sf im}^{\infty}(x)}

where
\pi_{i}\sigma_{i}=1_{{\sf im}^{\infty}(x)}
making
\pi_{i}
a section of the retraction
\sigma_{i}
. This induces the idempotent
x^{\infty}\colon=\sigma_{i}\pi_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, which is independent of the choice of
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

In an arbitrary category, not all endomorphisms have eventual image duality, see for example
[
21
, Example 2.2]
which explains why most endomorphisms in
\mathsf{SET}
do not have eventual image duality. Furthermore, eventual image duality need not be absolute, that is, eventual images are not necessarily preserved by all functors. However, there is a class of endomorphisms which do have
absolute
eventual image duality, and this class includes the ones induced by being Drazin.

Definition 5.15

For
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, we say that
(s_{i}:E\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A,r_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}E)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}

eventuates

x
with
index

k\in\mathbb{N}
in case:

[ev.1]

For all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
s_{i}r_{i}=1_{E}
;

[ev.2]

For all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
,
r_{i}s_{i}=r_{j}s_{j}
;

[ev.3]

For all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
s_{i}x=s_{i+1}
and
xr_{i+1}=r_{i}
;

[ev.4]

For all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
r_{i}s_{i}x^{k+1}=x^{k+1}=x^{k+1}r_{i}s_{i}
.

We first show that if an endomorphism has a family of maps which eventuates it, then it has an absolute eventual image duality.

Lemma 5.16

If
(s_{i}:E\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A,r_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}E)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
eventuates
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
with index
k
, then
x
has an absolute eventual image duality.

Proof:
Note that by
[ev.3]
,
(r_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}E)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a cone and
(s_{i}:E\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
a cocone over the diagram
\mathsf{EI}(x)
. So we need to show that they are respectively a limit and a colimit. So let’s show that
(s_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a universal cone over
\mathsf{EI}(x)
. So suppose that
(z_{i}:Z\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a cone over
\mathsf{EI}(x)
:

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{z_{i}}
\scriptstyle{z_{i+1}}

\scriptstyle{...}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{...}

Then we first observe that:

z_{i+1}r_{i+1}=z_{i}xr_{i+1}=z_{i}r_{i}

So for any
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have that
z_{i}r_{i}=z_{j}r_{j}
. So define the map
h:Z\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}E
as the composite
h\colon=z_{i}r_{i}
(for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
). Next note that by the cone, we have that
z_{i}=z_{i-n}x^{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular by
[ev.4]
, we get that:

hs_{i}=z_{i}r_{i}s_{i}=z_{i-(k+1)}x^{k+1}r_{i}s_{i}=z_{i-(k+1)}x^{k+1}=z_{i}

So we get that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{z_{i}}
\scriptstyle{z_{i+1}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{i}}
\scriptstyle{r_{i}}

\scriptstyle{...}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{...}

Now by
[ev.1]
, each
s_{i}
is monic. Therefore it follows that
h
is necessarily unique, and therefore we get that
(s_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a limit cone. Similarly, by dual arguments, we also get that
(r_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a colimit cocone. By
[ev.1]
, the canonical map is
\tilde{x}=s_{i}r_{i}=1_{E}
. So we have that
x
has eventual image duality. Finally, this (co)limit is clearly absolute as the reasoning only uses constructs which are preserved by all functors.

\Box

We now show that if an endomorphism is Drazin split, then we can build a family which eventuates it:

Lemma 5.17

If
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin split where
e_{x}
splits via
r:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}E
and
s:E\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, then
(s_{i}:E\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A,r_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}E)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
eventuates
x
with index
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
where:

[
i=0
]:

s_{0}\colon=s
,
r_{0}\colon=r
;

[
i>0
]:

s_{i}\colon=sx^{i}
and
r_{i}\colon=(x^{D})^{i}r
;

[
i<0
]:

4
4

4

Notice in the last case since
i<0
that
-i
is positive so that the definitions of
s_{i}
and
r_{i}
make sense.

s_{i}\colon=s(x^{D})^{-i}
and
r_{i}=x^{-i}r
.

Proof:

[ev.1]

Recall that
sr=1_{E}
. When
i=0
,
s_{0}r_{0}=sr=1_{E}
. If
i>0
then by
[D.3]
and that
e_{x}
is idempotent:

s_{i}r_{i}=sx^{i}(x^{D})^{i}r=s(xx^{D})^{i}r=se_{x}^{i}r=se_{x}r=srsr=1_{E}

while if
i<0
then similarly:

s_{i}r_{i}=s(x^{D})^{-i}x_{i}^{-i}=s(x^{D}x)^{-i}r=se_{x}^{-i}r=se_{x}r=srsr=1_{E}

[ev.2]

When
i=0
,
r_{0}s_{0}=rs=e_{x}
. When
i>0
, by
[D.3]
and that
e_{x}
is idempotent, we compute that:

r_{i}s_{i}=(x^{D})^{i}rsx^{i}=(x^{D})^{i}e_{x}x^{i}=(x^{D})^{i}x^{D}xx^{i}=(x^{D})^{i+1}x^{i+1}=(x^{D}x)^{i+1}=e_{x}^{i+1}=e_{x}

When
i<0
, we compute that:

\displaystyle r_{i}s_{i}=x^{-i}rs(x^{D})^{-i}=x^{-i}e_{x}(x^{D})^{-i}=x^{-i}xx^{D}(x^{D})^{-i}

\displaystyle=x^{-i+1}(x^{D})^{-i+1}=(xx^{D})^{-i+1}=e_{x}^{-i+1}=e_{x}

So for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
r_{i}s_{i}=e_{x}
.

[ev.3]

When
i=0
:

\displaystyle s_{0}x=sx=s_{1}

\displaystyle xr_{1}=xx^{D}r=e_{x}r=rsr=r=r_{0}

When
i>0
:

\displaystyle s_{i}x=sx^{i}x=sx^{i+1}=s_{i+1}

\displaystyle xr_{i+1}=x(x^{D})^{i+1}r=(x^{D})^{i}xx^{D}r=(x^{D})^{i}e_{x}r=(x^{D})^{i}rsr=(x^{D})^{i}r=r_{i}

When
i<0
:

\displaystyle s_{i}x=s(x^{D})^{-i}x\!=\!sxx^{D}(x^{D})^{-(i+1)}=se_{x}(x^{D})^{-(i+1)}=srs(x^{D})^{-(i+1)}=s(x^{D})^{-(i+1)}=s_{i+1}

\displaystyle xr_{i+1}=xx^{-(i+1)}r=x^{-i}r=r_{i}

[ev.4]

As shown above, for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
we have that
r_{i}s_{i}=e_{x}
. Now in the proof of Theorem
5.14
, we showed that
x^{k+1}:(A,e_{x})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{x})
was a map in the idempotent splitting. Recall this means that
e_{x}x^{k+1}=x^{k+1}=x^{k+1}e_{x}
. So we get that
r_{i}s_{i}x^{k+1}=x^{k+1}=x^{k+1}r_{i}s_{i}
.

So we conclude that
(s_{i},r_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
eventuates
x
.

\Box

Then by applying Lemma
5.16
to a Drazin split endomorphism, we get that:

Corollary 5.18

An endomorphism that is Drazin split has eventual image duality.

This raises the question when if
(s_{i},r_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
eventuates
x
, whether
x
is Drazin. In general, this does not appear to be the case. We now add what is missing:

Lemma 5.19

x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin split if and only if there exists a family of maps
(s_{i}:E\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A,r_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}E)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
which eventuates
x
with index
k
and
s_{i}xr_{i}:E\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}E
is an isomorphism for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), suppose that
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin split where
e_{x}
splits via
r:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}E
and
s:E\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
. Then in Lemma
5.17
, we showed that the family
(s_{i}:E\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A,r_{i}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A)_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
eventuates
x
. Now in the proof of Lemma
5.6
, we showed that
\alpha=sxr
was an isomorphism. However recall that
s_{0}=s
and
r_{0}=r
. So
s_{0}xr_{0}
is an isomorphism.

For (
\Leftarrow
), suppose that
(s_{i},r_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
eventuates
x
with index
k
and
s_{i}x^{k}r_{i}
is an isomorphism for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. To show that
x
is Drazin split, we will apply Lemma
5.6
. By
[ev.1]
and
[ev.2]
, we get a split idempotent
e=r_{i+1}s_{i+1}
with splitting
r=r_{i+1}
and
s=s_{i+1}
. Moreover, we also have our isomorphism by setting
\alpha=s_{i}xr_{i}
. Lastly, take the natural number to be
k+1
. Now note that by
[ev.3]
, we get that
x^{n}r_{j+n}=r_{j}
and
s_{j}x^{n}=s_{j+n}
for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. From these and
[ev.4]
, we compute that:

r\alpha=r_{i}s_{i}xr_{i}=r_{i}s_{i}xx^{k}r_{i+k}=r_{i}s_{i}x^{k+1}r_{i+k}=x^{k+1}r_{i+k}=xx^{k}r_{i+k}=xr_{i}=xr

\alpha s=s_{i}xr_{i}s_{i}=s_{i-k}x^{k}xr_{i}s_{i}=s_{i-k}x^{k+1}r_{i}s_{i}=s_{i-k}x^{k+1}=s_{i-k}x^{k}x=s_{i}x=sx

Moreover, by
[ev.4]
we get that
x^{k+1}rs=x^{k+1}
. So the diagram in Lemma
5.6
commutes, and therefore we conclude that
x
is Drazin split.

\Box

6
Drazin Inverses in Additive Categories

In this section we consider Drazin inverses in
additive
categories, following Robinson and Puystjens’ work in
[
27
]
. In this paper, by an additive
5
5

5

Additive categories are sometimes called “ringoids” (a many object ring), or
\mathsf{Ab}
-enriched categories.
category, we mean a category
\mathbb{X}
which is enriched over Abelian groups, that is, every homset
\mathbb{X}(A,B)
is an Abelian group and composition,
\mathbb{X}(A,B)\otimes\mathbb{X}(B,C)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}(A,C)
, and composition units,
\mathbb{Z}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}(A,A)
, are Abelian group homomorphisms.

6.1
Right/Left
\pi
-Regularity in Additive Categories

In Section
3.2
, we reviewed how being Drazin was equivalent to being strongly
\pi
-regular. The definition of being strongly
\pi
-regular can be split into two parts: being left
\pi
-regular and being right
\pi
-regular. Dischinger showed that for a ring, being left
\pi
-regular was equivalent to being right
\pi
-regular
6
6

6

Dischinger only states and proves that for a ring, being right
\pi
-regular implies being left
\pi
-regular. However, by dualizing the proof, it is clear that for a ring, being left
\pi
-regular also implies being right
\pi
-regular.

[
11
, Thm 1]
. Unfortunately the same is not true for an arbitrary category (in fact Dischinger even remarks that his proof does not extend to semigroups). Here we show that Dischinger’s result generalizes to the setting of an additive category. Therefore, for an additive category, being Drazin is equivalent to being left
\pi
-regular or right
\pi
-regular.

We first define the notions of left/right
\pi
-regularity for an arbitrary category.

Definition 6.1

In a category
\mathbb{X}
,
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is said to be:

(i)

right
\pi
-regular
if there is an endomorphism
x^{R}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x^{k+1}x^{R}=x^{k}
. The
k\in\mathbb{N}
is a called a
right
\pi
-index
.

(ii)

left
\pi
-regular
if there is an endomorphism
x^{L}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x^{L}x^{k+1}=x^{k}
. The
k\in\mathbb{N}
is a called a
left
\pi
-index
.

An object
A\in\mathbb{X}
is said to be
right (resp. left)
\pi
-regular
if every endomorphism of type
A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is right (resp. left)
\pi
-regular. Similarly, a category
\mathbb{X}
is said to be
right (resp. left)
\pi
-regular
if every endomorphism in
\mathbb{X}
is right (resp. left)
\pi
-regular.

Of course, by definition, we have that an endomorphism which is strongly
\pi
-regular is both right
\pi
-regular and left
\pi
-regular. Now recall from Section
3.2
that being strongly
\pi
-regular was equivalent to being Drazin. So we may state:

Lemma 6.2

In a category
\mathbb{X}
, if
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin, then
x
is both right
\pi
-regular and left
\pi
-regular. Moreover, if
\mathbb{X}
is a Drazin category (resp. if
A
is a Drazin object), then
\mathbb{X}
(resp.
A
) is both right
\pi
-regular and left
\pi
-regular.

We work towards showing that in an
additive category
, the converse is also true. To do so, we first make the following useful observation:

Lemma 6.3

In a category
\mathbb{X}
,
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is right (resp. left)
\pi
-regular if and only if there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x^{k+1}
is right (resp. left)
\pi
-regular with a right (resp. left)
\pi
-index
1
.

Proof:
We prove the statement for right
\pi
-regularity – the left
\pi
-regular statement is proved in dual fashion. For (
\Rightarrow
), suppose
x
is right
\pi
-regular with right
\pi
-index
k\in\mathbb{N}
, that is, there is an endomorphism
x^{R}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
x^{k+1}x^{R}=x^{k}
. We first prove by induction that for all
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
x^{k+m}(x^{R})^{m}=x^{k}
. The base case
m=0
is trivial. So suppose that for all
0\leq j\leq m
,
x^{k+j}(x^{R})^{j}=x^{k}
. Then for
m+1
we compute that:

x^{k+m+1}(x^{R})^{m+1}=xx^{k+m}(x^{R})^{m}x^{R}=xx^{k}x^{R}=x^{k+1}x^{R}=x^{k}

So
x^{k+m}(x^{R})^{m}=x^{k}
holds for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then setting
(x^{k+1})^{R}=(x^{R})^{k+1}
, we compute:

(x^{k+1})^{2}(x^{k+1})^{R}=xx^{k+k+1}(x^{R})^{k+1}=xx^{k}=x^{k+1}

Therefore,
x^{k+1}
is right
\pi
-regular with a right
\pi
-index of
1
.

For (
\Leftarrow
), Suppose that for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
x^{k+1}
is right
\pi
-regular with a right
\pi
-index of
1
. So there is an endomorphism
y:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
(x^{k+1})^{2}y=x^{k+1}
. Now define
x^{R}=yx^{k}
. So we compute:

x^{2k+1+1}x^{R}=(x^{k+1})^{2}yx^{k}=x^{k+1}x^{k}=x^{2k+1}

So
x
is right
\pi
-regular.

\Box

We are now in a position to prove our desired result for additive categories. The proof follows the same steps as in
[
11
]
, however, we fill in some gaps and provide some of the details which were omitted.

Theorem 6.4

In an additive category
\mathbb{X}
, an object
A
is right
\pi
-regular if and only if
A
is left
\pi
-regular. Therefore, an additive category
\mathbb{X}
is right
\pi
-regular if and only if
\mathbb{X}
is left
\pi
-regular.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), suppose that
A
is right
\pi
-regular. Then every endomorphism
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is right
\pi
-regular. As such by Lemma
6.3
, there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x^{k+1}
is right
\pi
-regular with a right
\pi
-index of
1
. So let
y:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
be an endomorphism such that
(x^{k+1})^{2}y=x^{k+1}
. Now
y
is also right
\pi
-regular, so there is a
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
y^{k+1}
is right
\pi
-regular with a right
\pi
-index of
1
. Setting
b=y^{j+1}
, that means we have an endomorphism
c:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
b^{2}c=b
. Now setting
a=(x^{k+1})^{j+1}
, we compute that:

\displaystyle a^{2}b=((x^{k+1})^{j+1})^{2}y^{j+1}\!=\!(x^{k+1})^{2j+2}y^{j+1}=(x^{k+1})^{j}(x^{k+1})^{1+j+1}y^{j+1}=(x^{k+1})^{j}x^{k+1}=(x^{k+1})^{j+1}=a

So
a
is right
\pi
-regular with a right
\pi
-index of
1
, and in particular
a^{2}b=a
. Now from
a^{2}b=a
and
b^{2}c=c
, we compute the following equalities:

\displaystyle abc

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a^{2}b^{2}c=a^{2}b=a

\displaystyle ac

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a^{2}bc=a(abc)=a^{2}

\displaystyle(c-a)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c^{2}-ac-ca+a^{2}=c^{2}-ca=c(c-a)

\displaystyle ab(c-a)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle abc(c-a)=a(c-a)=ac-a^{2}=0

\displaystyle b(c-a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b^{2}c(c-a)=b^{2}(c-a)^{2}

The last equation can be iterated to give
b(c-a)=b^{n+1}(c-a)^{n+1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Now, as
(c-a)
is also right
\pi
-regular, we have a
d
and a
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(c-a)^{p+1}d=(c-a)^{p}
. From this, we get that:

\displaystyle ab(c-a)=ab^{p+1}(c-a)^{p+1}=ab^{p+1}(c-a)^{p+2}d^{2}=

\displaystyle ab^{p+1}(c-a)^{p+1}(c-a)d^{2}=ab(c-a)(c-a)d^{2}=ab(c-a)^{2}d^{2}=0

So from
ab(c-a)=0
we get that
a=abc=aba
. This gives us the following:

\displaystyle ab-ab^{2}a=ab^{2}c-ab^{2}a=ab^{2}(c-a)=abb(c-a)=

\displaystyle abb^{p+1}(c-a)^{p+1}=ab^{p+2}(c-a)^{p+2}d^{2}=ab(c-a)d^{2}=0

As such we get that
ab=ab^{2}a
. Finally, we have that:

ab^{2}a^{2}=ab^{2}aa=aba=a

Therefore
a=(x^{k+1})^{j+1}
is left
\pi
-regular with index 1. Then by Lemma
6.3
,
x
is left
\pi
-regular. For (
\Leftarrow
), the proof of this direction is dual to that of the previous direction.

Now a category is a right (resp. left)
\pi
-regular if and only if every object is right (resp. left)
\pi
-regular. Then since we have shown that for an additive category, an object is right
\pi
-regular if and only if it is left
\pi
-regular, it follows that an additive category is right
\pi
-regular if and only if it is left
\pi
-regular, as desired.

\Box

The above theorem immediately gives us an equivalent characterization of being Drazin for an additive category:

Corollary 6.5

In an additive category
\mathbb{X}
, an object
A
is Drazin if and only if
A
is right (or left)
\pi
-regular. So
\mathbb{X}
is Drazin if and only if
\mathbb{X}
is right (or left)
\pi
-regular.

6.2
Negatives and Scalar Multiplication

In an additive category, we can ask how the Drazin inverse interacts with the sum and the negation. As for usual inverses, the Drazin inverse does not necessarily behave well with sums. Indeed, the sum of Drazin endomorphisms
x
and
y
is not necessarily Drazin, and even if
x+y
was Drazin, then the Drazin inverse
(x+y)^{D}
is not necessarily the sum of the Drazin inverses
x^{D}+y^{D}
. On the other hand, the negation of a Drazin endomorphism is Drazin with Drazin inverse the negation of the original Drazin inverse.

Lemma 6.6

In an additive catgory
\mathbb{X}
, if
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin, then
-x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin where
(-x)^{D}=-x^{D}
and
\mathsf{ind}(-x)=\mathsf{ind}(x)
.

Proof:
We show that
(-x)^{D}=-x^{D}
satisfies the three axioms of a Drazin inverse.

[D.1]

Suppose that
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
, then applying
[D.1]
for
x
we compute that:

\displaystyle(-x)^{k+1}(-x)^{D}=(-x)^{k+1}(-x^{D})=(-1)^{k+2}x^{k+1}x^{D}=(-1)^{k}x^{k}=(-x)^{k}

So
{\sf ind}(-x)\leq k
.

[D.2]

Applying
[D.2]
for
x
we compute that:

(-x)^{D}(-x)(-x)^{D}=(-x^{D})(-x)(-x^{D})=(-1)^{3}x^{D}xx^{D}=-x^{D}=(-x)^{D}

[D.3]

Applying
[D.3]
for
x
we compute that:

(-x)^{D}(-x)=(-x^{D})(-x)=(-1)^{2}x^{D}x=(-1)^{2}xx^{D}=(-x)(-x)^{D}

So
-x
is Drazin with Drazin inverse
(-x)^{D}=-x^{D}
. Now if
\mathsf{ind}(-x)=j<k=\mathsf{ind}(x)
, then the above calculation would tells us that
[D.1]
for
-(-x)=x
holds for
j
, which contradicts that
k
is the Drazin index. So
\mathsf{ind}(-x)=\mathsf{ind}(x)
.

\Box

Similarly, if we happen to be in a setting where we can scalar multiply maps by rationals
\frac{p}{q}\in\mathbb{Q}
, then if
x
is Drazin so is
\frac{p}{q}x
where
(\frac{p}{q}x)^{D}=\frac{q}{p}x^{D}
when
\frac{p}{q}\neq 0
. In particular, in such a setting, for
m\geq 1
, we would have that if
x
is Drazin, then
mx=x+x+\ldots+x
is also Drazin with
(mx)^{D}=\frac{1}{m}x^{D}
. More generally, if
R
is a commutative ring, then in a category
\mathbb{X}
which is enriched over
R
-modules, for any unit
u\in R
, if
x
is Drazin then
ux
is Drazin where
(ux)^{D}=u^{-1}x^{D}
. The case of scalar multiplying by
0
is discussed in the next section.

6.3
Nilpotents

Recall that in an additive category, an endomorphism
n:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is said to be
nilpotent
if there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
n^{k}=0
, and the smallest such
k
is called the
nilpotent index
of
n
. It turns out that nilpotent endomorphisms are precisely the Drazin endomorphisms whose Drazin inverse is zero. In this case, the Drazin index and the nilpotent index coincide.

Lemma 6.7

In any additive category
\mathbb{X}
,
n:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is nilpotent if and only if
n
is Drazin with
n^{D}=0
. In particular, the zero morphism
0:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin and its own Drazin inverse,
0^{D}=0
.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), suppose that
n
is nilpotent with nilpotent index
k
, so
n^{k}=0
. We must show that
n^{D}=0
satisfies the three axioms of a Drazin inverse. However, note that
[D.2]
and
[D.3]
trivially hold since
0n0=0
and
n0=0=0n
. On the other hand,
[D.1]
also holds since
n^{k+1}0=0=n^{k}
. So
n
is Drazin with Drazin inverse
0
. Now we know that
\mathsf{ind}(n)\leq k
. But if
\mathsf{ind}(n)<k
, then there would be a
j<k
such that
n^{j}=n^{j+1}0=0
, which contradicts
k
being the nilpotent index. So we have that
\mathsf{ind}(n)=k
as desired.

For (
\Leftarrow
), suppose that
n
is Drazin with Drazin inverse
n^{D}=0
and
\mathsf{ind}(n)=k
. Then
[D.1]
tells us that
n^{k}=n^{k+1}0=0
. So
n
is nilpotent. If there was a
j<k
such that
n^{j}=0
, then we would also have that
n^{j}=n^{j+1}0=0
, which contradicts
k
being the Drazin index. So we have that the nilpotent index of
n
is its Drazin index.

\Box

6.4
Core-Nilpotent Decomposition

For matrices, an important concept in relation to the Drazin inverse is the notion of the
core-nilpotent decomposition

[
23
, Thm 2.2.21]
. The
core

[
6
, Def 7.3.1]
of a matrix is defined as the Drazin inverse of its Drazin inverse, while its
nilpotent part

[
6
, Def 7.3.2]
is the matrix minus its core. The
core-nilpotent decomposition
is the statement that a matrix is equal to the sum of its core and nilpotent parts. Drazin later generalized this concept for elements in a ring
[
13
, Sec 1]
, and showed that having a core-nilpotent decomposition was equivalent to being Drazin
[
13
, Prop 3.1 & 3.2]
. Here, we discuss core-nilpotent decompositions of endomorphisms in an additive category, in other words, core-nilpotent decomposition in the ring
\mathbb{X}(A,A)
.

Definition 6.8

In an additive category
\mathbb{X}
, a
core-nilpotent decomposition
of
{x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A}
is a pair
(c,n)
of endomorphisms
c:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
n:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that:

[CND.1]

c
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(c)\leq 1
(so
c
has a group inverse);

[CND.2]

n
is nilpotent with nilpotent index
k\in\mathbb{N}
(so that
n^{k}=0
);

[CND.3]

cn=0=nc
;

[CND.4]

x=c+n
.

Before we revisit the proof that having a core-nilpotent decomposition is equivalent to being Drazin, it will be useful to explicitly define the core and nilpotent parts of a Drazin endomorphism.

Definition 6.9

In any additive category
\mathbb{X}
, for a Drazin endomorphism
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
,

(i)

The
core
7
7

7

Note that by Lemma
3.6
.(
i
), the core can in fact be defined in an arbitrary category.
of
x
is the endomorphism
c_{x}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
defined by
c_{x}\colon=x^{DD}=xx^{D}x
.

(ii)

The
nilpotent part
of
x
is the endomorphism
n_{x}\colon=x-c_{x}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
.

Theorem 6.10

[
13
, Prop 3.1, 3.2, & 3.3]
In an additive category
\mathbb{X}
,
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin if and only if
x
has a core-nilpotent decomposition. Moreover, when
x
is Drazin,
(c_{x},n_{x})
is its unique core-nilpotent decomposition.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), Suppose that
x
is Drazin with index
k
. We show that
(c_{x},n_{x})
satisfies the four axioms of a core-nilpotent decomposition.

[CND.1]

This is the statement of Lemma
3.6
.(
i
).

[CND.2]

Using the binomial theorem (which recall holds in any additive category),
[D.3]
, and Lemma
2.2
.(
ii
), we compute that:

\displaystyle n_{x}^{k}=(x-c_{x})^{k}=\sum\limits^{k}_{i=0}(-1)^{i}\binom{k}{i}x^{k-i}c_{x}^{i}=\sum\limits^{k}_{i=0}(-1)^{i}\binom{k}{i}x^{k-i}(xx^{D}x)^{i}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits^{k}_{i=0}(-1)^{i}\binom{k}{i}x^{k-i}x^{2i}{x^{D}}^{i}=\sum\limits^{k+1}_{i=0}(-1)^{i}\binom{k}{i}x^{k-i+2i}{x^{D}}^{i}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits^{k}_{i=0}(-1)^{i}\binom{k}{i}x^{k+i}{x^{D}}^{i}=\sum\limits^{k}_{i=0}(-1)^{i}\binom{k}{i}x^{k}=(1-1)^{k}x^{k}=0

So
n_{x}
has nilpotent index less than or equal to
k
.

[CND.3]

Using
[D.2]
and
[D.3]
, we have:

\displaystyle c_{x}n_{x}=c_{x}(x-c_{x})=c_{x}x-c_{x}c_{x}=xx^{D}xx-xx^{D}xxx^{D}x

\displaystyle=x^{3}x^{D}-x^{3}x^{D}xx^{D}=x^{3}x^{D}-x^{3}x^{D}=0

So
c_{x}n_{x}=0
, and similarly
n_{x}c_{x}=0
.

[CND.4]

From the definition of
n_{x}
it follows that
x=c_{x}+(x-c_{x})=c_{x}+n_{x}
.

For (
\Leftarrow
), Suppose that
x
has a core-nilpotent decomposition
(c,n)
. Let
k\geq 1
be the nilpotent index of
n
so that
n^{k}=0
. Using
[CND.1]
, we set
x^{D}\colon=c^{D}
and show that
x^{D}
satisfies the three Drazin inverse axioms.

[D.1]

First note that, for
m>0
,
x^{m}=(c+n)^{m}=c^{m}+n^{m}
as the cross-terms vanish using
[CND.3]
. So, in particular, for
j\geq k
, we have that
x^{j}=c^{j}
using
[CND.2]
. Now since
\mathsf{ind}(c)\leq 1\leq k
, by Lemma
2.2
.(
i
) we compute that:

\displaystyle x^{k+1}x^{D}=(c+n)^{k+1}c^{D}=(c^{k+1}+n^{k+1})c^{D}=c^{k+1}c^{D}

\displaystyle=c^{k}=c^{k}+n^{k}=(c+n)^{k}=x^{k}

So
{\sf ind}(x)\leq k
.

[D.2]

By
[CND.3]
, we have that
cn=0=0c
. So by Lemma
3.10
, we have that
c^{D}n=0c^{D}
, so
c^{D}n=0
. Using this and
[D.2]
, we compute that:

x^{D}xx^{D}=c^{D}(c+n)c^{D}=c^{D}cc^{D}+c^{D}nc^{D}=c^{D}+0=c^{D}=x^{D}

[D.3]

By
[CND.3]
, we have that
cn=nc
. So by Lemma
3.10
, we have that
c^{D}n=nc^{D}
. Using this and
[D.3]
, we have:

xx^{D}=(c+n)c^{D}=cc^{D}+nc^{D}=c^{D}c+c^{D}n=c^{D}(c+n)=x^{D}x

So
x
is Drazin with Drazin inverse
x^{D}\colon=c^{D}
and
{\sf ind}(x)\leq k
.

It remains to show that a core-nilpotent decomposition is unique. So let
x
be Drazin. Suppose that
x
has another core-nilpotent decomposition
(c,n)
. Then we have that
c_{x}^{D}=x^{D}=c^{D}
. Since
\mathsf{ind}(c)\leq 1
and
\mathsf{ind}(c_{x})\leq 1
, by Lemma
3.6
.(
iii
) we have that
c=(c^{D})^{D}=(c^{D}_{x})^{D}=c_{x}
, so
c=c_{x}
. By
[CND.4]
,
c+n=x=c_{x}+n_{x}
, so subtracting
c=c_{x}
from both sides gives us
n=n_{x}
. So we conclude that
(c_{x},n_{x})
is the unique core-nilpotent decomposition of
x
.

\Box

Therefore, we obtain another characterization of when an additive category is Drazin:

Corollary 6.11

An additive category
\mathbb{X}
is Drazin if and only if every endomorphism has a core-nilpotent decomposition.

6.5
Kernel-cokernel coincidence

In
[
27
, Thm 2]
, Robinson and Puystjens provide conditions for being Drazin in an additive category in terms of kernels and cokernels. Here we revisit their result and show how their setup is in fact equivalent to being Drazin such that the
complement
of the induced idempotent is split.

In an additive category, the
complement
of an idempotent
e:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is the endomorphism
e^{c}\colon=1_{A}-e:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
. Note that since
e
is an idempotent, we have that
ee^{c}=0=e^{c}e
. Moreover, the complement of an idempotent is again an idempotent, so we may consider when it splits. In an additive category, we say that an idempotent
e
is
complement-split
if
e^{c}
is split. In particular, we may consider a Drazin endomorphism whose induced idempotent (from Lemma
5.1
) is complement-split.

Definition 6.12

In an additive category
\mathbb{X}
,
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is
Drazin complement-split
if
x
is Drazin and its induced idempotent
e_{x}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is complement-split.

We now show an endomorphism
x
is Drazin complement-split precisely when some power of
x
has a kernel and a cokernel which are isomorphic. In particular, this means that we can describe the Drazin inverse of
x
in terms of the splitting of the complement of the induced idempotent.

Theorem 6.13

In an additive category
\mathbb{X}
,
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin complement-split if and only if there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x^{k+1}
has a kernel and cokernel:

\textstyle{\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})~\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x^{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{\mathsf{coker}(x^{k+1})}

such that
\kappa\lambda
is an isomorphism and
x^{k+1}:(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
, where
e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa}
is the complement of the idempotent
e_{\lambda,\kappa}=\lambda(\kappa\lambda)^{-1}\kappa
.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), suppose that
x
is Drazin complement-split where
e^{c}_{x}
splits via
r^{c}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}K
and
s^{c}:K\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
. Let
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
. We first show that
s^{c}:K\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is the kernel of
x^{k+1}
and that
r^{c}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}K
is the cokernel of
x^{k+2}
. Starting with the kernel, we first compute using Lemma
2.2
.(
i
) that:

x^{k+1}e^{c}_{x}=x^{k+1}(1_{A}-e_{x})=x^{k+2}-x^{k+1}e_{x}=x^{k+1}-x^{k+1}xx^{D}=x^{k+1}-x^{k+1+1}x^{D}=x^{k+1}-x^{k+1}=0

So
x^{k+1}e^{c}_{x}=0
. Since
e^{c}_{x}r^{c}=s^{c}
, post-composing both since by
r^{c}
gives us that
x^{k+1}s^{c}=0
. Now suppose that we had a map
f:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that
fx^{k+1}=0
as well. Now observe that using idempotency and
[D.3]
, we have that:

fe_{x}=f(e_{x})^{k+1}=f(xx^{D})^{k+1}=fx^{k+2}(x^{D})^{k+1}=0(x^{D})^{k+1}=0

Since
fe_{x}=0
, it follows that
fe^{c}_{x}=f
. Then define
f^{\sharp}:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}K
as
f^{\sharp}\colon=fr^{c}
. Since
r^{c}s^{c}=e^{c}_{x}
, it follows that
f^{\sharp}s^{c}=f
. Since
s^{c}
is monic (since
s^{c}r^{c}=1_{K}
), it follows that
f^{\sharp}
must be unique. So we conclude that
s^{c}:K\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is the kernel of
x^{k+1}
as desired. By similar arguments, one can also show that
r^{c}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}K
is the cokernel of
x^{k+1}
. Now setting
\kappa=s^{c}
and
\lambda=r^{c}
, trivially
\kappa\lambda=1_{K}
is an isomorphism. Moreover:

e_{\lambda,\kappa}=\lambda(\kappa\lambda)^{-1}\kappa=\lambda\kappa=r^{c}s^{c}=e^{c}_{x}

So
e_{\lambda,\kappa}=e^{c}_{x}
and
e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa}=e_{x}
. Now as was shown in the proof of Theorem
5.14
, we have that
x^{k+1}:(A,e_{x})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{x})
is an isomorphism in the idempotent splitting, or in other words,
x^{k+1}:(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
as desired.

For (
\Leftarrow
), By Theorem
5.14
, since
x^{k+1}:(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
, we get that
x
is Drazin. So it remains to explain why its induced idempotent is complement-split. Recall that in the proof of Theorem
5.14
, we had showed that the Drazin inverse of
x
is
x^{D}\colon=vx^{k}=x^{k}v
, where
v:(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})
is the inverse of
x^{k+1}:(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})
in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. So its induced idempotent is
e_{x}=x^{k+1}v
. However
x^{k+1}v=1_{(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})}=e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa}
. So
e_{x}=e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa}
and thus
e_{x}^{c}=e_{\lambda,\kappa}
. However, clearly
e^{c}_{x}=e_{\lambda,\kappa}
splits via
\lambda:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{coker}(x^{k+1})
and
(\kappa\lambda)^{-1}\kappa:\mathsf{coker}(x^{k+1})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
. So we conclude that
x
is Drazin complement-split.

\Box

In
[
27
, Thm 2]
, rather than asking that
x^{k+1}
is an isomorphism in the idempotent splitting, Robinson and Puystjens ask that
x^{k+1}+e_{\lambda,\kappa}
be an actual isomorphism. However, the following lemma shows that these statements are equivalent.

Lemma 6.14

In an additive category
\mathbb{X}
,
f:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
if and only if
f+e^{c}
is an isomorphism in
\mathbb{X}
.

Proof:
Assume that
f:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
is a map in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
, which recall means that
ef=f=fe
. So for (
\Rightarrow
), suppose that
f:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
with inverse
g:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
. Recall that this means that:
eg=g=ge
and also that
fg=e=gf
. Now note that this implies that
fe^{c}=0=fe^{c}
and
ge^{c}=0=e^{c}g
. Then define
(f+e^{c})^{-1}\colon=g+e^{c}
. So we compute that:

(f+e^{c})(f+e^{c})^{-1}=(f+e^{c})(g+e^{c})=fg+fe^{c}+e^{c}g+e^{c}e^{c}=e+0+0+e^{c}=e+e^{c}=1_{A}

(f+e^{c})^{-1}(f+e^{c})=(g+e^{c})(f+e^{c})=gf+ge^{c}+e^{c}f+e^{c}e^{c}=e+0+0+e^{c}=e+e^{c}=1_{A}

So
f+e^{c}
is an isomorphism.

For (
\Leftarrow
), suppose that
f+e^{c}
is an isomorphism. Our goal is to find the inverse of
f:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. So consider the map
e(f+e^{c})^{-1})e
. We clearly have that
e(f+e^{c})^{-1}e:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
is a map in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
, so it remains to show that it is the inverse of
f:(A,e)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e)
. So we compute:

\displaystyle fe(f+e^{c})^{-1})e=ef(f+e^{c})^{-1})e=(ef+0)(f+e^{c})^{-1})e

\displaystyle=(ef+ee^{c})(f+e^{c})^{-1})e=e(f+e^{c})(f+e^{c})^{-1})e=ee=e

So
fe(f+e^{c})^{-1})e=e
, and similarly we can show that
e(f+e^{c})^{-1})ef=e
as well.

\Box

The reader may notice that in the statement of the lemma, we started with a map in the idempotent splitting, while in
[
27
, Thm 2]
this is not stated. However, this is not an issue. Indeed, if
x^{k+1}
has a kernel and cokernel as above, such that
\kappa\lambda
is an isomorphism, then we can compute that:

x^{k+1}e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa}=x^{k+1}(1_{A}-\lambda(\kappa\lambda)^{-1}\kappa)=x^{k+1}-x^{k+1}\lambda(\kappa\lambda)^{-1}\kappa=x^{k+1}-0=x^{k+1}

So
x^{k+1}e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa}=x^{k+1}
and similarly
x^{k+1}e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa}=x^{k+1}
. So
x^{k+1}:(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})
is a map in the idempotent splitting. Then under the other conditions of the above theorem or
[
27
, Thm 2]
, by applying the above lemma, the statement that
x^{k+1}:(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa})
is an isomorphism is equivalent to saying that
x^{k+1}+e_{\lambda,\kappa}
is an isomorphism. Finally, we also recapture the formula for the Drazin inverse in
[
27
, Thm 2]
in terms of the complement of the induced idempotent (recalling that we showed that
e_{\lambda,\kappa}=e^{c}_{x}
and
e^{c}_{\lambda,\kappa}=e_{x}
):

Corollary 6.15

In an additive category
\mathbb{X}
, if
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin complement-split and
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
, then
x^{D}=x^{k}(x^{k+1}+e^{c}_{x})^{-1}=(x^{k+1}+e^{c}_{x})^{-1}x^{k}
.

Proof:
As shown in the proof of Theorem
5.14
,
x^{k+1}:(A,e_{x})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{x})
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. In the same proof, we also showed that
x^{D}=vx^{k}=x^{k}v
, where
v:(A,e_{x})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{x})
is the inverse of
x^{k+1}:(A,e_{x})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{x})
in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. However, by Lemma
6.14
, we have that
x^{k+1}+e^{c}_{x}
is an isomorphism and that
v=e_{x}(x^{k+1}+e^{c}_{x})e_{x}
. Therefore, we get that
x^{D}=x^{k}(x^{k+1}+e^{c}_{x})^{-1}=(x^{k+1}+e^{c}_{x})^{-1}x^{k}
as desired.

\Box

6.6
Fitting Decomposition

In Section
2.3
, we reviewed how to compute the Drazin inverse of a matrix, we first decomposed that matrix as a block diagonal matrix, involving an invertible matrix and a nilpotent matrix, conjugated by another invertible matrix. This approach generalizes to finite-dimensional vector spaces thanks to
Fitting’s Decomposition Theorem

[
20
, Lemma 2.4]
. Here, we generalize this decomposition to additive categories with
finite biproducts
, and call it a
Fitting decomposition
. Moreover, we show that having a Fitting decomposition is equivalent to being both Drazin split and Drazin complement-split, which we call being
Drazin decomposable
.

Definition 6.16

In an additive category
\mathbb{X}
,
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is
Drazin decomposable
if
x
is both Drazin split and Drazin complement-split.

We now work in an additive category with finite biproducts: we denote the biproduct by
\oplus
. To help understand Fitting decompositions, it will be useful to recall the matrix representation for maps between biproducts. A map of type
F:A_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus B_{m}
is uniquely determined by a family of maps
f_{i,j}:A_{i}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B_{j}
. As such,
F
can be represented as an
n\times m
matrix:

F\colon=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1,1}&f_{1,2}&\ldots&f_{1,m}\\
f_{2,1}&f_{2,2}&\ldots&f_{2,m}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
f_{n,1}&f_{n,2}&\ldots&f_{n,m}\end{bmatrix}

Moreover, composition corresponds to matrix multiplication, and identities correspond to the identity matrix. See
[
17
, Sec 2.2.4]
for details of the matrix representation of biproducts.

We now define a Fitting decomposition of an endomorphism by turning
[
6
, Thm 7.2.1]
into a definition:

Definition 6.17

In an additive category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts, a
Fitting decomposition
of
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is a triple
(p,\alpha,\eta)
consisting of an isomorphism
p:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}I\oplus K
, an isomorphism
\alpha:I\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}I
, and a nilpotent endomorphism
\eta:K\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}K
such that the following equality holds:

x=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}

Theorem 6.18

In an additive category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts,
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin decomposable if and only if
x
has a Fitting decomposition.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), suppose that
x
is Drazin decomposable, where
e_{x}
splits via
r:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}I
and
s:I\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, and
e^{c}_{x}
splits via
r^{c}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}K
and
s^{c}:K\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
. We first show that
p=\begin{bmatrix}r&r^{c}\end{bmatrix}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}I\oplus K
is an isomorphism. So define
p^{-1}:I\oplus K\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
as follows:

\displaystyle p^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}s\\
s^{c}\end{bmatrix}

Now using that
e_{x}=rs
and
e_{x}^{c}=r^{c}s^{c}
, we first compute that:

pp^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}r&r^{c}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}s\\
s^{c}\end{bmatrix}=rs+r^{c}s^{c}=e_{x}+e_{x}^{c}=e_{x}+1_{A}-e_{x}=1_{A}

On the other hand, since
ee^{c}=0=e^{c}e
, it follows that
sr^{c}=0
and
s^{c}r=0
. So by using that
sr=1_{I}
and
s^{c}r^{c}=1_{K}
, we compute that:

p^{-1}p=\begin{bmatrix}s\\
s^{c}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}r&r^{c}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}sr&sr^{c}\\
s^{c}r&s^{c}r^{c}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1_{I}&0\\
0&1_{K}\end{bmatrix}=1_{I\oplus K}

So
p
is indeed an isomorphism. Now define
\alpha:I\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}I
and
\eta:K\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}K
as
\alpha=sxr
and
\eta=s^{c}n_{x}r^{c}
respectively, where recall that
n_{x}
is the nilpotent of the core-nilpotent decomposition of
x
. In the proof of Lemma
5.6
we have already shown that
\alpha
is an isomorphism as well. On the other hand if
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
, then in the proof of Theorem
6.10
we showed that
n_{x}
was indeed nilpotent and that
n^{k+1}_{x}=0
. Moreover, recall that in the proof of Theorem
6.10
we also showed that
x^{D}=c^{D}_{x}
and
n_{x}c^{D}_{x}=0
. So it follows that
e_{x}n_{x}=0
and similarly we also have
n_{x}e_{x}=0
. Therefore, we get that
e^{c}_{x}n_{x}=n_{x}=n_{x}e^{c}_{x}
. Then using this and
e_{x}^{c}=r^{c}s^{c}
, we compute that:

\eta^{k+1}=s^{c}n_{x}r^{c}s^{c}n_{x}r^{c}\ldots s^{c}n_{x}r^{c}=s^{c}n_{x}e^{c}_{x}n_{x}\ldots e^{c}n_{x}r^{c}=s^{c}n^{k+1}_{x}r^{c}=s^{c}0r^{c}=0

So
\eta
is nilpotent as desired. Now recall that by Lemma
5.6
, it follows that
r\alpha s=xe_{x}=xx^{D}x
. In other words,
r\alpha s=c_{x}
. On the other hand, from
e^{c}_{x}n_{x}=n_{x}=n_{x}e^{c}_{x}
, it also follows that
r^{c}\eta s^{c}=n_{x}
. Therefore, by the core-nilpotent decomposition, we finally compute that

p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}r&r^{c}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}s\\
s^{c}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}r\alpha&r^{c}\eta\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}s\\
s^{c}\end{bmatrix}=r\alpha s+r^{c}\eta s^{c}=c_{x}+n_{x}=x

and conclude that that
(p,\alpha,\eta)
is a Fitting’s decomposition of
x
as desired.

For
(\Leftarrow)
, we first show that
x
is Drazin by showing that
x^{D}
satisfies the three Drazin inverse axioms.

[D.1]

First note that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that:

x^{m}=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{m}&0\\
0&\eta^{m}\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}

Now let
k
be the smallest natural number such that
\eta^{k+1}=0
. Then we compute:
