\displaystyle x^{k+1+1}x^{D}=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{k+1+1}&0\\
0&\eta^{k+1+1}\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{k+1}\alpha&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}

\displaystyle=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{k+1}\alpha\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{k+1}&0\\
0&\eta^{k+1}\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=x^{k+1}

[D.2]

We easily compute that:

\displaystyle x^{D}xx^{D}=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}

\displaystyle=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}\alpha\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\eta 0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=x^{D}

[D.3]

We easily compute that:

\displaystyle xx^{D}=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta 0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}

\displaystyle=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\eta\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=x^{D}x

So
x
is Drazin. Next, we compute its induced idempotent:

e_{x}=xx^{D}=p\begin{bmatrix}1_{I}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}r&r^{c}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1_{I}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}s\\
s^{c}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}r&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}s\\
s^{c}\end{bmatrix}=rs

So
e_{x}=rs
. We compute its complement to be:

e^{c}_{x}=1_{A}-e_{x}=pp^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}r&r^{c}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}s\\
s^{c}\end{bmatrix}-e_{x}=rs+r^{c}s^{c}-e_{x}=e_{x}+r^{c}s^{c}-e_{x}=r^{c}s^{c}

So
e^{c}_{x}=r^{c}s^{c}
. Now note that we also have that:

\begin{bmatrix}1_{I}&0\\
0&1_{K}\end{bmatrix}=1_{I\oplus K}=p^{-1}p=\begin{bmatrix}s\\
s^{c}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}r&r^{c}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}sr&sr^{c}\\
s^{c}r&s^{c}r^{c}\end{bmatrix}

In particular, this gives us that
sr=1_{I}
and
s^{c}r^{c}=1_{K}
. So both
e_{x}
and
e^{c}_{x}
split, and we conclude that
x
is Drazin decomposable as desired.

\Box

Corollary 6.19

In an additive category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts, if
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
has a Fitting decomposition
(p,\alpha,\eta)
, and is therefore Drazin, then the core and nilpotent-part of
x
are determined by:

\displaystyle c_{x}\colon=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}

\displaystyle n_{x}\colon=p\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}

Proof:
We compute:

\displaystyle c_{x}=xx^{D}x=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha^{-1}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}

\displaystyle=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha\alpha^{-1}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta 0\eta\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}=p\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}p^{-1}

So the desired equality holds. By the definition of
n_{x}
, the other equality holds as well.

\Box

Theorem
6.18
also implies that if an additive category with finite biproducts is also idempotent complete, then an endomorphism is Drazin if and only if it has a Fitting decomposition. Therefore:

Corollary 6.20

An additive category
\mathbb{X}
with finite biproducts is Drazin complete if and only if every endomorphism has a Fitting decomposition.

6.7
Image-Kernel Decomposition

In Section
2.4
, we mentioned that for a ring
R
and an
R
-module
M
, an
R
-linear endomorphism
f:M\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M
was Drazin if and only if
f
had a “Fitting decomposition” in the sense that
M=\mathsf{im}(f^{k})\oplus\mathsf{ker}(f^{k})
for some
k\geq 1

[
30
, Lemma 2.1.(4)]
. We generalize this sort of decomposition in an Abelian category and show that it is indeed equivalent to being Drazin. In an Abelian category
\mathbb{X}
, denote the kernel and image of an endomorphism
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, as follows:

\textstyle{\mathsf{ker}(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{\mathsf{im}(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}

where recall that
\kappa
and
\iota
are monic, and
\epsilon
is epic. Now define
\psi
as the canonical map:

\psi=\begin{bmatrix}\iota\\
\kappa\end{bmatrix}:\mathsf{im}(x)\oplus\mathsf{ker}(x)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A

Definition 6.21

In an Abelian category
\mathbb{X}
, a map
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
has an
image-kernel decomposition
in case the map
\psi
as defined above is an isomorphism.

Theorem 6.22

In an Abelian category
\mathbb{X}
,
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin if and only if there is some
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x^{k+1}
has an image-kernel decomposition.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), suppose that
x
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(x)=k
. Our objective is to show that
\psi:\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})\oplus\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is an isomorphism. To construct its inverse, we need to first construct maps
A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})
and
A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})
. First note that by idempotency and
[D.3]
, we get that
e_{x}=e_{x}^{k+1}=(x^{D})^{k+1}x^{k+1}
. So by using the universal property of the image, there is a monic map
\mathsf{im}(e_{x})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})
, which then allows us to build a map
\phi_{1}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})
such that
\phi_{1}\iota=e_{x}
. On the other hand, recall that in the proof of Theorem
5.14
, we showed that
e_{x}x^{k+1}=x^{k+1}
. Then it follows that
e^{c}_{x}x^{k+1}=0
. So by the universal property of the kernel, let
\phi_{2}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})
be the unique map such that
\phi_{2}\kappa^{\circ}_{x^{k+1}}=e^{c}_{x}
. Then define
\phi
as:

\phi=\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{1}&\phi_{2}\end{bmatrix}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})\oplus\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})

So we first compute that:

\phi\psi=\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{1}&\phi_{2}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\iota\\
\kappa\end{bmatrix}=\phi_{1}\iota+\phi_{2}\kappa=e_{x}+e^{c}_{x}=1_{A}

To prove that
\phi\psi
is the identity, we must work out what
\iota\phi_{1}
,
\iota\phi_{2}
,
\kappa\phi_{1}
, and
\kappa\phi_{2}
are. First, recall again in the proof of Theorem
5.14
, we showed that
x^{k+1}e_{x}=x^{k+1}
, which also implies that
x^{k+1}e^{c}_{x}=0
. So we first compute that:

\epsilon\iota\phi_{1}\iota=x^{k+1}e_{x}=x^{k+1}=\epsilon\iota

\epsilon\iota\phi_{2}\kappa=x^{k+1}e^{c}_{x}=0=\epsilon 0\iota

Since
\epsilon
is epic and
\iota
is monic, it follows that
\iota\phi_{1}=1_{\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})}
and
\iota\phi_{2}=0
. Next, using idempotency and
[D.3]
,
e_{x}=x^{k+1}(x^{D})^{k+1}
, then we have that
\kappa e_{x}=0
. So we compute:

\kappa\phi_{1}\iota=\kappa e_{x}=0=0\iota

\kappa\phi_{2}\kappa=\kappa e^{c}_{x}=\kappa(1_{A}-e_{x})=\kappa-\kappa e_{x}=\kappa-0=\kappa

Since
\iota
and
\kappa
are monic, it follows that
\kappa\phi_{1}=0
and
\kappa\phi_{2}=1_{\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})}
. So finally we get that:

\psi\phi=\begin{bmatrix}\iota\\
\kappa\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{1}&\phi_{2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\iota\phi_{1}&\iota\phi_{2}\kappa\phi_{1}&\kappa\phi_{2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1_{\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})}&0\\
0&1_{\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})}\end{bmatrix}=1_{\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})\oplus\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})}

So we conclude that
\psi:\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})\oplus\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is an isomorphism.

For
(\Leftarrow)
, suppose that
\psi:\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})\oplus\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is an isomorphism. To show that
x
is Drazin, we will show that
\psi
is part of a Fitting decomposition of
x
. So let
\phi_{1}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})
and
\phi_{2}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})
be the components of
\psi^{-1}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})\oplus\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})
. Then from
\psi\psi^{-1}=1_{\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})\oplus\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})}
and
\psi^{-1}\psi=1_{A}
, we get the following equalities:

\displaystyle\iota\phi_{1}=1_{\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})}\hskip 18.49988pt\iota\phi_{2}=0\hskip 18.49988pt\kappa\phi_{1}=0\hskip 18.49988pt\kappa\phi_{2}=1_{\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})}\hskip 18.49988pt\phi_{1}\iota+\phi_{2}\kappa=1_{A}

Now for the isomorphism part of our decomposition, define
\alpha:\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})
to be the composite
\alpha\colon=\iota x\phi_{1}
. To show that
\alpha
is an isomorphism, we will use the fact that in an Abelian category, a map is an isomorphism if and only if it is monic and epic. To show that
\alpha
is monic and epic, we will need to first compute some useful identities. To start with,
\epsilon\iota=x^{k+1}
and
\iota\phi_{1}=1_{\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})}
together imply that
x^{k+1}\phi_{1}=\epsilon
. Moreover, from
\epsilon\iota=x^{k+1}
and
\kappa x^{k+1}=0
, since
\iota
is monic, it follows that
\kappa\epsilon=0
. From this and
\phi_{1}\iota+\phi_{2}\kappa=1_{A}
we also get that
\phi_{1}\iota\epsilon=\epsilon
. On the other hand, we also get:

x\epsilon=xx^{k+1}\phi_{1}=x^{k+1}x\phi_{1}=\epsilon\iota x\phi_{1}=\epsilon\alpha

So, from
x\epsilon=\epsilon\alpha
, we have:

\epsilon\alpha\iota=x\epsilon\iota=xx^{k+1}=x^{k+1}x=\epsilon\iota x

Then since
\epsilon
is epic, we get that
\alpha\iota=\iota x
, which also tells us that
\iota x^{n}=\alpha^{n}\iota
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then from
\iota\phi_{1}=1_{\mathsf{im}(x^{k+1})}
, we get that
\alpha^{n}=\iota x^{n}\phi_{1}
.

Now since we are in an Abelian category, to show that
\alpha
is monic, it suffices to show that if
f\alpha=0
then
f=0
. So suppose that
f\alpha=0
. Then we have that:

f\iota x^{k+1}=f\iota xx^{k}=f\alpha\iota x^{k}=0\iota x^{k}=0

Since
f\iota x^{k+1}=0
, by the universal property of the kernel, there exists a unique
h
such that
f\iota=h\kappa
. Post-composing both sides by
\phi_{2}
, since
\kappa\phi_{2}=1_{\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})}
and
\iota\phi_{2}=0
, we get that
h=0
and so
f\iota=0
. However,
\iota
is monic, therefore
f=0
as desired. So
\alpha
is monic. Dually, since we are in an Abelian category, to show that
\alpha
is epic, it suffices to show that if
\alpha g=0
then
g=0
. So suppose that
\alpha g=0
. Then

\epsilon g=\phi_{1}\iota\epsilon g=\phi_{1}\iota x^{k+1}\phi_{1}g=\phi_{1}\iota\alpha^{k+1}g=\phi_{1}\iota\alpha^{k}\alpha g=\phi_{1}\iota\alpha^{k}0=0

Since
\epsilon
is epic, it follows that
g=0
, as desired. So
\alpha
is epic. Therefore, we conclude that
\alpha
is an isomorphism.

For the nilpotent part of the decomposition, note that since
\kappa x^{k+1}=0
, we also have that
\kappa xx^{k+1}=0
. Therefore, by the universal property of the kernel, there exists a unique map
\eta:\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})
such that
\eta\kappa=\kappa x
. However, since
\kappa\phi_{2}=1_{\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})}
, we get that
\eta=\kappa x\phi_{2}
. Moreover, we compute that:

\kappa x=\kappa x\left(\phi_{1}\iota+\phi_{2}\kappa\right)=\kappa x\phi_{1}\iota+\kappa x\phi_{2}\kappa=\eta\kappa\phi_{1}\iota+\kappa x\phi_{2}\kappa=0+\kappa x\phi_{2}\kappa=\kappa x\phi_{2}\kappa

Since
\kappa x=\kappa x\phi_{2}\kappa
, we can prove by induction that
\eta^{m}=\kappa x^{m}\phi_{2}
. The base case
m=0
follows from
\kappa\phi_{2}=1_{\mathsf{ker}(x^{k+1})}
. So suppose that for all
0\leq j\leq n
,
\eta^{n}=\kappa x^{n}\phi_{2}
.

\eta^{n+1}=\eta\eta^{n}=\eta\kappa x^{n}\phi_{2}=\kappa x\phi_{2}\kappa x^{n}\phi_{2}=\kappa xx^{n}\phi_{2}=\kappa x^{n+1}\phi_{2}

So we do have that
\eta^{m}=\kappa x^{m}\phi_{2}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Now for
m=k+1
, we get that:

\eta^{k+1}=\kappa x^{k+1}\phi_{2}=0\phi_{2}=0

So
\eta
is nilpotent as desired.

Lastly, we finally compute that:

\displaystyle\psi^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}\psi=\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{1}&\phi_{2}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\eta\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\iota\\
\kappa\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{1}\alpha&\phi_{2}\eta\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\iota\\
\kappa\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}\alpha\iota+\phi_{2}\eta\kappa=\phi_{1}\iota x+\phi_{2}\kappa x=\left(\phi_{1}\iota+\phi_{2}\kappa\right)x=x

So we conclude that
(\psi^{-1},\alpha,\eta)
is a Fitting’s decomposition of
x
. So by Theorem
6.18
, we get that
x
is Drazin.

\Box

7
Drazin Inverses of Opposing Pairs of Maps

Arriving with categorical eyes to the subject of Drazin inverses it is natural to want to have a Drazin inverse of an arbitrary map. However, to have a Drazin inverse of a map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, one really needs an
opposing
map
g:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
to allow for the iteration which is at the heart of the notion of a Drazin inverse. The Drazin inverse of an opposing pair,
(f,g)
, is itself an opposing pair whose properties we develop in this section. Throughout this section we work in an arbitrary category
\mathbb{X}
.

7.1
The Drazin inverse of an opposing pair

We denote a pair of maps of dual type
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
g:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
by
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
and refer to it as an
opposing pair
.

Definition 7.1

A
Drazin inverse
of
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is an opposing pair of dual type
\textstyle{(f^{\frac{D}{g}},g^{\frac{D}{f}}):B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
satisfying the following properties:

[DV.1]

There is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(fg)^{k}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=(fg)^{k}
and
(gf)^{k}gg^{\frac{D}{f}}=(gf)^{k}
.

[DV.2]

f^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=f^{\frac{D}{g}}
and
g^{\frac{D}{f}}gg^{\frac{D}{f}}=g^{\frac{D}{f}}
;

[DV.3]

ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=g^{\frac{D}{f}}g
and
f^{\frac{D}{g}}f=gg^{\frac{D}{f}}
.

The map
f^{\frac{D}{g}}:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is called the Drazin inverse of
f
over
g
, while the map
g^{\frac{D}{f}}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is called the Drazin inverse of
g
over
f
. The
Drazin index
of
(f,g)
is the smallest
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
[DV.1]
holds, which we denote by
\mathsf{ind}(f,g)=k
.

There is a slight redundancy in the above definition in that, thanks to
[DV.3]
, we do not need to assume both equalities for
[DV.1]
.

Lemma 7.2

In a category
\mathbb{X}
, if for
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
there is a
\textstyle{(f^{\frac{D}{g}},g^{\frac{D}{f}}):B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
which satisfies
[DV.3]
, then the following are equivalent:

(i)

[DV.1]
holds;

(ii)

There is a
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(fg)^{p}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=(fg)^{p}
;

(iii)

There is a
q\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(gf)^{q}gg^{\frac{D}{g}}=(gf)^{q}
.

Proof:
We prove that
(i)\Rightarrow(ii)\Rightarrow(iii)\Rightarrow(i)
. Clearly, we already have that
(i)\Rightarrow(ii)
. For
(ii)\Rightarrow(iii)
, suppose that there is a
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(fg)^{p}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=(fg)^{p}
. Then using
[DV.3]
and that
(gf)^{p+1}=g(fg)^{p}f
, we compute:

(gf)^{p+1}gg^{\frac{D}{g}}=g(fg)^{p}fgg^{\frac{D}{f}}=g(fg)^{p}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f=g(fg)^{p}f=(gf)^{p+1}

So
(iii)
holds. For
(iii)\Rightarrow(i)
, suppose that there is a
q\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(gf)^{q}gg^{\frac{D}{g}}=(gf)^{q}
. Now that we also have that
(gf)^{q+1}gg^{\frac{D}{g}}=(gf)^{q+1}
. On the other hand, using
[DV.3]
and that
(fg)^{q+1}=f(gf)^{q}g
, we compute:

(fg)^{q+1}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=f(gf)^{q}gff^{\frac{D}{g}}=f(gf)^{q}gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g=f(gf)^{q}g=(gf)^{q+1}

So
[DV.1]
holds. So we conclude that
(i)\Leftrightarrow(ii)\Leftrightarrow(iii)
as desired.

\Box

Drazin inverses of opposing pairs share many of the same properties as Drazin inverses of endomorphisms, such as the fact that they are unique (if they exist) and also absolute. Before establishing these properties, we will show how Drazin inverses of opposing pairs are linked to Drazin inverses of endomorphisms.

7.2
Drazin Opposing Pairs

From an opposing pair
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
, we get two endomorphisms
fg:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
gf:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
. Our objective is to show that
(f,g)
has a Drazin inverse if and only if the composites
fg
and
gf
both have Drazin inverses. To do so, we first show that, surprisingly, if one of these composites is Drazin, then so is the other one: this is sometimes known as the Cline’s formula
[
9
]
.

Lemma 7.3

For an opposing pair
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
,
fg:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin if and only if
gf:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is Drazin.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), Suppose that
fg
is Drazin. We show that
(gf)^{D}\colon=g(fg)^{D}(fg)^{D}f
satisfies the three axioms of a Drazin inverse.

[D.1]

Suppose that
\mathsf{ind}(fg)=k
. Then using Lemma
2.2
.(ii) for
fg
, we compute:

\displaystyle(gf)^{k+2}(gf)^{D}=g(fg)^{k+1}f(gf)^{D}=g(fg)^{k+1}fg(fg)^{D}(fg)^{D}f

\displaystyle=g(fg)^{k+2}((fg)^{D})^{2}f=g(fg)^{k}f=(gf)^{k+1}

[D.2]

Using
[D.2]
and
[D.3]
for
fg
, we compute that:

\displaystyle(gf)^{D}gf(gf)^{D}=g(fg)^{D}(fg)^{D}fgfg(fg)^{D}(fg)^{D}f=

\displaystyle g(fg)^{D}fg(fg)^{D}(fg)^{D}fg(fg)^{D}f=g(fg)^{D}(fg)^{D}f=(gf)^{D}

[D.3]

Using
[D.3]
for
fg
, we compute that:

\displaystyle(gf)^{D}gf=g(fg)^{D}(fg)^{D}fgf=g(fg)^{D}fg(fg)^{D}f=gfg(fg)^{D}(fg)^{D}f=gf(gf)^{D}

So we conclude that
gf
is Drazin.

For (
\Leftarrow
), suppose that
gf
is Drazin. By similar calculations as above, we can show that
(fg)^{D}\colon=f(gf)^{D}(gf)^{D}g
satisfies the three axioms of a Drazin inverse. So we conclude that
fg
is Drazin.

\Box

This suggests the following definition:

Definition 7.4

A
Drazin opposing pair
is an opposing pair
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
such that either
fg
or
gf
is Drazin (so, by Lemma
7.3
, both are Drazin).

Below we will show that being a Drazin opposing pair is equivalent to having a Drazin inverse for the pair. In order to prove this, it will be useful to establish the following identities for Drazin opposing pairs:

Lemma 7.5

If
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is a Drazin opposing pair, then we have that
(fg)^{D}f=f(gf)^{D}
and
(gf)^{D}g=g(fg)^{D}
.

Proof:
Suppose that
(f,g)
is a Drazin opposing pair. Then by Lemma
7.3
we have that
(gf)^{D}=g(fg)^{D}(fg)^{D}f
and
(fg)^{D}=f(gf)^{D}(gf)^{D}g
. Then using
[D.2]
and
[D.3]
for
gf
, we compute that:

(fg)^{D}f=f(gf)^{D}(gf)^{D}gf=f(gf)^{D}gf(gf)^{D}=f(gf)^{D}

So
(fg)^{D}f=f(gf)^{D}
. Similarly, we can also show that
(gf)^{D}g=g(fg)^{D}
.

\Box

Theorem 7.6

\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
has a Drazin inverse if and only if
(f,g)
is a Drazin opposing pair.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), suppose that
(f,g)
has a Drazin inverse
(f^{\frac{D}{g}},g^{\frac{D}{f}})
with
\mathsf{ind}(f,g)=k
. We first show that
(fg)^{D}\colon=g^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\frac{D}{g}}
satisfies the three axioms of a Drazin inverse.

[D.1]

Note that
(fg)^{k+1}=f(gf)^{k}g
and also that
f(gf)^{k}=(fg)^{k}f
. Then using
[DV.1]
, we compute that:

\displaystyle(fg)^{k+1}(fg)^{D}\!=\!f(gf)^{k}g(fg)^{D}\!=\!f(gf)^{k}gg^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\frac{D}{g}}=f(gf)^{k}f^{\frac{D}{g}}=(fg)^{k}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}\!=\!(fg)^{k}

[D.2]

Using
[DV.2]
and
[DV.3]
, we compute that:

\displaystyle(fg)^{D}fg(fg)^{D}\!=\!g^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\frac{D}{g}}fgg^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\frac{D}{g}}=g^{\frac{D}{f}}gg^{\frac{D}{f}}gg^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\frac{D}{g}}=g^{\frac{D}{f}}gg^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\frac{D}{g}}=g^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\frac{D}{g}}=(fg)^{D}

[D.3]

Using
[D.3]
for
fg
, we compute that:

\displaystyle(fg)^{D}fg\!=\!g^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\frac{D}{g}}fg=g^{\frac{D}{f}}gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g=g^{\frac{D}{f}}g=ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=ff^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=fgg^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\frac{D}{g}}=fg(fg)^{D}

So we conclude that
fg
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(fg)\leq\mathsf{ind}(f,g)
. Then by Lemma
7.3
, we also get that
gf
is Drazin, and it is easy to check that
\mathsf{ind}(gf)\leq\mathsf{ind}(f,g)
as well. Now using the formula from Lemma
7.3
, we can also check using
[DV.2]
and
[DV.3]
that:

\displaystyle(gf)^{D}=g(fg)^{D}(fg)^{D}f=gg^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\frac{D}{g}}g^{\frac{D}{f}}gg^{\frac{D}{f}}=f^{\frac{D}{g}}g^{\frac{D}{f}}

So we conclude that
(f,g)
is a Drazin opposing pair.

For (
\Leftarrow
), Suppose that
(f,g)
is a Drazin opposing pair. Note by Lemma
7.5
that setting
f^{\frac{D}{g}}\colon=g(fg)^{D}=(gf)^{D}g
and
g^{\frac{D}{f}}\colon=f(gf)^{D}=(fg)^{D}f
is well-defined. So now we must show that
(f^{\frac{D}{g}},g^{\frac{D}{f}})
satisfies the three axioms of a Drazin inverse. We start with the third one:

[DV.3]

Using
[D.3]
for
fg
, we compute that:

\displaystyle ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=fg(fg)^{D}=(fg)^{D}fg=g^{\frac{D}{f}}g

So
ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=g^{\frac{D}{f}}g
, and similarly we can show that
f^{\frac{D}{g}}f=gg^{\frac{D}{f}}
.

[DV.1]

Let
k=\mathsf{max}(\mathsf{ind}(fg),\mathsf{ind}(gf))
. Then by Lemma
2.2
.(
i
) for
fg
, we compute that:

\displaystyle(fg)^{k}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=(fg)^{k}fg(fg)^{D}=(fg)^{k+1}(fg)^{D}=(fg)^{k}

As such, since
[DV.3]
holds, by Lemma
7.2
we then get that
[DV.1]
holds.

[DV.2]

Using
[D.2]
for
gf
, we compute that:

\displaystyle f^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=(gf)^{D}gf(gf)^{D}g=(gf)^{D}g=f^{\frac{D}{g}}

So
f^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=f^{\frac{D}{g}}
, and similarly we can show that
g^{\frac{D}{f}}gg^{\frac{D}{f}}=g
as well.

So we conclude that
(f,g)
has a Drazin inverse and
\mathsf{ind}(f,g)\!\leq\!\mathsf{max}(\mathsf{ind}(fg),\mathsf{ind}(gf))
.
\Box

Therefore, from now on we will simply say that an opposing pair
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is
Drazin
if
(f,g)
is a Drazin opposing pair or equivalently if
(f,g)
has a Drazin inverse.

7.3
Properties of Drazin Opposing Pairs

Now that we have related Drazin inverses of opposing pairs to usual Drazin inverses of endomorphisms, we can use this to access properties of Drazin opposing pairs which are analogues for those of Drazin endomorphisms. We begin with the all-important property of uniqueness:

Proposition 7.7

If
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
has a Drazin inverse, then it is unique.

Proof:
Suppose that
(f,g)
has two Drazin inverses
(h,k)
and
(p,q)
. By Theorem
7.6
,
(f,g)
is also Drazin, so
fg
and
gf
are both Drazin, and we may use
(h,k)
and
(p,q)
to build their Drazin inverses. Since Drazin inverses are unique, we must have that
qp=(fg)^{D}=kh
and
pq=(gf)^{D}=hk
. Now using
[DV.2]
and
[DV.3]
, we compute:

\displaystyle k=kgk=khf=qpf=qgq=q

So
k=q
, and similarly we can also show that
h=p
. So we conclude that Drazin inverses of opposing pairs are unique.

\Box

This allows us to talk of
the
Drazin inverse of
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

and to write it as:
\textstyle{(f,g)^{D}\colon=(f^{\frac{D}{g}},g^{\frac{D}{f}}):B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
. Moreover, this implies that the formulas of the Drazin inverses in the proof of Theorem
7.6
are canonical, and the Drazin index of the opposing pair is equal to the max of the Drazin indexes of the composites.

Corollary 7.8

If
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is Drazin then:

(i)

(fg)^{D}=g^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\frac{D}{g}}
and
(gf)^{D}=f^{\frac{D}{g}}g^{\frac{D}{f}}
;

(ii)

f^{\frac{D}{g}}=g(fg)^{D}=(gf)^{D}g
and
g^{\frac{D}{f}}=f(gf)^{D}=(fg)^{D}f

Moreover,
\mathsf{ind}(f,g)=\mathsf{max}(\mathsf{ind}(fg),\mathsf{ind}(gf))
.

It is also straightforward to see that if an opposing pair is Drazin, then its symmetric pair will also be Drazin:

Lemma 7.9

\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is Drazin if and only if
\textstyle{(g,f):B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is Drazin.

Proof:
By definition,
(f,g)
is Drazin if and only if
fg
is Drazin or
gf
is Drazin if and only if
(g,f)
is Drazin.

\Box

We can also recover the Drazin inverses of an endomorphism from the Drazin inverse of the opposing pair consisting of the endomorphism paired with the identity.

Lemma 7.10

x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin if and only if
\textstyle{(x,1_{A}):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is Drazin, or equivalently if
\textstyle{(1_{A},x):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is Drazin.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), Suppose that
x
is Drazin. Then trivially
x1_{A}=x=1_{A}x
is Drazin. So
(x,1_{A})
(or equivalently
(1_{A},x)
) is Drazin. Applying Corollary
7.8
, we get that
x^{\frac{D}{1_{A}}}=1_{A}(1_{A}x)^{D}=x^{D}
and
1^{\frac{D}{x}}=x(1_{A}x)^{D}=xx^{D}
. For (
\Leftarrow
), suppose that
(x,1_{A})
is Drazin. So
x1_{A}=x=1_{A}x
is Drazin. Applying Corollary
7.8
, we compute that:
x^{D}=(1_{A}x)^{D}=x^{\frac{D}{1_{A}}}1^{\frac{D}{x}}
and
x^{D}=(x1_{A})^{D}=1^{\frac{D}{x}}x^{\frac{D}{1_{A}}}
.

\Box

Thus, we have yet another equivalent characterization of when a category is Drazin.

Corollary 7.11

A category is Drazin if and only if every opposing pair in it is Drazin.

Proof:
If
\mathbb{X}
is Drazin, then for an opposing pair
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
,
fg
and
gf
are both Drazin, so
(f,g)
is Drazin. Conversely, if every opposing pair is Drazin, then by Lemma
7.6
, every endomorphism must be Drazin.

\Box

Drazin inverses of opposing pairs are also absolute:

Proposition 7.12

Let
\mathsf{F}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{Y}
be a functor and let
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
be Drazin in
\mathbb{X}
, then
\textstyle{(\mathsf{F}(f),\mathsf{F}(g)):\mathsf{F}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is Drazin.

Proof:
Suppose that
(f,g)
is Drazin, which in particular means that
fg
and
gf
are Drazin. By Proposition
3.7
, Drazin inverses are absolute, so
\mathsf{F}(fg)=\mathsf{F}(f)\mathsf{F}(g)
and
\mathsf{F}(gf)=\mathsf{F}(g)\mathsf{F}(f)
are also Drazin. As such, we get that
(\mathsf{F}(f),\mathsf{F}(g))
is Drazin. Moreover, applying Corollary
7.8
, we compute that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}(f)^{\frac{D}{\mathsf{F}(g)}}=\mathsf{F}(g)\mathsf{F}(fg)^{D}=\mathsf{F}(g)\mathsf{F}((fg)^{D})=\mathsf{F}(g(fg)^{D})=\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{F}(f^{\frac{D}{g}}))

So
\mathsf{F}(f)^{\frac{D}{\mathsf{F}(g)}}=\mathsf{F}(f^{\frac{D}{g}})
, and similarly we can also compute that
\mathsf{F}(g)^{\frac{D}{\mathsf{F}(f)}}=\mathsf{F}(g^{\frac{D}{f}})
.

\Box

Much as before, the Drazin inverse of an opposing pair commutes with everything with which the opposing pair commutes with:

Lemma 7.13

Suppose that
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
and
\textstyle{(v,w):A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
are Drazin. Then if the diagram on the left commutes, then the diagram on the right commutes:

\begin{array}[c]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 10.50624pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.50624pt\raise-15.26721pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-25.17331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.97946pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.97946pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.84956pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 17.16466pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise-25.17331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 74.84956pt\raise-14.68056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.85934pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{w^{\frac{D}{v}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.84956pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.24684pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f^{\frac{D}{g}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-57.13547pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.99854pt\raise-26.77466pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1875pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{b}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.72374pt\raise-31.96216pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.72374pt\raise-31.96216pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.84956pt\raise-46.64272pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.85934pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v^{\frac{D}{w}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.84956pt\raise-55.96214pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise-63.92432pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.59787pt\raise-68.28055pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.35625pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{a}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.97946pt\raise-63.92432pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.97946pt\raise-63.92432pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A^{\prime}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

Proof:
If
(f,g)
and
(v,w)
are Drazin, we have that
fg
and
vw
are Drazin. So by Lemma
3.10
, the outer square of the diagram on the left implies that
(fg)^{D}a=a(vw)^{D}
. Now by using this and Corollary
7.8
, we compute that:

g^{\frac{D}{f}}b=(fg)^{D}fb=(fg)^{D}av=a(vw)^{D}v=aw^{\frac{D}{v}}

So
g^{\frac{D}{f}}b=aw^{\frac{D}{v}}
, and similarly we can also show that
f^{\frac{D}{g}}a=bv^{\frac{D}{w}}
. So the diagram on the right commutes as desired.

\Box

7.4
Opposing Pairs of Isomorphisms

We now show that an opposing pair has a Drazin index of zero precisely when it is a pair of opposing isomorphisms. The Drazin inverse of an opposing pair of isomorphisms is the opposing pair of their inverses.

Lemma 7.14

\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(f,g)=0
if and only if
f
and
g
are isomorphisms.

Proof:
For
(\Rightarrow)
, Suppose that
(f,g)
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(f,g)=0
. Then
[DV.1]
tells us that
ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=1_{A}
and
gg^{\frac{D}{f}}=1_{B}
. Now
[DV.3]
gives us that
f^{\frac{D}{g}}f=gg^{\frac{D}{f}}=1_{B}
and
g^{\frac{D}{f}}g=ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=1_{A}
. So
f
and
g
are isomorphisms with
f^{-1}=f^{\frac{D}{g}}
and
g^{\frac{D}{f}}=g^{-1}
.

For
(\Leftarrow)
, Suppose that
f
and
g
are isomorphisms. Then the composites
fg
and
gf
are also isomorphisms. By Lemma
3.3
, we get that
fg
and
gf
are Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(fg)=0
and
\mathsf{ind}(gf)=0
. As such,
(f,g)
is Drazin. Applying Corollary
7.8
and Lemma
3.3
, we compute that:

f^{\frac{D}{g}}=g(fg)^{D}=g(fg)^{-1}=gg^{-1}f^{-1}=f^{-1}

So
f^{\frac{D}{g}}=f^{-1}
, and similarly we also get that
g^{\frac{D}{f}}=g^{-1}
. Moreover, we also have that
\mathsf{ind}(f,g)=0
.

\Box

7.5
Drazin Opposing Pairs and Idempotents

In Section
5
we saw how to equivalently characterize having a Drazin inverse in terms of isomorphisms in the idempotent completion. The objective of this section is to do the same for the Drazin inverse of an opposing pair. We first recall from Lemma
5.1
that every Drazin endomorphism
x
induces an idempotent
e_{x}=xx^{D}=x^{D}x
. Then for a Drazin opposing pair
(f,g)
, we get two idempotents
e_{fg}=fg(fg)^{D}
and
e_{gf}=gf(gf)^{D}=(gf)^{D}gf
. Moreover, we can also use the Drazin inverse to get isomorphisms between these two idempotents.

Lemma 7.15

If
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is Drazin, then:

(i)

e_{fg}=ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=g^{\frac{D}{f}}g
and
e_{gf}=f^{\frac{D}{g}}f=gg^{\frac{D}{f}}
are induced idempotents;

(ii)

ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f:(A,e_{fg})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e_{gf})
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
whose inverse is given by
f^{\frac{D}{g}}:(B,e_{gf})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{fg})
;

(iii)

gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g:(B,e_{gf})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{fg})
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
whose inverse is given by
g^{\frac{D}{f}}:(A,e_{gf})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e_{fg})
.

Proof:

(i)

Recall from Corollary
7.8
that
f^{\frac{D}{g}}=g(fg)^{D}=(gf)^{D}g
and
g^{\frac{D}{f}}=f(gf)^{D}=(fg)^{D}f
. Then combining these with the definition of the induced idempotents, we immediately get that
e_{fg}=ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=g^{\frac{D}{f}}g
and
e_{gf}=f^{\frac{D}{g}}f=gg^{\frac{D}{f}}
as desired.

(ii)

We must first show that
ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f
and
f^{\frac{D}{g}}
are maps in the idempotent completion. First by using (
i
) and
[DV.2]
we compute that:

e_{gf}f^{\frac{D}{g}}e_{fg}=f^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=f^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=f^{\frac{D}{g}}

So
e_{gf}f^{\frac{D}{g}}e_{fg}=f^{\frac{D}{g}}
. On the other hand,

e_{fg}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}fe_{gf}=ff^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f=ff^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f=ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f

So
e_{fg}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}fe_{gf}=ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f
as well. As such, we get
ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f:(A,e_{fg})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e_{gf})
and
f^{\frac{D}{g}}:(B,e_{gf})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{fg})
are indeed maps in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. Now we must show that they are inverses of each other in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. However, using (
i
) again, we easily see that:

\displaystyle ff^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=e_{fg}e_{fg}=e_{fg}=1_{(A,e_{fg})}

\displaystyle f^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f=e_{gf}e_{gf}=e_{gf}=1_{(B,e_{gf})}

So we conclude that
ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f:(A,e_{fg})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e_{gf})
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
with inverse
f^{\frac{D}{g}}:(B,e_{gf})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{fg})
, as desired.

(iii)

This is shown by similar argument.

\Box

We now prove the analogue of Theorem
5.14
for Drazin inverses of opposing pairs:

Theorem 7.16

In a category
\mathbb{X}
,
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is Drazin if and only if there are idempotents
e_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
e_{B}:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, and a
k\in\mathbb{N}
, such that both of the maps
(fg)^{k}f:(A,e_{A})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e_{B})
and
(gf)^{k}g:(B,e_{B})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{A})
are isomorphisms
8
8

8

It is important to note that this statement does not say that
(fg)^{k}f
and
(gf)^{k}g
are inverses in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), suppose that
(f,g)
is Drazin. So
fg
and
gf
are Drazin. As our idempotents, we take the induced ones
e_{fg}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
e_{gf}:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
. Now we first note that by Lemma
2.2
.(
i
) and using the same arguments as in the first half of the proof of Theorem
5.14
, we have that for a
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
which is Drazin, for all
\mathsf{ind}(x)\leq j
that
x^{j+1}:(A,e_{x})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{x})
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. So let
k=\mathsf{max}\left(\mathsf{ind}(fg),\mathsf{ind}(gf)\right)
. Then we get that
(fg)^{k+1}:(A,e_{fg})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{fg})
and
(gf)^{k+1}:(A,e_{gf})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{gf})
are isomorphisms. Now by Lemma
7.15
, we also have that
f^{\frac{D}{g}}:(B,e_{gf})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{fg})
and
g^{\frac{D}{f}}:(A,e_{gf})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e_{fg})
are isomorphisms. So the composites,
(fg)^{k+1}g^{\frac{D}{f}}:(A,e_{gf})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e_{fg})
and
(gf)^{k+1}f^{\frac{D}{g}}:(A,e_{gf})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{gf})
are also isomorphisms. However, applying Corollary
7.8
and Lemma
2.2
.(
i
), we get that:

(fg)^{k+1}g^{\frac{D}{f}}=(fg)^{k+1}(fg)^{D}f=(fg)^{k}f

So
(fg)^{k+1}g^{\frac{D}{f}}=(fg)^{k}f
, and similarly we can also show that
(gf)^{k+1}f^{\frac{D}{g}}=(gf)^{k}g
. So we conclude that
(fg)^{k}f:(A,e_{A})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e_{B})
and
(gf)^{k}g:(B,e_{B})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{A})
are isomorphisms in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
as desired.

For (
\Leftarrow
), suppose for some
e_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
and
e_{B}:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, and a
k\in\mathbb{N}
, that both
(fg)^{k}f:(A,e_{A})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e_{B})
and
(gf)^{k}g:(B,e_{B})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{A})
are isomorphisms in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
, with respective inverses
v:(B,e_{B})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{A})
and
u:(A,e_{A})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e_{B})
. Explicitly this means we have the following identities:

\displaystyle(fg)^{k}fe_{B}=(fg)^{k}f=e_{A}(fg)^{k}f

\displaystyle e_{B}(gf)^{k}g=(gf)^{k}g=(gf)^{k}ge_{A}

\displaystyle ve_{A}=v=e_{B}v

\displaystyle e_{A}u=u=ue_{B}

\displaystyle(fg)^{k}fv=e_{A}=u(gf)^{k}g

\displaystyle v(fg)^{k}f=e_{B}=(gf)^{k}gu

We will now prove that
fg
is Drazin. To do so, we will first show that
(fg)^{k+1}
is an isomorphism in the idempotent splitting. Using the above identity, we first compute:

e_{A}(fg)^{k+1}=e_{A}(fg)^{k}fg=(fg)^{k}fg=(fg)^{k+1}

(fg)^{k+1}e_{A}=f(gf)^{k}ge_{A}=f(gf)^{k}g=(fg)^{k+1}

So we get that
(fg)^{k+1}:(A,e_{A})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{A})
is indeed a map in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. On the other hand, since
u:(A,e_{A})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(B,e_{B})
and
v:(B,e_{B})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{A})
are maps in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
, it follows that
u(gf)^{k}v:(A,e_{A})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{A})
is also a map
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. To show that this map is the inverse of
(fg)^{k+1}
, we first need to do some calculations:

\displaystyle(gf)^{k}v=(gf)^{k}e_{B}v=(gf)^{k}(gf)^{k}guv=(gf)^{k}g(fg)^{k}uv=e_{B}(gf)^{k}g(fg)^{k}uv

\displaystyle=e_{B}(gf)^{k}(gf)^{k}guv=e_{B}(gf)^{k}e_{B}v=e_{B}(gf)^{k}v

So
(gf)^{k}v=e_{B}(gf)^{k}v
, and similarly we can also show that
u(gf)^{k}=u(gf)^{k}e_{A}
. With these identities in hand, we can observe that:

\displaystyle(fg)^{k+1}u(gf)^{k}v=f(gf)^{k}gu(gf)^{k}v=fe_{B}(gf)^{k}v=f(gf)^{k}v=(fg)^{k}fv=e_{A}=1_{(A,e_{A})}

\displaystyle u(gf)^{k}v(fg)^{k+1}=u(gf)^{k}v(fg)^{k}fg=u(gf)^{k}e_{A}g=u(gf)^{k}g=e_{A}=1_{(A,e_{A})}

So we get that
(fg)^{k+1}:(A,e_{A})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(A,e_{A})
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{Split}(\mathbb{X})
. Then by Theorem
5.14
,
fg
is Drazin. Therefore we conclude that
(f,g)
is Drazin.

\Box

7.6
Drazin Opposing Pairs and Binary Idempotents

Recall from Section
5.2
that a binary idempotent is, using the terminology from this section, an opposing pair
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
such that
fgf=f
and
gfg=g
, and this implies that
fg
and
gf
are idempotents. Here we explain how binary idempotents are linked to Drazin opposing pairs. Moreover, binary idempotents will play a key role in the rest of the sections.

We first observe that similar to an idempotent being its own Drazin inverse, a binary idempotent regarded as an opposing pair is its own Drazin inverse.

Lemma 7.17

\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is a binary idempotent if and only if
(g,f)
is its Drazin inverse, so
(f,g)^{D}=(g,f)
(so that
f^{\frac{D}{g}}=g
and
g^{\frac{D}{f}}=f
).

Proof:
For
(\Rightarrow)
, Since
(f,g)
is a binary idempotent,
fg
and
gf
are idempotents, and so by Lemma
5.2
, are Drazin and their own Drazin inverses. Therefore
(f,g)
is Drazin. Applying Corollary
7.8
, we compute:

f^{\frac{D}{g}}=g(fg)^{D}=gfg=g

So
f^{\frac{D}{g}}=g
, and similarly, we can also show that
g^{\frac{D}{f}}=f
.

For
(\Leftarrow)
, if
(f,g)
is Drazin with itself as a Drazin inverse, then
[DV.2]
gives us that

gfg=g
and
fgf=f
. Thus,
(f,g)
is a binary idempotent.

\Box

On the other hand, from any Drazin opposing pair we get
two
binary idempotents.

Proposition 7.18

If
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is Drazin then:

(i)

(f,g)^{D}\colon=(f^{\frac{D}{g}},g^{\frac{D}{f}})
is Drazin with the Drazin inverse
(f,g)^{DD}\colon=(ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f,gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g)
and
\mathsf{ind}((f,g)^{D})\leq 1
;

(ii)

(f,g)^{DD}\colon=(ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f,gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g)
is Drazin with the Drazin inverse
(f,g)^{D}
;

(iii)

(ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f,f^{\frac{D}{g}})
and

(gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g,g^{\frac{D}{f}})
are binary idempotents.

Proof:
(i) Recall from Corollary
7.8
we have that
g^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\frac{D}{g}}=(gf)^{D}
and
f^{\frac{D}{g}}g^{\frac{D}{f}}=(fg)^{D}
. However by Lemma
3.6
.(
i
), a Drazin inverse is always Drazin, so
g^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\frac{D}{g}}
and
f^{\frac{D}{g}}g^{\frac{D}{f}}
are Drazin. As such,
(f^{\frac{D}{g}},g^{\frac{D}{f}})
is Drazin. Now applying the formulas from Lemma
3.6
.(
i
) and Corollary
7.8
, as well as using
[DV.2]
and
[DV.3]
, we compute:

\displaystyle g^{\frac{D}{f}}(f^{\frac{D}{g}}g^{\frac{D}{f}})^{D}=g^{\frac{D}{f}}((gf)^{D})^{D}=g^{\frac{D}{f}}gf(gf)^{D}gf=g^{\frac{D}{f}}gff^{\frac{D}{g}}g^{\frac{D}{f}}gf

\displaystyle=ff^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f=ff^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f=ff^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f=ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f

So the Drazin inverse of
f^{\frac{D}{g}}
over
g^{\frac{D}{f}}
is
ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f
. Similarly, we can also compute that the Drazin inverse of
g^{\frac{D}{f}}
over
f^{\frac{D}{g}}
is
gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g
.

(ii) By (
i
), we know that
(ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f,gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g)
is a Drazin inverse. So by (
i
) again, we get that
(ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f,gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g)
has a Drazin inverse, which by
[DV.2]
is precisely
(f^{\frac{D}{g}},g^{\frac{D}{f}})
.

(iii) Using
[DV.2]
, we compute that:

\displaystyle(ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f)f^{\frac{D}{g}}(ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f)=f(f^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}})f(f^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}})f=f(f^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}})f=ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f

\displaystyle f^{\frac{D}{g}}(ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f)f^{\frac{D}{g}}=(f^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}})ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=f^{\frac{D}{g}}ff^{\frac{D}{g}}=f^{\frac{D}{g}}

So
(ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f,f^{\frac{D}{g}})
is a binary idempotent. Similarly we can also show that
(gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g,g^{\frac{D}{f}})
is a binary idempotent.

\Box

Using binary idempotents, we can also characterize when an opposing pair is the Drazin inverse of another opposing pair. To do so, it will be first useful to observe the following:

Lemma 7.19

\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is a Drazin inverse if and only if there is an opposing pair of dual type
\textstyle{(f^{\frac{D}{g}},g^{\frac{D}{f}}):B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
which satisfy
[DV.1
′
]

ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f=f
and
gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g=g
,
[DV.2]
and
[DV.3]
.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), suppose that
(f,g)
is the Drazin inverse of
(h,k)
, so
(f,g)=(h^{\frac{D}{k}},k^{\frac{D}{h}})
. Then by Proposition
7.18
.(
i
), we get that
(f,g)
is Drazin with Drazin inverse
(f^{\frac{D}{g}},g^{\frac{D}{f}})=(hh^{\frac{D}{k}}h,kk^{\frac{D}{h}}k)=(hfh,kgk)
. This means that
[DV.2]
and
[DV.3]
hold. For
[DV.1
′
]
, since
f=h^{\frac{D}{k}}
and
f^{\frac{D}{g}}=hh^{\frac{D}{k}}h
, using
[DV.2]
twice we compute that:

ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f=h^{\frac{D}{k}}hh^{\frac{D}{k}}hh^{\frac{D}{k}}=h^{\frac{D}{k}}hh^{\frac{D}{k}}=h^{\frac{D}{k}}=f

So
ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f=f
, and similarly we can also show that
gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g=g
.

For (
\Leftarrow
), we show that
(f^{\frac{D}{g}},g^{\frac{D}{f}})
is the Drazin inverse of
(f,g)
. By assumption,
[DV.2]
and
[DV.3]
hold, so it remains to show
[DV.1]
. Using
[DV.1
′
]
and
[DV.3]
, we show that
[DV.1]
holds for
k=1
:

\displaystyle fgff^{\frac{D}{g}}=fgg^{\frac{D}{f}}g=fg

\displaystyle gfgg^{\frac{D}{f}}=gff^{\frac{D}{g}}f=gf

So
(f,g)
is Drazin. Now by
[DV.1
′
]
we have that
(f,g)=(ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f,gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g)
. Then Proposition
7.18
.(
i
) tells us that
(f,g)=(ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f,gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g)
is the Drazin inverse of
(f^{\frac{D}{g}},g^{\frac{D}{f}})
.

\Box

Proposition 7.20

\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is a Drazin inverse if and only if
(f,g)
is Drazin and both

(f,f^{\frac{D}{g}})
and
(g^{\frac{D}{f}},g)
are binary idempotents.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), this is simply Lemma
7.18
. For (
\Leftarrow
), since by assumption
(f,g)
is Drazin (so
[DV.2]
and
[DV.3]
hold), by Lemma
7.19
we need only show that
[DV.1
′
]
holds. However this is immediate since
(f,f^{\frac{D}{g}})
and
(g^{\frac{D}{f}},g)
are binary idempotents. So by Lemma
7.19
we get that
(f,g)
is a Drazin inverse.

\Box

Corollary 7.21

If
\textstyle{(f,g):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is a Drazin inverse if and only if
(f,g)
is Drazin,
{\sf ind}((f,g))\leq 1
,and satisfies
[DV.1
′
]

ff^{\frac{D}{g}}f=f
and
gg^{\frac{D}{f}}g=g
.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), by Proposition
7.18
.(
i
), we get that
(f,g)
is Drazin with
{\sf ind}((f,g))\leq 1
. While that
(f,g)
and its Drazin inverse satisfy
[DV.1
′
]
follows Proposition
7.20
that
(f,f^{\frac{D}{g}})
and
(g^{\frac{D}{f}},g)
are binary idempotents. For (
\Leftarrow
), since by assumption
(f,g)
is Drazin (so
[DV.2]
and
[DV.3]
hold), and also that
[DV.1
′
]
holds, by Lemma
7.19
we get that
(f,g)
is a Drazin inverse.

\Box

7.7
Being Drazin in a Dagger Category

In this section, we consider Drazin inverses in
dagger
categories. In particular, in the next section we will relate Drazin inverses to another sort of generalized inverse that arises in dagger categories, called the
Moore-Penrose inverse
.

Recall that a
dagger category
is a category
\mathbb{X}
equipped with a contravariant functor
\dagger:\mathbb{X}^{op}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
which is the identity on objects and involutive. Explicitly, this means that for every map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, there is a map of dual type
f^{\dagger}:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
called the
adjoint
of
f
such that
(fg)^{\dagger}=g^{\dagger}f^{\dagger}
,
1_{A}^{\dagger}=1_{A}
, and
f^{\dagger\dagger}=f
.

For a more in-depth introduction to dagger categories, we refer the reader to
[
17
]
. There are plenty of examples of dagger categories: in particular, the category of opposing maps itself is the cofree dagger category of a category
[
16
, Definition 3.1.16]
. Thus we have already secretly been working with dagger categories!

Since the adjoint of an endomorphism is an endomorphism, it makes sense to ask whether the adjoint of a Drazin endomorphism is Drazin. It turns out that the Drazin inverse of the adjoint is the adjoint of the Drazin inverse:

Lemma 7.22

In a dagger category
\mathbb{X}
, if
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is Drazin then
x^{\dagger}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is also Drazin where
(x^{\dagger})^{D}=(x^{D})^{\dagger}
.

Proof:
Clearly, if
x
is Drazin in
\mathbb{X}
then
x
is Drazin in
\mathbb{X}^{op}
with
x^{D}
as its Drazin inverse. Since Drazin inverses are absolute by Proposition
3.7
, it follows that
x^{\dagger}
is Drazin and
(x^{\dagger})^{D}=(x^{D})^{\dagger}
as desired.

\Box

In a dagger category, for any arbitrary map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, we get the opposing pair
\textstyle{(f,f^{\dagger}):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
, which we call an
adjoint opposing pair
. When an adjoint opposing pair
(f,f^{\dagger})
is Drazin, it turns out that the Drazin inverse of the map over its adjoint, that is
f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}
, is the adjoint of the Drazin inverse of the adjoint over the map:

Lemma 7.23

In a dagger category
\mathbb{X}
, if
\textstyle{(f,f^{\dagger}):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
has a Drazin inverse then:

(i)

f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}=((f^{\dagger})^{\frac{D}{f}})^{\dagger}
and so
(f,f^{\dagger})^{D}
is an adjoint pair;

(ii)

The induced idempotents are
\dagger
-idempotents
, that is,
e_{ff^{\dagger}}^{\dagger}=e_{ff^{\dagger}}
and
e_{f^{\dagger}f}^{\dagger}=e_{f^{\dagger}f}
.

Proof:
(i) If
(f,f^{\dagger})
has a Drazin inverse, by Theorem
7.6

(f,f^{\dagger})
is Drazin, so
ff^{\dagger}
and
f^{\dagger}f
are Drazin. Then using Corollary
7.8
and Lemma
7.22
, we compute:

((f^{\dagger})^{\frac{D}{f}})^{\dagger}=(f(f^{\dagger}f)^{D})^{\dagger}=((f^{\dagger}f)^{D})^{\dagger}f^{\dagger}=((f^{\dagger}f)^{\dagger})^{D}f^{\dagger}=(f^{\dagger}f)^{D}f^{\dagger}=f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}

So
f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}=((f^{\dagger})^{\frac{D}{f}})^{\dagger}
as desired.

(ii) By Lemma
7.15
.(
i
), we know that
e_{ff^{\dagger}}=(f^{\dagger})^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}
and
e_{f^{\dagger}f}=f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}(f^{\dagger})^{\frac{D}{f}}
. However by (
i
), we then get that
e_{ff^{\dagger}}=\left(f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}\right)^{\dagger}f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}
and
e_{f^{\dagger}f}=f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}\left(f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}\right)^{\dagger}
– which implies that
e_{ff^{\dagger}}
and
e_{f^{\dagger}f}
are
\dagger
-idempotents.

\Box

We can also precisely say when an adjoint opposing pair is the Drazin inverse of another adjoint opposing pair.

Lemma 7.24

In a dagger category
\mathbb{X}
,
\textstyle{(f,f^{\dagger}):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is a Drazin inverse if and only if
(f,f^{\dagger})
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(f,f^{\dagger})\leq 1
and
ff^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}f=f
.

Proof:
For (
\Rightarrow
), this is immediate from Lemma
7.19
. For (
\Leftarrow
), if
ff^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}f=f
, then Lemma
7.23
.(
i
) tell us that the dagger of this identity is precisely
f^{\dagger}(f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}})^{\dagger}f^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger}
. Then by applying Lemma
7.19
, we get that
(f,f^{\dagger})
is a Drazin inverse.
\Box

7.8
The Moore-Penrose Inverse

The remainder of this section is dedicated to discussing the relationship between Drazin opposing pairs and Moore-Penrose inverses. For a more in-depth introduction to Moore-Penrose inverses and examples, we invite the reader to see
[
6
,
10
]
.

In a dagger category
\mathbb{X}
, a
Moore-Penrose inverse

[
10
, Def 2.3]
of a map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is a map of dual type
f^{\circ}:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
such that:

\textbf{[MP.1]}~ff^{\circ}f=f;~~~~~\textbf{[MP.2]}~f^{\circ}ff^{\circ}=f^{\circ};~~~~~\textbf{[MP.3]}~(ff^{\circ})^{\dagger}=ff^{\circ};~~~~~\textbf{[MP.4]}~(f^{\circ}f)^{\dagger}=f^{\circ}f.

Like Drazin inverses, Moore-Penrose inverses are unique
[
10
, Lemma 2.4]
. Moreover, if
f
has a Moore-Penrose inverse, then so does
f^{\dagger}
, where
f^{\dagger\circ}=f^{\circ\dagger}

[
10
, Lemma 2.5.(ii)]
. We also observe that having a Moore-Penrose inverse gives a binary idempotent:

Lemma 7.25

In a dagger category
\mathbb{X}
, if
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
has a Moore-Penrose inverse if and only if
(f,f^{\circ})
is a binary idempotent
whose induced idempotents are
\dagger
-idempotents.

Proof:

[MP.1]
and
[MP.2]
ensure that it is a binary idempotent while
[MP.3]
and
[MP.4]
ensure that the induced idempotents are
\dagger
-idempotents.

\Box

Having a Moore-Penrose inverse can also be characterized in terms of Drazin inverses. Specifically a map has a Moore-Penrose inverse if and only if its induced adjoint opposing pair
is
a Drazin inverse.

Theorem 7.26

In a dagger category
\mathbb{X}
,
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
has a Moore-Penrose inverse if and only if
\textstyle{(f,f^{\dagger}):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is a Drazin inverse.

Note that
being
a Drazin inverse is a considerably stronger property than
having
a Drazin inverse.

Proof:
For
(\Rightarrow)
, we show that
(f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}},(f^{\dagger})^{\frac{D}{f}})\colon=(f^{\circ},f^{\circ\dagger})
satisfies the requirements from Lemma
7.19
.

[DV.1
′
]

Using
[MP.1]
, we have:

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}(f^{\dagger})^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger}f^{\circ\dagger}f^{\dagger}=(ff^{\circ}f)^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger}

\displaystyle ff^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}f=ff^{\circ}f=f

[DV.2]

Using
[MP.2]
, we have:

\displaystyle f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}ff^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}=f^{\circ}ff^{\circ}=f^{\circ}=f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}

\displaystyle(f^{\dagger})^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\dagger}(f^{\dagger})^{\frac{D}{f}}=f^{\circ\dagger}f^{\dagger}f^{\circ\dagger}=(f^{\circ}ff^{\circ})^{\dagger}=f^{\circ\dagger}=(f^{\dagger})^{\frac{D}{f}}

[DV.3]

Using
[MP.3]
and
[MP.4]
, we have:

\displaystyle ff^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}=ff^{\circ}=(ff^{\circ})^{\dagger}=f^{\circ\dagger}f^{\dagger}=(f^{\dagger})^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\dagger}

\displaystyle f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}f=f^{\circ}f=(f^{\circ}f)^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger}f^{\circ\dagger}=f^{\dagger}(f^{\dagger})^{\frac{D}{f}}

So by Lemma
7.19
,
(f,f^{\dagger})
is a Drazin inverse. In fact,
(f,f^{\dagger})
is Drazin with Drazin inverse
(f^{\circ\dagger},f^{\circ})
and
\mathsf{ind}(f,g)\leq 1
.

For
(\Leftarrow)
, by Corollary
7.21
,
(f,f^{\dagger})
is Drazin. So we will show that
f^{\circ}=f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}
satisfies the four axioms of a Moore-Penrose inverse. However note that
[MP.1]
is precisely
[DV.1
′
]
, while
[MP.2]
is precisely
[DV.3]
. Now for
[MP.3]
, using Lemma
7.23
and
[DV.3]
, we compute:

\displaystyle(ff^{\circ})^{\dagger}=(f^{\circ})^{\dagger}f^{\dagger}=(f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}})^{\dagger}f^{\dagger}=(f^{\dagger})^{\frac{D}{f}}f^{\dagger}=ff^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}=ff^{\circ}

While
[MP.4]
is shown using similar calculations. So we conclude that
f
has a Moore-Penrose inverse.

\Box

In general, while not every map in a dagger category has a Moore-Penrose inverse, for an adjoint opposing pair which is Drazin, the Drazin inverse of the map over its adjoint always has a Moore-Penrose inverse.

Lemma 7.27

In a dagger category
\mathbb{X}
, if
\textstyle{(f,f^{\dagger}):A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
is Drazin then
f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}
has a Moore-Penrose inverse, where
\left(f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}\right)^{\circ}=ff^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}f
.

Proof:
If
(f,f^{\dagger})
is Drazin, by Lemma
7.23
.(
i
), we have that
(f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}},(f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}})^{\dagger})
is its Drazin inverse. In other words, using Lemma
7.9
, we get that
((f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}})^{\dagger},f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}})
is the Drazin inverse of the adjoint opposing pair
(f^{\dagger},f)
. Then by Theorem
7.26
,
f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}
has a Moore-Penrose inverse given by
\left(f^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}\right)^{\circ}=ff^{\frac{D}{f^{\dagger}}}f
.

\Box

Now a
Moore-Penrose dagger category

[
10
, Def 2.3]
is a dagger category such that every map has a Moore-Penrose inverse. Then by Theorem
7.26
, we obtain a novel equivalent description of when a dagger category is Moore-Penrose.

Corollary 7.28

A dagger category
\mathbb{X}
is Moore-Penrose if and only if every adjoint opposing pair is a Drazin inverse.

7.9
When Moore-Penrose is Drazin

We conclude with the natural question of when the Drazin inverse and Moore-Penrose inverse coincide. For complex square matrices, this happens precisely when the matrix is an EP matrix
[
6
, Thm 7.3.4]
, which means that it commutes with its Moore-Penrose inverse
[
6
, Def 4.3.1]
. Here we show that the same holds true in an arbitrary dagger category.

Definition 7.29

In a dagger category
\mathbb{X}
, a map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is said to be
EP
if it has a Moore-Penrose inverse and it commutes with it, that is,
ff^{\circ}=f^{\circ}f
.

Definition 7.30

In a dagger category
\mathbb{X}
, an endomorphism
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
is said to be
Moore-Penrose-Drazin
if
x
is Drazin such that its Drazin inverse is also its Moore-Penrose inverse,
x^{D}=x^{\circ}
.

Theorem 7.31

In a dagger category
\mathbb{X}
, for an endomorphism
x:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

x
is Moore-Penrose-Drazin;

(ii)

x
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(x)\leq 1
and the induced idempotent
e_{x}
is a
\dagger
-idempotent;

(iii)

x
is EP.

Proof:
For
(i)\Rightarrow(ii)
, by definition
x
is Drazin. Using
[DV.3]
and
[MP.1]
, we compute:

x^{2}x^{D}=xxx^{D}=xx^{D}x=xx^{\circ}x=x

So
\mathsf{ind}(x)\leq 1
. Now using
[MP.3]
, we compute that:

e_{x}^{\dagger}=(xx^{D})^{\dagger}=(xx^{\circ})^{\dagger}=xx^{\circ}=e_{x}

So
e_{x}^{\dagger}
is a
\dagger
-idempotent.

For
(ii)\Rightarrow(iii)
, we show that
x^{D}
satisfies the four Moore-Penrose inverse axioms. However, note that since
\mathsf{ind}(x)\leq 1
,
x^{D}
is a group inverse of
x
. Then
[MP.1]
and
[MP.2]
are precisely the same as
[G.1]
and
[G.2]
respectively. Now since
e_{x}^{\dagger}
is a
\dagger
-idempotent, meaning
e_{x}^{\dagger}=e_{x}
, which gives us precisely that
(xx^{D})^{\dagger}=xx^{D}
and
(x^{D}x)^{\dagger}=x^{D}x
, which are
[MP.3]
and
[MP.4]
. So
x^{\circ}=x^{D}
is a Moore-Penrose inverse of
x
, and by
[D.3]
, we also have that
xx^{\circ}=x^{\circ}x
.

For
(iii)\Rightarrow(i)
, we show that setting
x^{D}=x^{\circ}
satisfies the three group inverse axioms. However, note that
[G.1]
and
[G.2]
are the same as
[MP.1]
and
[MP.2]
. While
[G.3]
is precisely the extra assumption
xx^{\circ}=x^{\circ}x
. So
x^{\circ}
is a group inverse of
x
, which implies that
x
is Drazin, whose Drazin inverse is its Moore-Penrose inverse. Thus,
x
is Moore-Penrose-Drazin as desired.

\Box

In particular, for any map with a Moore-Penrose inverse, we always get two Moore-Penrose-Drazin endomorphisms.

Lemma 7.32

In a dagger category
\mathbb{X}
, if
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
has a Moore-Penrose inverse, then
ff^{\dagger}
and
f^{\dagger}f
are Moore-Penrose-Drazin.

Proof:
By Theorem
7.26
and Corollary
7.21
,
(f,f^{\dagger})
is Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(f,f^{\dagger})\leq 1
, which tells us that
ff^{\dagger}
and
f^{\dagger}f
are Drazin with
\mathsf{ind}(ff^{\dagger})\leq 1
and
\mathsf{ind}(f^{\dagger}f)\leq 1
(where the index inequalities comes from the proof of Theorem
7.6
). While Lemma
7.23
.(
ii
) tells us that
e_{ff^{\dagger}}
and
e_{ff^{\dagger}}
are
\dagger
-idempotents. So by Lemma
7.31
, we conclude that
ff^{\dagger}
and
f^{\dagger}f
are Moore-Penrose-Drazin.

\Box
